Chapter 9

Process Address Space

How Linux 7.0.12 represents a process's virtual memory with mm_struct, vm_area_struct, the maple tree, mmap(), ASLR, and munmap() on x86_64 and ARM64.

Chapter 8 explained how Linux obtains physical pages and allocator metadata. This chapter shifts perspective and asks a different question: what does a process think memory looks like? The answer is not a flat array of RAM. It is a sparse, policy-rich, demand-paged virtual address space, assembled from executable images, anonymous heap growth, shared libraries, stacks, file mappings, guard gaps, and architecture-specific landing areas such as the VDSO. The kernel records that view in a small number of central structures: the memory descriptor (mm_struct) and a set of virtual memory areas (vm_area_struct objects, or VMAs).

The conceptual contrast with Chapter 8 is useful. Kernel allocators begin with physical pages, zones, folios, and object caches because the kernel must manage the machine's real memory supply. User-space allocation begins with an abstraction boundary: a process requests address ranges, protections, and sharing semantics, while the kernel delays physical allocation as long as possible. That delay is not laziness; it is policy. It allows demand paging, copy-on-write, lazy stack growth, sparse address spaces, NUMA placement, huge-page promotion, and file-backed sharing to coexist without forcing every request to consume RAM immediately.

The Linux 7.0.12 code in this chapter differs markedly from Linux 2.6. The old per-process VMA topology built from an rb-tree plus a linked list has been replaced by the maple tree. The old mmap_sem has long since been renamed mmap_lock, and it is no longer the only lock that matters: per-VMA locks let most page faults run without touching it at all. That is not an optional extra on the machines this book follows — CONFIG_PER_VMA_LOCK is def_bool y, and both x86_64 and ARM64 select the architecture support it depends on, so it is always compiled in. Whole interfaces that did not exist in Linux 2.6 now shape the address space: userfaultfd(), process_madvise(), MAP_FIXED_NOREPLACE, guard regions installed through madvise(), and mseal(), which makes a mapping’s layout and protections immutable for the remaining life of the process. And the mainstream architectural story is now x86_64 and ARM64, not 32-bit i386.

The code has moved as well as changed. In Linux 2.6 essentially all address-space manipulation lived in mm/mmap.c. In 7.0.12 the VMA machinery — merging, splitting, insertion, removal, and the iterators over the maple tree — has been split into mm/vma.c, which is now the larger of the two files. A reader who knows where things used to be will find much of it moved rather than rewritten.

The chapter is in eleven parts, followed by a summary, and the table of contents above lists what is in each. What that list does not show is that the parts answer five different questions. Sections 2 and 3 ask what the kernel records: the memory descriptor and the regions hanging off it. Sections 4, 5, 9 and 10 ask how a process changes that record — mmap(), munmap(), brk() and the operations that resize, reprotect and advise. Sections 6 and 7 ask when the promise becomes real memory, which is the page fault handler and the interface that lets user space resolve faults itself. Section 8 asks how an address space begins and ends, across fork, exec and exit. Section 11 asks how the rest of the kernel reaches into it: how a driver, a DMA engine or a debugger obtains the physical pages behind a user address and keeps them in place. The recurring theme is that the first group is cheap bookkeeping and the third group is where the cost finally lands.

All source-code references in this chapter refer to the Linux 7.0.12 tree. The files the chapter works from are listed by topic in Source Trails.

Process Address Space Overview

Every normal user process in Linux owns, or shares with its threads, a private virtual address space. The CPU sees virtual addresses; the MMU turns them into physical accesses using page tables whose top-level pointer is stored in the process's mm_struct. But the kernel cannot reason about an address space one page-table entry at a time. It needs larger semantic units that describe whole regions and their purpose: one interval is a readable file-backed code segment, another is a private writable heap, a third is a downward-growing stack, yet another is a huge-page mapping, and some carry userfaultfd metadata for user-space fault handling. Those semantic units are the virtual memory areas (VMAs).

A useful mental model is this:

  • mm_struct is the whole address-space object.
  • vm_area_struct is one contiguous region with uniform policy.
  • page tables provide the fine-grained translation actually consumed by the hardware.
  • fault handlers populate or modify page tables when execution touches an unmapped or write-protected page.

That hierarchy is why Linux can keep the logical shape of an address space independent from the immediate presence of physical pages. A VMA may cover hundreds of megabytes while only a handful of its pages have ever been faulted in. Conversely, a single file mapping may span multiple VMAs if protections, NUMA policy, anonymous naming, or userfaultfd registration change within the range. The VMA layer therefore sits exactly at the boundary between user-visible memory operations and low-level page-table mechanics.

Table 9-1. Modern System Calls That Shape a Process Address Space
System call Primary role Typical effect on the VMA topology
brk() Grow or shrink the traditional heap. Extends or trims the anonymous data-segment VMA near mm->brk.
mmap() Create an anonymous or file-backed mapping. Allocates a new VMA or merges with adjacent compatible VMAs.
munmap() Remove a mapping interval. Deletes VMAs or splits boundary VMAs and removes the middle range.
mremap() Resize or move an existing mapping. May split, merge, move, or duplicate VMA metadata.
mprotect() Change protections on an interval. Often splits a VMA, modifies flags, then attempts post-change merges.
madvise() Apply access-pattern or reclamation advice. Usually changes policy without changing topology, but some advice requires splits.
mlock()/mlock2()/munlock() Pin or unpin pages in memory. Sets or clears locking-related VMA flags and updates locked_vm; mlock2() adds MLOCK_ONFAULT, which pins on first touch instead of immediately.
userfaultfd() Register user-space fault handling. Associates missing, write-protect, or minor-fault handling state with VMAs.
process_madvise() Apply selected advice to another process. Traverses a remote mm_struct under its own locking rules.
process_vm_readv()/process_vm_writev() Read or write another process's address space. Uses VMA lookup and GUP-style checks without changing layout.
clone()/fork() Create a task sharing or copying the address space. Either increments mm_users or duplicates the maple tree and VMAs.
execve() Replace the current process image. Destroys the old VMA set and builds a new one from the executable image.
_exit() Release the exiting task's memory context. Drops mm_users; the last drop triggers exit_mmap() and final teardown.
mseal() Make an interval's layout and protections immutable. Sets VM_SEALED, splitting or merging as any flag change would; afterwards mprotect(), mremap(), munmap() and destructive madvise() all fail on the range.
map_shadow_stack() Allocate a hardware-managed return-address stack. Creates one anonymous VM_SHADOW_STACK VMA that ordinary stores cannot write.
pkey_mprotect() Change protections and assign a protection key. Same splits and merges as mprotect(), plus the key encoded in the VMA's VM_PKEY_BITn flags.
memfd_secret() Create a file whose pages are hidden from the kernel. None directly; mapping the descriptor yields a locked, undumpable VMA whose pages leave the kernel direct map.
process_mrelease() Reclaim a dying process's memory promptly. None — it frees pages behind the VMAs and leaves the topology untouched.

Many of these operations do not allocate or free physical memory directly. mmap() typically creates metadata first and lets the actual data pages arrive through faults. mprotect() often leaves the VMA count unchanged and merely changes flags. fork() duplicates address-space metadata but marks writable anonymous pages for later copy-on-write rather than copying them eagerly. The virtual-memory subsystem is therefore more about policy transitions than bulk memory copying.

The last row of the table makes the same point from the opposite side. process_mrelease() walks a dying process's maple tree and unmaps its anonymous pages, returning potentially gigabytes to the page allocator, yet it does not delete a single VMA: the topology it walks is exactly the topology it leaves behind. Between the two extremes — mmap(), which changes the topology without allocating memory, and process_mrelease(), which frees memory without changing the topology — sits the whole design. Address-space structure and physical residency are separate concerns joined only at fault time, and nearly every difficulty in this chapter comes from keeping the two consistent while they change independently.

The kernel's representation of a process's view of memory is explicitly interval-based. A VMA records the half-open range [vm_start, vm_end), its permissions, backing file and page offset if any, anonymous reverse-mapping metadata, operations callbacks, policy, and several newer fields for userfaultfd, anonymous naming, and per-VMA locking. The whole set of VMAs is stored in mm->mm_mt, the maple tree rooted in the memory descriptor. That storage decision affects almost every operation in this chapter because all mapping changes begin with interval lookup.

Two Architectures, One Vocabulary

This book follows x86_64 and ARM64, and the address-space layer is a good place to see how little of the difference between them reaches generic code. The two newest hardware memory features on each side are a case in point, because in both cases the architectures arrived at the same kernel abstraction from different silicon.

Shadow stacks are the clearest example. Intel implements them with CET (CONFIG_X86_USER_SHADOW_STACK), ARM with the Guarded Control Stack (CONFIG_ARM64_GCS); the register interfaces, the enabling bits, and the fault behavior are entirely different. Yet both architectures export the same map_shadow_stack() system call, and inside it both reach the same three lines:

/* arch/x86/kernel/shstk.c and arch/arm64/mm/gcs.c — separate files, same request */
mmap_write_lock(mm);
mapped_addr = do_mmap(NULL, addr, size, PROT_READ, flags,
                      VM_SHADOW_STACK | VM_WRITE, 0, &unused, NULL);
mmap_write_unlock(mm);

Only the flags computation differs, and barely: both start from MAP_ANONYMOUS | MAP_PRIVATE and add MAP_FIXED_NOREPLACE when the caller names an address, with x86 additionally forcing the mapping above 4 GB. Everything that matters to the memory-management code is in the arguments shown.

The combination is deliberately contradictory: user space asked for PROT_READ, but the VMA carries VM_WRITE. That is the whole trick. The region must be writable by the hardware's call and return instructions and by nothing else, so the write permission lives in the VMA — where the fault handler will consult it — while the protection bits the process can reach through mprotect() say read-only. A shadow stack is also given a guard gap below it, because VM_SHADOW_STACK is one of the two flags in VM_STARTGAP_FLAGS, the other being VM_GROWSDOWN. The generic mmap code needs to know nothing about CET or GCS to get all of this right.

Protection keys tell the same story with a small twist. Intel calls the feature PKU and ARM calls it the Permission Overlay Extension, but both select ARCH_HAS_PKEYS, both are def_bool y, and both expose pkey_alloc() and pkey_mprotect(). The key is stored in spare high bits of the VMA's flags, so a protection-domain change costs no page-table edit at all. Here the architectures do not quite converge: CONFIG_ARCH_PKEY_BITS is 4 on x86_64 and 3 on ARM64, giving 16 keys against 8. That single integer is the entire visible difference in generic code — a fair measure of how much the VMA abstraction absorbs.

Observing an Address Space

Everything this chapter describes is visible from user space, which makes it worth knowing how to look before reading about the mechanism. /proc/<pid>/maps prints one line per VMA — range, permissions, file offset, device, inode, and either a path or a synthetic label such as [heap], [stack], or [vdso]. It is, almost literally, a formatted walk of the maple tree, so the VMA merging and splitting described later in this chapter is directly observable as lines appearing, disappearing, and dividing. /proc/<pid>/smaps adds per-VMA physical accounting — resident, shared, private, dirty, and huge-page counts — which is where the gap between mapped and resident becomes concrete, and smaps_rollup sums the same figures over the whole address space in one pass.

Text is a poor interface for a tool that wants one VMA rather than all of them, and profilers that symbolize addresses want exactly that. Linux 7.0.12 therefore offers PROCMAP_QUERY, an ioctl on the maps file that returns a single struct procmap_query for the VMA covering a given address, with no parsing. Flags refine the search: PROCMAP_QUERY_COVERING_OR_NEXT_VMA returns the next VMA when the address falls in a hole, which turns the interface into an iterator, and PROCMAP_QUERY_FILE_BACKED_VMA restricts it to file mappings. The data for one VMA is self-consistent, but the kernel promises nothing between successive calls — the address space may be reshaped in between, which is a property of the thing being observed rather than a defect of the interface.

Demand paging and copy-on-write are central consequences of this design, but they deserve their own treatment. The sections that follow this one focus on address-space topology: what regions exist, how the kernel describes them, how they are inserted and removed, and how layout policy such as ASLR influences their placement. Fault handling, CoW write-protection, and page-table population return in the sections on the page fault handler and userfaultfd.

One last overview point ties this chapter back to earlier ones. Chapter 2 introduced virtual addressing and multi-level page tables; Chapter 8 introduced the allocators that can eventually supply physical pages; Chapter 20 will revisit program execution and image construction during execve(). This chapter sits squarely between them. It is where the kernel decides which intervals will exist before it decides which pages will fill them.

The Memory Descriptor (mm_struct)

The process address space as a whole is described by struct mm_struct, defined in include/linux/mm_types.h. Every normal task points at an mm_struct through task_struct->mm; all threads in a thread group share the same one. The structure combines topology, accounting, page-table root, locking, architecture-specific context, and lifecycle state. If you want to know how many VMAs a process has, where its heap starts, whether the layout is top-down, or which page-table root must be loaded into the hardware context-switch path, you eventually land in mm_struct.

The modern layout emphasizes two things that were much less visible in old texts:

  • the maple tree root (mm_mt) as the authoritative VMA index;
  • the locking split between the coarse mmap_lock, the per-VMA locking state that most page faults now use instead, and specialized sequence counters such as write_protect_seq.
/* include/linux/mm_types.h — abbreviated struct mm_struct */
struct mm_struct {
	struct {
		struct {
			/* mm_users count as one reference here */
			atomic_t mm_count;
		} ____cacheline_aligned_in_smp;

		struct maple_tree mm_mt;

		unsigned long mmap_base;
		unsigned long mmap_legacy_base;
		unsigned long task_size;
		pgd_t *pgd;
#ifdef CONFIG_MEMBARRIER
		atomic_t membarrier_state;
#endif
		atomic_t mm_users;
		struct mm_mm_cid mm_cid;
		atomic_long_t pgtables_bytes;
		int map_count;
		spinlock_t page_table_lock;
		struct rw_semaphore mmap_lock;
		struct list_head mmlist;
#ifdef CONFIG_PER_VMA_LOCK
		struct rcuwait vma_writer_wait;
		seqcount_t mm_lock_seq;
#endif
		...
		unsigned long hiwater_rss;
		unsigned long hiwater_vm;
		unsigned long total_vm;
		unsigned long locked_vm;
		atomic64_t pinned_vm;
		unsigned long data_vm;
		unsigned long exec_vm;
		unsigned long stack_vm;
		vm_flags_t def_flags;
		seqcount_t write_protect_seq;
		spinlock_t arg_lock;
		unsigned long start_code, end_code, start_data, end_data;
		unsigned long start_brk, brk, start_stack;
		unsigned long arg_start, arg_end, env_start, env_end;
		unsigned long saved_auxv[AT_VECTOR_SIZE];
		struct percpu_counter rss_stat[NR_MM_COUNTERS];
		struct linux_binfmt *binfmt;
		mm_context_t context;
		mm_flags_t flags;      /* Must use mm_flags_* helpers to access */
#ifdef CONFIG_MEMCG
		struct task_struct __rcu *owner;
#endif
		struct user_namespace *user_ns;
		struct file __rcu *exe_file;
#ifdef CONFIG_MMU_NOTIFIER
		struct mmu_notifier_subscriptions *notifier_subscriptions;
#endif
		atomic_t tlb_flush_pending;
		...
	};
};
Table 9-2. Key Fields of struct mm_struct
Field Meaning Why it matters
mm_mt The maple tree containing all VMAs. Replaces the old rb-tree + linked-list organization and drives all interval lookup.
mmap_lock Process-wide rwsem protecting VMA topology. Most mapping changes take it for write; faults and readers often take it for read or use RCU/per-VMA helpers.
pgd Pointer to the top-level page directory. Hardware context switching and page-table walks start here.
mm_users / mm_count Use-count and reference-count pair. Separate “who is using the address space?” from “who still holds a structural reference?”
map_count Number of VMAs. Checked against sysctl_max_map_count and useful as a fragmentation signal.
start_code, end_code, start_data, end_data Executable-image boundaries. Used by procfs, loaders, accounting, and heap-growth rules.
start_brk, brk Heap start and current program break. Define the classic data-segment heap managed by brk().
mmap_base Base address for non-legacy mmap() placement. Core ASLR and layout-policy state.
start_stack Initial user stack pointer area. Visible in procfs and important to stack-growth policy.
arg_start / arg_end Argument-vector span. Used by /proc/<pid>/cmdline and exec-image bookkeeping.
env_start / env_end Environment-vector span. Used by /proc and core-dump helpers.
total_vm, locked_vm, data_vm, exec_vm, stack_vm Per-category page counts. Allow fast accounting and limit checks without walking the whole tree.
rss_stat[] Four per-CPU counters for resident pages. The physical half of the accounting: what is actually in memory, not merely mapped.
hiwater_rss, hiwater_vm High-water marks for resident and virtual size. Source of the peak figures in /proc/<pid>/status and getrusage().
pgtables_bytes Memory consumed by this mm's page tables. Page tables are themselves a memory cost; the OOM killer and /proc both read it.
tlb_flush_pending Count of in-flight batched TLB flushes. Lets a PTE reader tell that a stale TLB entry may still exist elsewhere.
context Architecture-private state (mm_context_t). Holds the ASID on ARM64 and the PCID and LDT on x86_64 — the only field whose type changes with the architecture.
mm_cid Per-mm concurrency-ID allocator state. Backs rseq's compact CPU numbering so user-space allocators can size per-CPU arrays by threads rather than by CPUs.
exe_file The executable this image was loaded from. The target of /proc/<pid>/exe, and what keeps the file referenced after every mapping of it is gone.
notifier_subscriptions Registered MMU notifiers. How KVM, IOMMU drivers and GPUs learn that a mapping changed so they can invalidate their own shadow translations.
task_size, mmap_legacy_base Address-space ceiling and bottom-up mmap base. Together with mmap_base they encode the layout the architecture chose for this process.
def_flags Default VMA flags inherited by new mappings. Examples include future-locking state that should apply to later mappings.
write_protect_seq Sequence counter for temporary mm-wide write-protection. Important during fork()-driven copy-on-write setup.
mm_lock_seq Per-VMA-lock sequencing state under CONFIG_PER_VMA_LOCK. Coordinates write-side topology changes with lockless or RCU-assisted VMA readers.
flags A 64-bit bitmap of address-space flags (mm_flags_t). Includes layout choices such as MMF_TOPDOWN; marked __private, so it must be reached through the mm_flags_*() helpers.
owner Canonical task associated with the mm. Important for memcg ownership and accounting heuristics.
membarrier_state Membarrier-related flags close to pgd. Placed for context-switch locality because switching mms already touches nearby state.

The fields separate naturally into four groups:

  1. Topology: mm_mt, map_count, layout bases, and start/end markers.
  2. Translation: pgd, page-table lock, architecture context, and page-table byte counters.
  3. Accounting: the virtual counts (total_vm, data_vm, exec_vm, stack_vm, locked_vm, pinned_vm), the resident counts in rss_stat[], the high-water marks, and pgtables_bytes for the page tables themselves.
  4. Synchronization and lifetime: mmap_lock, sequence counters, mm_users, mm_count, and ownership metadata.

This grouping also explains why mm_struct exists separately from task_struct. Threads share an address space but not all other task state; kernel threads usually do not own a user address space at all; and the lifetime of an mm_struct often extends briefly beyond the lifetime of the last task actively executing in it because other kernel subsystems may still hold structural references.

One field has changed shape in a way that is easy to trip over. In older kernels mm->flags was an unsigned long tested with plain bit operations. The MMF_ flags outgrew a word, so it is now mm_flags_t — a DECLARE_BITMAP(__mm_flags, 64) — and the field is tagged __private, which makes direct access fail to compile. All reads and writes go through helpers in include/linux/mm.h:

bool mm_flags_test(int flag, const struct mm_struct *mm);
bool mm_flags_test_and_set(int flag, struct mm_struct *mm);
bool mm_flags_test_and_clear(int flag, struct mm_struct *mm);
void mm_flags_set(int flag, struct mm_struct *mm);
void mm_flags_clear(int flag, struct mm_struct *mm);

Code from the 2.6 era that wrote mm->flags |= MMF_... therefore has no mechanical translation; it must be rewritten in terms of these helpers. A similar shift is under way for vm_area_struct.vm_flags: the next section’s excerpt shows it declared as a const vm_flags_t inside a union with a writable vma_flags_t bitmap, so that existing reads keep compiling while every write is forced through an accessor.

Virtual Accounting Versus Resident Accounting

The accounting group is worth a closer look, because it contains two sets of counters that are easy to confuse and that the kernel deliberately implements in completely different ways. The distinction is the same one the whole chapter turns on: a mapping that exists and a page that is present are not the same thing.

total_vm, data_vm, exec_vm, stack_vm and locked_vm measure address space. They count pages that some VMA covers, whether or not any physical memory backs them, and they change only when the VMA topology changes. That is why they are plain unsigned long fields with no atomics: every path that modifies them already holds mmap_lock for write. pinned_vm is the exception, an atomic64_t, because pages are pinned and unpinned by drivers and by get_user_pages() callers that are not holding mmap_lock at the time (Section 11.4).

rss_stat[] measures residency, and the contrast in implementation is instructive:

/* include/linux/mm_types_task.h — what is counted */
enum {
	MM_FILEPAGES,   /* Resident file mapping pages  */
	MM_ANONPAGES,   /* Resident anonymous pages     */
	MM_SWAPENTS,    /* Anonymous swap entries       */
	MM_SHMEMPAGES,  /* Resident shared memory pages */
	NR_MM_COUNTERS
};

/* include/linux/mm.h — how it is read */
static inline unsigned long get_mm_counter(struct mm_struct *mm, int member)
{
	return percpu_counter_read_positive(&mm->rss_stat[member]);
}

static inline unsigned long get_mm_counter_sum(struct mm_struct *mm, int member)
{
	return percpu_counter_sum_positive(&mm->rss_stat[member]);
}

static inline unsigned long get_mm_rss(struct mm_struct *mm)
{
	return get_mm_counter(mm, MM_FILEPAGES) +
		get_mm_counter(mm, MM_ANONPAGES) +
		get_mm_counter(mm, MM_SHMEMPAGES);
}

Three things in that excerpt repay attention. First, these are struct percpu_counter, not plain longs and not atomics. RSS changes on every page fault and on every page unmapped, which is the hottest accounting path in the kernel; a shared counter would put a single cache line under fire from every CPU running threads of a multithreaded process. A per-CPU counter absorbs the updates locally and folds them into the shared total only when a per-CPU batch overflows.

Second, that batching is why there are two read functions. get_mm_counter() reads only the shared total and can be short by up to one batch per CPU; get_mm_counter_sum() walks every CPU's residual to get an exact figure and costs proportionally more. The kernel picks between them by how much the answer matters. /proc/<pid>/status and the OOM killer's badness score take the exact form — an OOM decision is irreversible, so it is worth paying for. /proc/<pid>/stat, NUMA balancing's page-fault heuristics and taskstats accounting take the cheap form, because all three are sampling a number that is changing anyway. Both forms are the _positive variants, clamping to zero, because a partially folded per-CPU counter can legitimately read negative in the middle of an update.

Third, get_mm_rss() deliberately omits MM_SWAPENTS. Swap entries are anonymous pages the process still owns but that are no longer resident, so counting them would defeat the purpose of the number. They are tracked separately precisely so that a process's swapped-out footprint can be seen without inflating its resident size.

The high-water marks sit slightly apart from both groups. hiwater_rss and hiwater_vm record peaks rather than current values, and the kernel goes to some trouble not to touch them on the common path. They are updated only when total_vm or RSS is about to be lowered — the moment when the current value is about to stop being the peak — and are left alone while either figure is rising. That is why every reader must reconcile:

static inline unsigned long get_mm_hiwater_rss(struct mm_struct *mm)
{
	return max(mm->hiwater_rss, get_mm_rss(mm));
}

The stored value is stale by design whenever the process is at its peak, and taking the larger of the recorded mark and the present value costs nothing at read time. These fields supply VmHWM and VmPeak in /proc/<pid>/status, and ru_maxrss from getrusage() by way of setmax_mm_hiwater_rss(), which folds the mark into the signal struct as each thread exits.

One last counter belongs to neither group. pgtables_bytes tracks how much memory this address space's own page tables occupy, incremented and decremented as tables are allocated and torn down. It exists because page tables are not free: a process with a sparse multi-gigabyte mapping can spend a substantial fraction of its footprint on the tables describing it, and neither the virtual counts nor RSS would ever show that. The OOM killer counts it directly — its badness score is get_mm_rss_sum() plus swap entries plus mm_pgtables_bytes() / PAGE_SIZE — so a process whose cost is mostly page tables can still be chosen as the victim.

Allocation, storage, and release of an mm_struct

Every mm_struct is allocated from a dedicated slab cache called "mm_struct", created at boot by kernel/fork.c:

/* kernel/fork.c — boot-time cache creation */
static struct kmem_cache *mm_cachep;

mm_size = sizeof(struct mm_struct) + cpumask_size() + mm_cid_size();
mm_cachep = kmem_cache_create_usercopy("mm_struct",
        mm_size, ARCH_MIN_MMSTRUCT_ALIGN,
        SLAB_HWCACHE_ALIGN | SLAB_PANIC | SLAB_ACCOUNT,
        offsetof(struct mm_struct, saved_auxv),
        sizeof_field(struct mm_struct, saved_auxv),
        NULL);

The allocation includes extra space for a CPU mask and per-CPU concurrency-ID state appended after the fixed struct fields. The cache uses kmem_cache_create_usercopy rather than plain kmem_cache_create because the saved_auxv field inside mm_struct is copied to user space during ELF program loading — the usercopy variant explicitly marks that region as the only safe range for user-space copies, hardening against information leaks. The flags deserve attention too: SLAB_HWCACHE_ALIGN ensures each descriptor starts on a cache-line boundary (these fields are touched on every context switch, page fault, and TLB flush); SLAB_PANIC crashes the kernel if the cache cannot be created (the system cannot function without it); and SLAB_ACCOUNT charges allocations to memory cgroups so container-aware systems can track per-cgroup address-space overhead.

Each task_struct reaches its address space through two pointers (include/linux/sched.h):

struct task_struct {
    ...
    struct mm_struct  *mm;         /* user address space, NULL for kernel threads */
    struct mm_struct  *active_mm;  /* the mm currently loaded in the CPU's CR3/TTBR */
    ...
};

For normal user processes, mm and active_mm point to the same descriptor. Kernel threads set mm = NULL because they have no user address space, but borrow the previous task's active_mm to avoid an unnecessary TLB flush on context switch (lazy TLB mode — see Chapter 2).

The mmlist field links memory descriptors into a doubly linked list anchored at init_mm.mmlist and protected by mmlist_lock. It is not a list of every memory descriptor: an mm_struct is added to the list only when it first acquires a swapped page entry. The list is walked by try_to_unuse() during swapoff() to find every mm that may reference a given swap area. Memory descriptors that never touch swap are never linked into this list.

/* include/linux/mm_types.h */
struct list_head mmlist;  /* List of maybe swapped mm's.
                           * Globally strung together off
                           * init_mm.mmlist, and protected
                           * by mmlist_lock */

The basic lifetime path begins in kernel/fork.c. The two macros that perform raw slab allocation and release are:

#define allocate_mm()  (kmem_cache_alloc(mm_cachep, GFP_KERNEL))
#define free_mm(mm)    (kmem_cache_free(mm_cachep, (mm)))

New address spaces are allocated by mm_alloc(). Their long tail of cleanup is triggered when the last user reference drops and mmput() calls __mmput(), which tears down mappings through exit_mmap() before finally dropping the structural reference with mmdrop().

/* kernel/fork.c — allocation and last-user release */
struct mm_struct *mm_alloc(void)
{
	struct mm_struct *mm;

	mm = allocate_mm();
	if (!mm)
		return NULL;

	memset(mm, 0, sizeof(*mm));
	return mm_init(mm, current, current_user_ns());
}

void mmput(struct mm_struct *mm)
{
	might_sleep();

	if (atomic_dec_and_test(&mm->mm_users))
		__mmput(mm);
}

static inline void __mmput(struct mm_struct *mm)
{
	...
	exit_mmap(mm);
	...
	mmdrop(mm);
}

There are two deliberate design choices here. First, mm_alloc() does almost nothing itself; the hard work lives in mm_init(), which initializes architecture state, allocates page-table roots, sets up the maple tree, and installs defaults. Second, mmput() is driven by mm_users, not by the raw structural reference count. That distinction lets the kernel answer two different questions independently:

  • How many live users share this address space? That is mm_users.
  • How many total references exist to the structure, including non-user holders? That is mm_count.

mm_users versus mm_count

The comments in include/linux/mm_types.h are unusually explicit, and the distinction is worth memorizing because many MM bugs reduce to getting it wrong. mm_users counts active users “including userspace” and is modified with mmget(), mmget_not_zero(), and mmput(). When it falls to zero, the address space is no longer usable by tasks, so the kernel tears down user mappings and releases one embedded reference on mm_count.

mm_count, in contrast, is the underlying structural reference count. It includes the contribution represented by any nonzero mm_users plus extra references held by code that needs the object to remain allocated even after the last task stops using it — for example, a lazy-TLB CPU still loaded with this mm's PGD, or a /proc/<pid>/maps reader traversing VMAs after the process has already exited. When mm_count falls to zero, the mm_struct slab object itself is freed via __mmdrop().

The two counters are deliberately placed in separate cache lines (mm_count is wrapped in its own ____cacheline_aligned_in_smp struct) because they are written by different subsystems at different frequencies — mm_users changes on fork/exit, while mm_count changes on context switches, lazy-TLB transitions, and procfs access.

That is not the only place where the layout of mm_struct is load-bearing. A comment in the middle of the structure asks future contributors not to insert fields ahead of mmap_lock, because at the current offset the rwsem's two hot fields, count and owner, land in different cache lines; pushing them together would make a contended mmap_lock bounce a single line between every CPU taking it. The declaration order of this structure is therefore partly a performance artifact rather than a logical grouping, which is worth knowing before reading it as an outline of the design.

Linux 7.0.12 has quietly acquired a third notion of "how many". The concurrency-ID machinery keeps its own mm->mm_cid.users, and the comment on it explains why: it counts the tasks that genuinely share the address space, as distinct from mm_users, which is also incremented by mmget() callers that merely want the mm to stay alive while they inspect it. The scheduler needs the first number to size its per-mm concurrency IDs; it would get a wrong answer from the second. Even the use-count, in other words, turned out to be conflating two things.

Table 9-3. mm_struct reference-count API
FunctionCounterEffect
mmget(mm)mm_users++Pin the address space (prevents teardown of mappings)
mmget_not_zero(mm)mm_users++ if > 0Conditional pin — returns false if the mm is already dying
mmput(mm)mm_users--Release user ref; if it drops to 0, calls __mmput() → exit_mmap() → mmdrop()
mmgrab(mm)mm_count++Pin the structure (prevents slab free)
mmdrop(mm)mm_count--Release structural ref; if it drops to 0, calls __mmdrop() → free_mm()
mmdrop_lazy_tlb(mm)mm_count--Variant for lazy-TLB release, may be a no-op on architectures that track differently

The "use-count versus reference-count" split is a pattern that appears elsewhere in the kernel, but it is especially important in MM because teardown is multi-stage. Destroying page tables too early is catastrophic; freeing the container structure too late is a leak; and mixing the two notions into a single counter would make common transitions such as remote access, procfs inspection, and kernel-thread borrowing far more fragile. The kernel documentation at Documentation/mm/active_mm.rst provides an in-depth explanation.

Kernel threads and borrowed address spaces

Kernel threads usually have task_struct->mm == NULL. They execute in kernel mode only and never need their own page tables for user-space addresses. But the CPU still requires some set of page tables loaded in its page-table-base register (CR3 on x86_64, TTBR0_EL1 on ARM64) even when running kernel code. Switching page tables is expensive because it may flush the TLB, so Linux avoids it with a technique called lazy TLB.

Lazy TLB: how the scheduler borrows an mm

When the scheduler context-switches from a user process to a kernel thread, it does not load a new page-table root. Instead, the kernel thread simply inherits the previous process's active_mm. The relevant logic in kernel/sched/core.c covers four cases:

/* kernel/sched/core.c — context_switch() mm handling, abbreviated */
if (!next->mm) {                            /* switching TO kernel thread */
    enter_lazy_tlb(prev->active_mm, next);
    next->active_mm = prev->active_mm;
    if (prev->mm)                            /* coming FROM user process */
        mmgrab_lazy_tlb(prev->active_mm);    /* bump mm_count */
    else
        prev->active_mm = NULL;              /* kernel→kernel: transfer */
} else {                                     /* switching TO user process */
    switch_mm_irqs_off(prev->active_mm, next->mm, next);
    if (!prev->mm) {                         /* coming FROM kernel thread */
        rq->prev_mm = prev->active_mm;      /* mmdrop_lazy_tlb() later */
        prev->active_mm = NULL;
    }
}

The four transitions work as follows:

  • User → kernel: the kernel thread borrows the user process's active_mm, and mmgrab_lazy_tlb() increments mm_count to keep the mm_struct alive while the TLB still references its page tables.
  • Kernel → kernel: the borrowed active_mm is simply transferred from the outgoing kernel thread to the incoming one — no extra reference needed.
  • Kernel → user: the scheduler calls switch_mm_irqs_off() to load the user process's own page tables into the hardware. The old borrowed mm is dropped via mmdrop_lazy_tlb() from finish_task_switch().
  • User → user: a straightforward page-table switch with no lazy-TLB involvement.

On some architectures, lazy-TLB references do not even need to increment mm_count at all. When CONFIG_MMU_LAZY_TLB_REFCOUNT is disabled, mmgrab_lazy_tlb() becomes a no-op and mmdrop_lazy_tlb() reduces to just a memory barrier. This saves atomic operations on the hot context-switch path, but requires architecture code to shoot down lazy-TLB references via IPI when an mm_struct is being freed.

Intentionally adopting a user address space

The lazy-TLB mechanism is passive — the kernel thread does not actually access user-space memory through the borrowed mm. Sometimes, however, a kernel thread must intentionally operate in a user address space — for example, to access user mappings on behalf of an I/O or migration path. Linux provides kthread_use_mm() and kthread_unuse_mm() for this purpose in kernel/kthread.c.

Older discussions often called this pattern use_mm()/unuse_mm(). In current mainline code the public helpers are named kthread_use_mm() and kthread_unuse_mm(), which makes their intended caller class explicit and avoids suggesting that arbitrary code may casually borrow an address space.

/* kernel/kthread.c — abbreviated borrowed-mm path */
void kthread_use_mm(struct mm_struct *mm)
{
	struct mm_struct *active_mm;
	struct task_struct *tsk = current;

	WARN_ON_ONCE(!(tsk->flags & PF_KTHREAD));
	WARN_ON_ONCE(tsk->mm);
	WARN_ON_ONCE(!mm->user_ns);

	mmgrab(mm);                              /* structural ref */
	...
	active_mm = tsk->active_mm;
	tsk->active_mm = mm;
	tsk->mm = mm;                            /* now "looks like" a user task */
	membarrier_update_current_mm(mm);
	switch_mm_irqs_off(active_mm, mm, tsk);  /* load mm's page tables */
	...
	mmdrop_lazy_tlb(active_mm);              /* release old borrowed mm */
}

void kthread_unuse_mm(struct mm_struct *mm)
{
	...
	tsk->mm = NULL;                          /* back to being a kernel thread */
	membarrier_update_current_mm(NULL);
	mmgrab_lazy_tlb(mm);                     /* convert to lazy-TLB ref */
	enter_lazy_tlb(mm, tsk);                 /* re-enter lazy mode */
	...
	mmdrop(mm);                              /* release the structural ref */
}

The key detail is that kthread_use_mm() sets tsk->mm = mm, temporarily making the kernel thread appear as a user-space task. This is necessary because many kernel subsystems check current->mm to decide whether they can walk user page tables, call get_user_pages(), or handle page faults. When kthread_unuse_mm() reverses this, it does not reload the old page tables — it simply converts the mm into a lazy-TLB reference via mmgrab_lazy_tlb() and enter_lazy_tlb(), leaving the page tables loaded until the next context switch to a user process.

A number of subsystems rely on this mechanism in practice. Among the most prominent callers:

  • vhost — the vhost kernel thread calls kthread_use_mm() to access virtio ring buffers that live in the QEMU process's address space.
  • GPU drivers — AMD KFD and Intel Xe use it when kernel-side work queues must access GPU command buffers mapped into user space.
  • VFIO / IOMMUFD — IOMMU page-table management threads need to pin and walk user pages for DMA mapping.
  • USB gadget — FunctionFS I/O worker threads access user buffers via borrowed mm.
  • DMA engines — Intel IDXD uses it for user-space descriptor processing.

This interface highlights a modern subtlety: an mm_struct is not merely "owned by a process." It is a first-class kernel object that can be shared by threads, observed remotely, temporarily adopted by kernel workers, and torn down only when both user-level and structural references vanish. That is one reason the structure carries synchronization state such as membarrier_state and mm_lock_seq alongside plain mapping metadata.

Compared with Linux 2.6, the most visible structural differences are easy to summarize:

  • mm_rb is gone; mm_mt is the canonical VMA index.
  • mmap_sem is now mmap_lock.
  • per-VMA locking adds mm_lock_seq and VMA-side sequence/refcount fields when configured.
  • write_protect_seq and membarrier_state capture synchronization roles that did not exist in Linux 2.6.
  • RSS moved from a per-task cache of a shared counter to percpu_counter rss_stat[], with separate approximate and exact readers.
  • flags grew from an unsigned long into a 64-bit private bitmap reachable only through mm_flags_*().
  • mm_cid and pgtables_bytes are entirely new: concurrency IDs for rseq, and an accounting of what the page tables themselves cost.

Memory Regions (vm_area_struct)

If mm_struct is the address-space container, vm_area_struct is the semantic building block. A VMA describes one contiguous interval of user virtual addresses for which Linux can treat permissions, backing store, page offset, fault callbacks, reverse mapping, and policy as uniform. The page tables may or may not contain present entries for that interval at any particular moment, but the VMA says what the interval means.

Modern Linux keeps much more state here than it did in Linux 2.6. Some of it reflects new features: vm_userfaultfd_ctx for userfaultfd, anon_name for naming anonymous mappings, vm_lock_seq and vm_refcnt for per-VMA locking. Some of it reflects the move away from list-oriented traversal toward maple-tree indexing. And some of it reflects the fact that filesystems, drivers, huge-page code, NUMA policy, KSM, and userfaultfd all need hooks at VMA granularity.

The following figure shows how all these descriptors connect in the 7.0 kernel. Compared with Linux 2.6, the linked list and rb-tree are gone; the maple tree is the single VMA index; per-VMA locking is new; and each VMA may carry userfaultfd context, NUMA policy, and an anonymous name.

VMA allocation and slab cache

Like mm_struct, VMA structures are allocated from a dedicated slab cache. The cache is initialized at boot time in mm/vma_init.c:

/* mm/vma_init.c — VMA slab cache setup */
static struct kmem_cache *vm_area_cachep;

void __init vma_state_init(void)
{
	struct kmem_cache_args args = {
		.use_freeptr_offset = true,
		.freeptr_offset = offsetof(struct vm_area_struct, vm_freeptr),
		.sheaf_capacity = 32,
	};

	vm_area_cachep = kmem_cache_create("vm_area_struct",
			sizeof(struct vm_area_struct), &args,
			SLAB_HWCACHE_ALIGN|SLAB_PANIC|SLAB_TYPESAFE_BY_RCU|
			SLAB_ACCOUNT);
}

Several details deserve attention:

  • SLAB_TYPESAFE_BY_RCU — unlike mm_cachep, the VMA cache uses type-safe-by-RCU semantics. This means the slab allocator guarantees that freed VMA memory is not reused for a different object type during an RCU grace period. In practice, a freed VMA slot may be immediately reused for another VMA, but RCU readers can still safely dereference the pointer and then validate the object (e.g., via vm_refcnt). This enables lockless VMA lookups during page faults under per-VMA locking without requiring an explicit call_rcu() on every VMA free.
  • vm_freeptr — when a VMA is free in the slab, the allocator stores its free-pointer inside the vm_start/vm_end union via the freeptr_t overlay, avoiding the need for a separate free-pointer field and saving space.
  • sheaf_capacity = 32 — a performance hint that tells the slab allocator to batch up to 32 VMA objects per CPU-local sheaf, reducing contention on the hot alloc/free path during operations like fork() and exit() that create or destroy many VMAs in rapid succession.

The lifecycle helpers are straightforward:

/* mm/vma_init.c — VMA lifecycle */
struct vm_area_struct *vm_area_alloc(struct mm_struct *mm)
{
	struct vm_area_struct *vma;
	vma = kmem_cache_alloc(vm_area_cachep, GFP_KERNEL);
	if (!vma) return NULL;
	vma_init(vma, mm);
	return vma;
}

struct vm_area_struct *vm_area_dup(struct vm_area_struct *orig)
{
	struct vm_area_struct *new = kmem_cache_alloc(vm_area_cachep, GFP_KERNEL);
	if (!new) return NULL;
	vm_area_init_from(orig, new);   /* copy all fields */
	vma_lock_init(new, true);       /* fresh per-VMA lock state */
	INIT_LIST_HEAD(&new->anon_vma_chain);
	vma_numab_state_init(new);
	dup_anon_vma_name(orig, new);
	return new;
}

void vm_area_free(struct vm_area_struct *vma)
{
	vma_assert_detached(vma);       /* must be removed from maple tree first */
	vma_numab_state_free(vma);
	free_anon_vma_name(vma);
	kmem_cache_free(vm_area_cachep, vma);
}

Notice the assertion in vm_area_free(): a VMA must be detached from its address space (removed from the maple tree and marked with vm_refcnt == 0) before it can be freed. This prevents use-after-free bugs where a concurrent fault handler still holds a reference to a VMA being destroyed.

Structure layout and cache-line placement

The vm_area_struct layout is carefully arranged for performance. The kernel source comment says "the first cache line has the info for VMA tree walking" — vm_start, vm_end, vm_mm, vm_page_prot, and vm_flags are grouped at the top of the structure so that tree lookups and fault-path decisions can complete without touching a second cache line in the common case.

Conversely, vm_refcnt carries ____cacheline_aligned_in_smp to ensure that the per-VMA lock's refcount sits on its own cache line. This prevents false sharing between the hot read path (lookups and faults that read vm_start/vm_end/vm_flags) and the per-VMA lock's atomic operations.

The entire structure is annotated with __randomize_layout, which tells the compiler's structure-layout randomization plugin (when enabled) to shuffle field order for hardening. This means that field offsets are not stable across builds and cannot be relied upon by out-of-tree code.

/* include/linux/mm_types.h — abbreviated struct vm_area_struct */
struct vm_area_struct {
	/* First cache line: VMA tree walking */
	union {
		struct {
			unsigned long vm_start;
			unsigned long vm_end;
		};
		freeptr_t vm_freeptr;    /* SLAB_TYPESAFE_BY_RCU free pointer */
	};

	struct mm_struct *vm_mm;
	pgprot_t vm_page_prot;
	union {
		const vm_flags_t vm_flags;
		vma_flags_t flags;
	};
#ifdef CONFIG_PER_VMA_LOCK
	unsigned int vm_lock_seq;
#endif

	/* Reverse mapping */
	struct list_head anon_vma_chain;
	struct anon_vma *anon_vma;

	/* Callbacks and backing store */
	const struct vm_operations_struct *vm_ops;
	unsigned long vm_pgoff;
	struct file *vm_file;
	void *vm_private_data;

#ifdef CONFIG_SWAP
	atomic_long_t swap_readahead_info;
#endif
#ifdef CONFIG_NUMA
	struct mempolicy *vm_policy;
#endif
#ifdef CONFIG_NUMA_BALANCING
	struct vma_numab_state *numab_state;
#endif

	/* Per-VMA lock — own cache line to avoid false sharing */
#ifdef CONFIG_PER_VMA_LOCK
	refcount_t vm_refcnt ____cacheline_aligned_in_smp;
#endif

	/* File-backed VMAs: linkage into address_space->i_mmap interval tree */
	struct {
		struct rb_node rb;
		unsigned long rb_subtree_last;
	} shared;

#ifdef CONFIG_ANON_VMA_NAME
	struct anon_vma_name *anon_name;
#endif
	struct vm_userfaultfd_ctx vm_userfaultfd_ctx;
#ifdef __HAVE_PFNMAP_TRACKING
	struct pfnmap_track_ctx *pfnmap_track_ctx;
#endif
} __randomize_layout;
Table 9-4. Key Fields of struct vm_area_struct
Field Meaning Operational importance
vm_start / vm_end Half-open virtual address interval. Every lookup, fault, split, merge, and procfs dump begins here.
vm_mm Owning address space. Provides access to locks, accounting, page tables, and layout policy.
vm_page_prot Architecture-specific page protection encoding. Used when materializing or changing page-table entries.
vm_flags Bitmask of permissions and mapping attributes. Encodes read/write/exec/shared, growth direction, huge-page hints, locking, userfaultfd state, and more.
vm_ops Operations callbacks for this VMA. Lets filesystems and special mappings participate in faults, splitting, mprotect, and naming.
vm_pgoff Page offset within the file or anonymous object. Critical for merge checks, file-backed lookups, and fault computation.
vm_file Backing file, or NULL for anonymous mappings. Distinguishes file-backed and anonymous VMAs and links into file mapping structures.
anon_vma Reverse-mapping root for anonymous memory. Needed for copy-on-write, reclaim, migration, and KSM interactions.
anon_vma_chain Links the VMA into anon_vma interval trees. Supports reverse mapping and merge rules.
vm_private_data Mapping-specific opaque pointer. Used heavily by drivers and special filesystem mappings.
swap_readahead_info Tracks swap readahead window and position. Guides how many swap pages to prefetch on a swap fault; per-VMA so different regions get independent readahead.
vm_policy NUMA policy for the interval. May block merging even when other attributes match.
numab_state NUMA balancing scan state. Tracks per-VMA scan timing and PID activity for automatic NUMA page migration under CONFIG_NUMA_BALANCING.
vm_userfaultfd_ctx Userfaultfd registration context. Part of merge compatibility and fault dispatch.
anon_name Optional human-readable name for anonymous mappings. Visible in diagnostics and also checked for merge compatibility.
shared Linkage into the file's address_space->i_mmap interval tree. Used by the reverse mapping layer to find all VMAs that map a given file range, enabling page reclaim and truncation.
pfnmap_track_ctx Tracks PFN-mapped regions for lifetime management. Ensures driver-mapped PFN ranges (VM_PFNMAP) are properly reference-counted across VMA duplication and destruction.
vm_lock_seq Per-VMA lock sequence value. Pairs with mm->mm_lock_seq when per-VMA locking is enabled.
vm_refcnt Per-VMA reader/write-lock coordination refcount. Allows scalable fault-time read-side locking under CONFIG_PER_VMA_LOCK.

VMA Flags: Declaration and Modification

Before looking at what the individual flags mean, it is worth looking at how they are declared, because that changed in a way that affects every piece of code which touches them. In the 2.6 kernel the VM_ constants were hand-written hexadecimal masks in a single unsigned long, and any code that held a VMA could write them directly. In 7.0.12 neither of those things is true.

The flags are now declared as numbered bit positions in an enumeration, and the familiar mask names are derived from them:

/* include/linux/mm.h — flags are bit numbers first, masks second */
typedef int __bitwise vma_flag_t;

#define DECLARE_VMA_BIT(name, bitnum) \
	VMA_ ## name ## _BIT = ((__force vma_flag_t)bitnum)
#define DECLARE_VMA_BIT_ALIAS(name, aliased) \
	VMA_ ## name ## _BIT = (VMA_ ## aliased ## _BIT)

enum {
	DECLARE_VMA_BIT(READ, 0),
	DECLARE_VMA_BIT(WRITE, 1),
	DECLARE_VMA_BIT(EXEC, 2),
	DECLARE_VMA_BIT(SHARED, 3),
	/* mprotect() hardcodes VM_MAYREAD >> 4 == VM_READ, and so for r/w/x bits. */
	DECLARE_VMA_BIT(MAYREAD, 4),    /* limits for mprotect() etc. */
	DECLARE_VMA_BIT(MAYWRITE, 5),
	DECLARE_VMA_BIT(MAYEXEC, 6),
	DECLARE_VMA_BIT(MAYSHARE, 7),
	...
	DECLARE_VMA_BIT(HIGH_ARCH_0, 32),
	...
	DECLARE_VMA_BIT(DROPPABLE, 40),
	DECLARE_VMA_BIT(UFFD_MINOR, 41),
	DECLARE_VMA_BIT(SEALED, 42),
};

#define INIT_VM_FLAG(name) BIT((__force int) VMA_ ## name ## _BIT)
#define VM_READ		INIT_VM_FLAG(READ)
#define VM_WRITE	INIT_VM_FLAG(WRITE)

Two things forced this. The first is simply that the flag space outgrew a 32-bit word long ago and is now past bit 42, so on any architecture the constants have to be built rather than written out. The second is type safety: vma_flag_t is declared int __bitwise, which makes sparse complain if a bit number is passed where a bit mask is expected, or the reverse. That is a real class of bug in code that manipulates both forms, and it used to be invisible because both were plain integers.

The enumeration also makes visible something the old hex constants hid: several high bits are aliased per architecture. Bits 32 through 38 are declared as generic HIGH_ARCH_ slots, and each architecture maps its own features onto them. Protection keys claim HIGH_ARCH_0 through HIGH_ARCH_4; VM_SHADOW_STACK is HIGH_ARCH_5 when the kernel is built for Intel CET, but HIGH_ARCH_6 when it is built for ARM64’s Guarded Control Stack, because ARM64 has already spent HIGH_ARCH_4 and HIGH_ARCH_5 on memory tagging. This is the mechanical form of the point made earlier in the chapter: the two architectures share the vocabulary, and differ in which bit the word is stored in.

Storage changed too. The field is no longer a bare unsigned long but a union of a const mask and a writable bitmap:

/* include/linux/mm_types.h */
	/*
	 * Flags, see mm.h.
	 * To modify use vm_flags_{init|reset|set|clear|mod} functions.
	 * Preferably, use vma_flags_xxx() functions.
	 */
	union {
		/* Temporary while VMA flags are being converted. */
		const vm_flags_t vm_flags;
		vma_flags_t flags;
	};

The const is the interesting half. Every existing read of vma->vm_flags — and there are thousands — keeps compiling unchanged, while every direct write stops compiling. Writers must go through the accessors, and the accessors are where the real rule lives:

/* include/linux/mm.h — modification takes the per-VMA write lock for you */
static inline void vm_flags_set(struct vm_area_struct *vma, vm_flags_t flags)
{
	vma_start_write(vma);
	vma_flags_set_word(&vma->flags, flags);
}

static inline void vm_flags_clear(struct vm_area_struct *vma, vm_flags_t flags)
{
	VM_WARN_ON_ONCE(!pgtable_supports_soft_dirty() && (flags & VM_SOFTDIRTY));
	vma_start_write(vma);
	vma_flags_clear_word(&vma->flags, flags);
}

/* Caller already holds the write lock; these only assert it. */
static inline void vm_flags_reset(struct vm_area_struct *vma, vm_flags_t flags)
{
	vma_assert_write_locked(vma);
	vm_flags_init(vma, flags);
}

/* Use only if VMA is not part of the VMA tree and therefore needs no locking. */
static inline void __vm_flags_mod(struct vm_area_struct *vma,
				  vm_flags_t set, vm_flags_t clear)
{
	vm_flags_init(vma, (vma->vm_flags | set) & ~clear);
}

Changing a flag on a live VMA and taking that VMA’s write lock are now the same operation. vm_flags_set() and vm_flags_clear() call vma_start_write() themselves; vm_flags_reset() and vm_flags_reset_once() assume the caller already did and assert it; and the double-underscore __vm_flags_mod() exists only for VMAs that are not yet in the maple tree and therefore have no other observers. Under per-VMA locking a fault handler may be reading vm_flags at this instant with nothing but RCU protecting the structure, so an unsynchronized write is a correctness bug rather than a style question — and the const is there to make that bug unrepresentable rather than merely discouraged.

The bitmap side of the union, vma_flags_t, is a DECLARE_BITMAP whose width is NUM_VMA_FLAG_BITS, currently defined as BITS_PER_LONG. So on the 64-bit architectures this book follows nothing has grown yet; what has been put in place is the machinery to grow past a single word without touching every user, exactly as mm_flags_t already did for the address-space flags. The kernel comment says as much: “Temporary while VMA flags are being converted.”

The vm_flags field is a bitmask that encodes a VMA's permissions, behavior, and special attributes. The kernel checks these flags on virtually every memory operation — fault handling, mprotect(), merge/split decisions, /proc/<pid>/maps output, and reverse-mapping walks. Some flags correspond directly to user-visible mmap() or mprotect() arguments (VM_READ, VM_WRITE, VM_EXEC, VM_SHARED); others are set internally by the kernel to mark special mapping semantics. Modern Linux has significantly expanded this set compared to 2.6, adding flags for userfaultfd modes, transparent huge pages, droppable volatile pages, and lock-on-fault behavior.

The four VM_MAY bits deserve a note of their own, because they are the flags most often misread. They are not permissions; they are the ceiling on permissions. When a mapping is created they are derived once from how the underlying file was opened — a file opened read-only yields a VMA with VM_MAYREAD but not VM_MAYWRITE — and thereafter mprotect() may raise or lower VM_READ, VM_WRITE and VM_EXEC only within that ceiling. This is why mprotect(PROT_WRITE) on a read-only file mapping fails with EACCES rather than silently succeeding. The bit numbering is not accidental either: the MAY bits are placed exactly four positions above the bits they bound, and mm/mprotect.c relies on that, converting a requested permission set into the corresponding ceiling with a shift rather than a table lookup. The kernel comment on the enumeration says so explicitly, which is a warning to anyone tempted to renumber them.

Flags also play a critical role in VMA merging: two adjacent VMAs can only be merged if their vm_flags are identical (among other conditions). This means that a single mprotect() call that changes permissions on part of a mapping will split the VMA and prevent future re-merging until the flags match again.

Table 9-5. Selected vm_flags Bits Used in Modern Linux
Flag Meaning Typical source
VM_READReadable mapping.PROT_READ
VM_WRITEWritable mapping.PROT_WRITE
VM_EXECExecutable mapping.PROT_EXEC or READ_IMPLIES_EXEC
VM_SHAREDShared mapping semantics.MAP_SHARED or MAP_SHARED_VALIDATE
VM_MAYREAD, VM_MAYWRITE, VM_MAYEXEC, VM_MAYSHAREThe ceiling: what this mapping could ever be allowed to become.Fixed at mmap() time from the file’s open mode; mprotect() can only move the corresponding VM_READ/VM_WRITE/VM_EXEC bits within it
VM_GROWSDOWNDownward-growing stack-like region.Stacks and guard-gap handling

VM_MIXEDMAPMapping may contain arbitrary PFNs.Device or special mappings

VM_SYNCSynchronous page faults.MAP_SYNC on DAX mappings

Operations on a VMA (vm_operations_struct)

Each VMA carries a pointer to a vm_operations_struct — a table of callbacks that lets filesystems, device drivers, and special mappings participate in the kernel's memory management decisions. When the kernel needs to handle a page fault, split or merge a VMA, change protections, or report a name in /proc/<pid>/maps, it calls the corresponding hook if one is registered. A NULL vm_ops pointer (common for simple anonymous mappings) causes the kernel to use its built-in default behavior for each operation.

The most performance-critical hook is fault, which the page fault handler invokes to resolve a missing or invalid page. For file-backed mappings, this typically reads from the page cache; for device mappings, it may program MMIO entries directly. The huge_fault hook provides a parallel path for transparent huge pages. The map_pages hook enables fault-around — pre-mapping several neighboring pages in a single fault to reduce future fault overhead.

/* include/linux/mm.h — selected vm_operations_struct hooks */
struct vm_operations_struct {
	void (*open)(struct vm_area_struct *area);
	void (*close)(struct vm_area_struct *area);
	int (*may_split)(struct vm_area_struct *area, unsigned long addr);
	int (*mremap)(struct vm_area_struct *area);
	int (*mprotect)(struct vm_area_struct *vma, unsigned long start,
			unsigned long end, unsigned long newflags);
	vm_fault_t (*fault)(struct vm_fault *vmf);
	vm_fault_t (*huge_fault)(struct vm_fault *vmf, unsigned int order);
	vm_fault_t (*map_pages)(struct vm_fault *vmf,
			pgoff_t start_pgoff, pgoff_t end_pgoff);
	unsigned long (*pagesize)(struct vm_area_struct *area);
	vm_fault_t (*page_mkwrite)(struct vm_fault *vmf);
	vm_fault_t (*pfn_mkwrite)(struct vm_fault *vmf);
	int (*access)(struct vm_area_struct *vma, unsigned long addr,
		      void *buf, int len, int write);
	const char *(*name)(struct vm_area_struct *vma);
	...
};
Table 9-6. Important Hooks in struct vm_operations_struct
Hook When the kernel calls it Typical users
openWhen a VMA is opened or duplicated.Filesystems, drivers, special mappings
closeWhen a VMA is removed.Resource release and accounting rollback
may_splitBefore the kernel splits the VMA.Drivers that cannot tolerate arbitrary subranges
mremapWhen a mapping is resized or moved.Special mappings with extra relocation rules
mprotectBefore finalizing a protection change.Driver or filesystem permission checks
faultOn a normal page fault.Page cache, anonymous fault paths, drivers
huge_faultOn a huge-page fault.THP or hugetlb-aware mappings
map_pagesTo map a fault-around range.Page-cache-backed filesystems
pagesizeTo report mapping-specific page size.Special page-size-aware mappings
page_mkwriteBefore making a read-only page writable.Filesystems enforcing writeback or journaling rules
pfn_mkwriteEquivalent write-enable hook for PFN or mixed maps.Drivers and DAX-style mappings
accessFallback for access_process_vm().Special VMAs without normal GUP semantics
nameWhen /proc/<pid>/maps wants a special label.Anonymous names, vdso-like regions, driver maps
find_special_pageWhen dumping or resolving nonstandard pages.Special mappings with synthetic pages

A single process normally contains far fewer VMAs than PTEs, but the VMA count is still performance-sensitive. Each split increases metadata overhead, lookup work, and locking pressure; each successful merge reduces it. That is why Linux makes merge compatibility surprisingly strict: if adjacent ranges really are equivalent, keeping them separate would only punish every later lookup.

The Maple Tree — VMA Storage

In Linux 2.6, the kernel maintained two parallel data structures to index VMAs inside an mm_struct: a red-black tree (mm->mm_rb) for O(log n) lookup by address, and a doubly linked list (mm->mmap) for ordered iteration. Every VMA carried both an rb_node and list pointers, and every insert, remove, or merge had to update both structures atomically. This dual-index design worked for the workloads of its era, but it became increasingly problematic as VMA counts grew into the tens of thousands on modern systems:

  • Poor cache locality — each rb-tree node held a single VMA pointer, so a lookup touching O(log n) nodes scattered across memory generated many cache misses.
  • Lockless traversal was impractical — the linked list provided no safe way for RCU readers to traverse while writers were modifying the tree, forcing all VMA operations to serialize on the heavy mmap_sem.
  • No native range support — VMAs are inherently intervals, but the rb-tree compared only start addresses, requiring extra logic for gap-finding and overlapping-range queries.

Starting with Linux 6.1, the kernel replaced both structures with a single maple tree — a B-tree variant specifically designed for range-indexed data. The maple tree was developed by Liam Howlett at Oracle and merged after extensive testing and benchmarking. In 7.0.12, the maple tree is the sole VMA index: mm->mm_mt (of type struct maple_tree) replaces both mm->mm_rb and the mm->mmap linked list.

The maple tree matters for three reasons:

  • Range-centric lookups: VMAs are inherently intervals, and maple-tree APIs speak in interval terms rather than in node-by-node tree manipulations.
  • Cache efficiency: internal nodes store multiple pivots and slots, so traversals typically touch fewer cache lines than a classic binary tree with one object per node.
  • RCU friendliness: the implementation was designed with modern lockless and restartable traversal patterns in mind, which is crucial for scalable fault and inspection paths.

The core generic API lives in include/linux/maple_tree.h. Internally, each maple tree node is 256 bytes — exactly four 64-byte cache lines — and comes in several variants. The most common for VMA storage is maple_arange_64, which stores up to 10 pivot/slot pairs plus a gap tracking field for efficient free-space searches (used by mmap() to find unmapped regions). The wide fan-out means a tree with 1,000 VMAs typically requires only 3–4 levels, touching at most a few kilobytes of memory per lookup. Nodes are freed via RCU, allowing lockless readers to traverse the tree safely even while writers are modifying it.

At the lowest level, MM code manipulates a struct ma_state cursor and uses helpers such as mas_walk(), mas_find(), mas_store(), and mas_erase(). The VMA layer wraps these with iterator helpers that read more like address-space operations than generic tree operations.

/* include/linux/maple_tree.h and include/linux/mm.h — core APIs */
void *mas_walk(struct ma_state *mas);
void *mas_store(struct ma_state *mas, void *entry);
void *mas_erase(struct ma_state *mas);
void *mas_find(struct ma_state *mas, unsigned long max);

static inline void vma_iter_init(struct vma_iterator *vmi,
		struct mm_struct *mm, unsigned long addr)
{
	mas_init(&vmi->mas, &mm->mm_mt, addr);
}

static inline struct vm_area_struct *vma_find(struct vma_iterator *vmi,
		unsigned long max)
{
	return mas_find(&vmi->mas, max - 1);
}

#define for_each_vma(__vmi, __vma) 	while (((__vma) = vma_next(&(__vmi))) != NULL)

These wrappers are not cosmetic. They encode MM-specific conventions such as half-open intervals, VMA iteration order, and the fact that many operations want “the first VMA whose end exceeds this address” rather than “the exact slot containing this key.” As a result, new MM code often reads in terms of VMA_ITERATOR(vmi, mm, addr), vma_find(), and for_each_vma() instead of manually walking tree nodes.

Writing to the tree follows a protocol that is worth knowing, because it explains an otherwise puzzling pair of calls scattered through mm/vma.c. Storing an entry in a maple tree may need to allocate internal nodes, and a failure part-way through a store would leave the index in a state the caller cannot undo. The MM layer therefore splits the operation in two: it preallocates whatever nodes the store could possibly need, at a point where returning -ENOMEM is still harmless, and then performs a store that cannot fail.

/* mm/vma.h — allocate first, then commit */
static inline int vma_iter_prealloc(struct vma_iterator *vmi,
		struct vm_area_struct *vma)
{
	return mas_preallocate(&vmi->mas, vma, GFP_KERNEL);
}

static inline void vma_iter_clear(struct vma_iterator *vmi)
{
	mas_store_prealloc(&vmi->mas, NULL);	/* returns void: cannot fail */
}

The signatures tell the story: vma_iter_prealloc() returns an error code, mas_store_prealloc() returns void. This is why VMA operations that modify the tree do all of their fallible work — allocating the new VMA, preallocating tree nodes, checking map_count — before they change anything, and why their error paths are correspondingly short. It also means the node allocation happens with GFP_KERNEL in ordinary context, rather than under whatever constraints happened to apply at the moment the tree was being restructured.

The maple tree did not eliminate every rb-tree from VMA-related code. For example, shared file mappings still use interval-tree machinery inside the file's address-space metadata, and vm_area_struct still contains a small shared rb-node bundle for that purpose. What changed is the process-private index of VMAs inside mm_struct.

VMA Lookup

Looking up a VMA by virtual address is one of the most frequent operations in the memory management subsystem. Every page fault must find the VMA that governs the faulting address. Every mprotect(), munmap(), madvise(), and mremap() call starts by locating the first VMA that overlaps its target range. Remote operations like process_vm_readv() and /proc/<pid>/maps iterate over ranges of VMAs. Even the OOM killer inspects VMAs to estimate a process's memory footprint.

In the Linux 2.6 era, find_vma() walked the rb-tree and then cached the result in mm->mmap_cache — a single-entry per-mm cache that accelerated the common case of repeated lookups in the same region (e.g., successive page faults within one mapping). That cache is gone in modern kernels: the maple tree's wider fan-out and better cache locality make a single-entry cache unnecessary, and removing it also eliminates a source of false sharing on SMP systems.

Four lookup helpers recur throughout MM code, and their differences matter:

  • find_vma(mm, addr) returns the first VMA with vm_end > addr. That may be the containing VMA or the next VMA after a hole.
  • vma_lookup(mm, addr) is a stricter point lookup and returns a VMA only when addr lies inside [vm_start, vm_end).
  • find_vma_prev(mm, addr, &prev) also reports the predecessor VMA.
  • find_vma_intersection(mm, start, end) returns the first VMA intersecting a range.

The distinction between find_vma() and vma_lookup() is a classic source of confusion. find_vma() answers “where should I continue scanning from here?” and is therefore ideal for interval algorithms. vma_lookup() answers “is this exact address already mapped?” and is ideal when a fault, access check, or remote operation needs a precise containing VMA. The implementation mirrors this difference: find_vma() uses a maple-tree range search; vma_lookup() is an exact-key load.

/* mm/mmap.c — find_vma() */
struct vm_area_struct *find_vma(struct mm_struct *mm, unsigned long addr)
{
	unsigned long index = addr;

	mmap_assert_locked(mm);
	return mt_find(&mm->mm_mt, &index, ULONG_MAX);
}

/* include/linux/mm.h — vma_lookup() */
static inline struct vm_area_struct *vma_lookup(struct mm_struct *mm,
					unsigned long addr)
{
	return mtree_load(&mm->mm_mt, addr);
}

The naming now makes more sense if you read it in maple-tree terms. mt_find() advances through the interval index until it finds a range overlapping or following the starting key. mtree_load() expects an exact slot for the key. That also explains why find_vma_intersection() is expressed as a range query that supplies both start and end bounds, while find_vma_prev() is written in terms of a VMA iterator and vma_prev().

These helpers show up everywhere:

  • mmap() uses find_vma_intersection() to implement MAP_FIXED_NOREPLACE.
  • munmap() and mprotect() begin by locating the first overlapping VMA.
  • fault paths use precise point lookups.
  • remote access interfaces such as process_vm_readv() and process_madvise() iterate over ranges of VMAs.

Under the old rb-tree model, textbooks often emphasized the asymptotic complexity of tree search. In the maple-tree era, it is more useful pedagogically to emphasize range semantics and restartable traversal under concurrency. The big win is not just “still logarithmic.” It is that the operations the MM subsystem actually needs are now the ones directly expressed by the data structure.

VMA Merging and Splitting

Merging and splitting are the two operations that shape the VMA topology at runtime. Every time the kernel creates, removes, or modifies a mapping, it must decide whether the result can be folded into an adjacent VMA (merge) or whether an existing VMA must be broken apart (split). Getting this right is crucial for performance: fewer VMAs mean faster lookups, less memory overhead, and lower locking pressure; but incorrect merging could silently combine regions with different semantics.

To appreciate why these operations matter, consider what happens during a single mprotect() call that changes permissions on the middle third of a large anonymous mapping. The kernel must:

  1. First try to merge the modified range into a neighbour, in case the new flags happen to match what is already next door.
  2. Only if that fails, split off the preceding portion of the VMA, then split off the trailing portion, leaving the middle piece as the one to modify.
  3. Take the VMA’s write lock, install the new vm_flags, and recompute vm_page_prot from them.
  4. Walk the page tables over the range, rewriting present PTEs and flushing the affected TLB entries.

The order of the first two steps is worth dwelling on, because it is the reverse of what the operation appears to do. The kernel does not split and then look for a merge; it attempts the merge first and treats splitting as the fallback. The reason is that a split is expensive and, worse, it is the step that can fail: it allocates a VMA and it is the only place that can hit the map_count ceiling. Trying the cheap outcome first means that the common case of an mprotect() that simply extends an existing permission region costs no allocation at all.

All of this lives in one place. vma_modify() in mm/vma.c is the single entry point for “change the attributes of part of a VMA”, and mprotect(), madvise(), mlock(), mseal() and userfaultfd registration all reach it through thin wrappers such as vma_modify_flags():

/* mm/vma.c — merge first, split only as a fallback */
static struct vm_area_struct *vma_modify(struct vma_merge_struct *vmg)
{
	struct vm_area_struct *vma = vmg->middle;
	unsigned long start = vmg->start, end = vmg->end;
	struct vm_area_struct *merged;

	/* First, try to merge. */
	merged = vma_merge_existing_range(vmg);
	if (merged)
		return merged;
	if (vmg_nomem(vmg))
		return ERR_PTR(-ENOMEM);

	/* Split any preceding portion of the VMA. */
	if (vma->vm_start < start) {
		int err = split_vma(vmg->vmi, vma, start, 1);
		if (err)
			return ERR_PTR(err);
	}

	/* Split any trailing portion of the VMA. */
	if (vma->vm_end > end) {
		int err = split_vma(vmg->vmi, vma, end, 0);
		if (err)
			return ERR_PTR(err);
	}

	return vma;
}

That single point of entry is a genuine change from the 2.6 code, where each caller open-coded its own sequence of splits and merge attempts. Note also what the function returns: either a different VMA, if the range was absorbed into a neighbour, or the original one, if it had to be split. Callers must use the returned pointer and not the one they passed in — a mistake that the older, per-caller code made easy to hide.

A subsequent mprotect() that restores the original permissions would reverse this: the three pieces become merge-compatible again, and the kernel folds them back into a single VMA. This constant churn between split and merged states is why the kernel enforces an upper bound on VMA count via sysctl_max_map_count. The default is not a round number by accident: it is USHRT_MAX minus MAPCOUNT_ELF_CORE_MARGIN, or 65,530, leaving five spare mappings so that a process at the limit can still be core-dumped. It is tunable through /proc/sys/vm/max_map_count, and workloads that map many small files — JVMs, some databases, and address sanitizers — routinely raise it. Without any limit, pathological workloads could create millions of VMAs and exhaust kernel memory or make lookups unacceptably slow.

Mapping changes rarely line up perfectly with existing VMA boundaries. If a program unmaps the middle of a region, Linux may need to split one VMA into two survivors. If it changes protections on a subrange, it may need three pieces: unchanged left, modified middle, unchanged right. Conversely, if a new mapping or protection change produces adjacent ranges with identical attributes, Linux tries to merge them immediately to keep map_count low.

The split path is visible in mm/vma.c:

/* mm/vma.c — split_vma() */
static int split_vma(struct vma_iterator *vmi, struct vm_area_struct *vma,
		     unsigned long addr, int new_below)
{
	if (vma->vm_mm->map_count >= sysctl_max_map_count)
		return -ENOMEM;

	return __split_vma(vmi, vma, addr, new_below);
}

The visible wrapper is small because the real work is intricate: duplicate the VMA, adjust boundaries and page offsets, preserve or rewire anon_vma state, update file mapping interval trees, store the new topology into the maple tree, and keep debugging validation happy. The key idea for the reader is simple: splitting is expensive because VMAs are rich objects, not just endpoints.

Merging is correspondingly conservative. The helper is_mergeable_vma() rejects merges whenever adjacent VMAs differ in policy, relevant flags, backing file, userfaultfd registration, or anonymous name. Separate helpers then validate file-offset continuity and anon_vma compatibility.

Readers coming from older kernels may expect a helper literally named vma_merge(). In Linux 7.0.12 the central insertion-time helper in mm/vma.c is vma_merge_new_range(), reflecting the fact that the code now reasons about a proposed range plus iterator state instead of only about a binary-tree neighborhood. Conceptually the job is the same: when a new interval is compatible with adjacent VMAs, fold it into them immediately.

There are in fact two merge entry points, and the split reflects a real difference. vma_merge_new_range() handles a range that does not exist yet — the mmap() and brk() case, where there is nothing between the neighbours but a hole. vma_merge_existing_range() handles a range that is already covered by a VMA whose attributes are about to change, which is the vma_modify() case above; it has to consider that the modified range may sit at the start, at the end, or in the middle of its VMA, and that absorbing it into a neighbour may shrink or delete the VMA it came from. The 2.6-era vma_merge() tried to be both functions at once, which is much of why it was notoriously hard to follow.

/* mm/vma.c — selected merge predicates */
static inline bool is_mergeable_vma(struct vma_merge_struct *vmg, bool merge_next)
{
	struct vm_area_struct *vma = merge_next ? vmg->next : vmg->prev;

	if (!mpol_equal(vmg->policy, vma_policy(vma)))
		return false;
	if ((vma->vm_flags ^ vmg->vm_flags) & ~VM_IGNORE_MERGE)
		return false;
	if (vma->vm_file != vmg->file)
		return false;
	if (!is_mergeable_vm_userfaultfd_ctx(vma, vmg->uffd_ctx))
		return false;
	if (!anon_vma_name_eq(anon_vma_name(vma), vmg->anon_name))
		return false;
	return true;
}

static bool can_vma_merge_before(struct vma_merge_struct *vmg)
{
	pgoff_t pglen = PHYS_PFN(vmg->end - vmg->start);

	if (is_mergeable_vma(vmg, true) &&
	    is_mergeable_anon_vma(vmg, true)) {
		if (vmg->next->vm_pgoff == vmg->pgoff + pglen)
			return true;
	}
	return false;
}

In prose, two ranges can merge only when they describe the same kind of mapping at both the semantic and file-offset levels. That generally means:

  • same effective vm_flags after ignoring a small set of merge-irrelevant bits;
  • same backing file, or both anonymous;
  • compatible page offsets so the combined file view remains contiguous;
  • compatible anon_vma state;
  • same NUMA policy;
  • same userfaultfd context;
  • same anonymous VMA name when naming is enabled.

One term in that excerpt is doing quiet work: VM_IGNORE_MERGE. The flag comparison is not a plain equality test but an exclusive-or masked by the flags the kernel is willing to overlook, and there are exactly two of them:
