/* include/linux/mm.h */
#define VM_STICKY (VM_SOFTDIRTY | VM_MAYBE_GUARD)
#define VM_IGNORE_MERGE VM_STICKY

Both are flags that record something that has happened to a region rather than something the user asked for: VM_SOFTDIRTY says the region has been written since the last soft-dirty reset, and VM_MAYBE_GUARD says the region’s page tables may hold guard-region markers installed by madvise(MADV_GUARD_INSTALL). Refusing to merge on either would let a debugging or hardening feature silently fragment a process’s address space. So the merge ignores them when comparing and then sets all of them on the result — the union of both sides, never the intersection. Being conservative in this direction is safe: claiming that a merged region may have been dirtied, or may contain guard markers, costs a little extra work later, whereas claiming it may not would discard information the kernel cannot reconstruct.

This has a visible consequence for callers. vma_modify_flags() writes the resulting flags back through its vm_flags pointer argument after a successful merge, precisely because the sticky bits it gets back may not be the ones it asked for. Code that assumed its requested flag set survived the call unchanged would be wrong.

This list is much longer than it used to be because the kernel's notion of “mapping identity” has grown. That is exactly why the maple tree helps: once the indexing layer became more robust and range-oriented, Linux could evolve the VMA payload without also dragging along an aging topology structure that was hard to reason about under concurrency.

The mmap() System Call

The most important constructor of address-space topology is mmap(). ELF loaders use it for executable segments and shared libraries; allocators use it for large anonymous arenas; JIT runtimes use it to reserve, protect, and recycle code buffers; databases use it for file-backed I/O windows; userfaultfd often starts with it; and tools such as sanitizers and language runtimes depend on precise placement flags. If Chapter 8's alloc_pages() is the canonical physical allocator, mmap() is the canonical virtual-region allocator.

The user-space signature is familiar from POSIX:

void *mmap(void *addr, size_t length, int prot, int flags, int fd, off_t offset);

But the kernel's internal work is considerably more involved than "put a VMA here." A single mmap() call must:

  1. Validate and normalize — page-align the length, sanitize the flags combination, convert POSIX prot/flags into internal vm_flags, and check security modules and capability requirements.
  2. Find an address — if the caller did not specify MAP_FIXED, search the address space for a suitably sized unmapped gap, respecting ASLR randomization, stack-gap guards, and architecture-specific VA constraints.
  3. Handle conflicts — with MAP_FIXED, unmap any existing mappings that overlap the target range; with MAP_FIXED_NOREPLACE, fail if anything overlaps.
  4. Create the VMA — allocate a vm_area_struct, set its fields, call the file's mmap handler if file-backed, insert it into the maple tree, and attempt to merge with adjacent compatible VMAs.
  5. Optionally populate — if MAP_POPULATE or MAP_LOCKED is set, prefault pages into the new mapping so they are resident immediately.

The entry path in Linux 7.0.12 runs from SYSCALL_DEFINE6(mmap_pgoff) in mm/mmap.c through ksys_mmap_pgoff(), then into vm_mmap_pgoff() and finally do_mmap(). The latter assumes the caller has already arranged locking and security context appropriate to a user-space mapping operation. It then validates parameters, derives VMA flags, obtains an unmapped address if needed, and calls mmap_region() to perform the actual insertion.

/* mm/mmap.c — simplified mmap syscall flow */
SYSCALL_DEFINE6(mmap_pgoff, unsigned long, addr, unsigned long, len,
		unsigned long, prot, unsigned long, flags,
		unsigned long, fd, unsigned long, pgoff)
{
	return ksys_mmap_pgoff(addr, len, prot, flags, fd, pgoff);
}

unsigned long do_mmap(struct file *file, unsigned long addr,
		unsigned long len, unsigned long prot,
		unsigned long flags, vm_flags_t vm_flags,
		unsigned long pgoff, unsigned long *populate,
		struct list_head *uf)
{
	...
	if (flags & MAP_FIXED_NOREPLACE)
		flags |= MAP_FIXED;
	...
	len = PAGE_ALIGN(len);
	...
	vm_flags |= calc_vm_prot_bits(prot, pkey) |
			calc_vm_flag_bits(file, flags) |
			mm->def_flags | VM_MAYREAD | VM_MAYWRITE | VM_MAYEXEC;
	addr = __get_unmapped_area(file, addr, len, pgoff, flags, vm_flags);
	...
	if (flags & MAP_FIXED_NOREPLACE) {
		if (find_vma_intersection(mm, addr, addr + len))
			return -EEXIST;
	}
	...
	addr = mmap_region(file, addr, len, vm_flags, pgoff, uf);
	...
	return addr;
}

Two things are worth noting about that skeleton before the details. The first is what is not in it: do_mmap() performs no locking and no LSM file check of its own. Both happen one level up, in vm_mmap_pgoff() in mm/util.c, which calls security_mmap_file() and fsnotify_mmap_perm(), takes mmap_write_lock_killable(), invokes do_mmap(), drops the lock, drains any pending userfaultfd unmap events, and only then calls mm_populate() if do_mmap() asked for population. Population deliberately happens outside the write lock, because faulting in a large mapping can take a long time. The second is that do_mmap() begins with mmap_assert_write_locked(mm), which documents that contract as an assertion rather than a comment.

Within do_mmap() the checks run in a fixed and quite deliberate order, because each one narrows what the next has to consider:

  1. Personality and hint fixups. A process with the READ_IMPLIES_EXEC personality bit gets PROT_EXEC added to any readable mapping, unless the file lives on a noexec mount. MAP_FIXED_NOREPLACE is turned into MAP_FIXED so that the address search treats the request as mandatory; the "don't replace" half of the promise is enforced separately a few lines later. A non-fixed hint is passed through round_hint_to_min(), which raises any non-NULL hint below mmap_min_addr up to that floor rather than rejecting it — a low hint is only advisory, so silently correcting it is friendlier than failing. A NULL hint is left alone and means "kernel's choice".
  2. Arithmetic safety. The length is rounded with PAGE_ALIGN(); a length that rounds to zero is rejected with -ENOMEM, which is how a request of, say, -1 bytes is caught. The page offset is checked for overflow against the length and rejected with -EOVERFLOW.
  3. Map-count limit. mm->map_count > sysctl_max_map_count fails with -ENOMEM. This is the same limit discussed earlier under VMA splitting, and it is checked here before any work is done rather than after the split that would exceed it.
  4. Protection to flags. A mapping requested as exactly PROT_EXEC tries to acquire an execute-only protection key via execute_only_pkey(), falling back to key 0 if none is available. Then vm_flags is assembled from calc_vm_prot_bits(prot, pkey), calc_vm_flag_bits(file, flags), the inherited mm->def_flags, and the three VM_MAY* ceiling bits.
  5. Address selection. __get_unmapped_area() either validates the caller's address or searches for a gap, as described later in this chapter.
  6. The MAP_FIXED_NOREPLACE promise. Now that a concrete address exists, find_vma_intersection() checks the range and returns -EEXIST if anything is already there. Doing this after address selection rather than before is what makes the flag race-free: the check and the subsequent insertion both happen under the same write lock.
  7. Locking limits. MAP_LOCKED requires can_do_mlock(), and mlock_future_ok() checks the request against RLIMIT_MEMLOCK, returning -EPERM and -EAGAIN respectively.
  8. Per-object validation. The file-backed and anonymous cases then diverge into two switch statements on flags & MAP_TYPE, described below.
  9. Reservation policy. MAP_NORESERVE sets VM_NORESERVE, but only if the overcommit policy is not OVERCOMMIT_NEVER — under strict overcommit the kernel refuses to let user space opt out of accounting. hugetlb files are the exception and always honour the flag.
  10. Population decision. After mmap_region() succeeds, *populate is set to the length if the mapping is VM_LOCKED, or if MAP_POPULATE was given without MAP_NONBLOCK. That out-parameter is what causes the caller to run mm_populate() later, outside the lock.

The file-backed switch is where most of the sharp edges live. It starts by computing a flags_mask of LEGACY_MAP_MASK, extended with MAP_SYNC only if the file's f_op->fop_flags contains FOP_MMAP_SYNC. Plain MAP_SHARED then silently masks the caller's flags down to the legacy set, while MAP_SHARED_VALIDATE falls through to a check that rejects anything outside the mask with -EOPNOTSUPP. That asymmetry is the entire reason MAP_SHARED_VALIDATE exists: it is MAP_SHARED with the silent-ignore behaviour removed, so that a program asking for MAP_SYNC learns whether it actually got it.

The rest of the file case checks that a writable shared mapping has FMODE_WRITE (-EACCES), refuses to map a live swapfile (-ETXTBSY), refuses to write to an append-only inode, downgrades VM_MAYWRITE and VM_SHARED when the file was opened read-only, requires FMODE_READ for every mapping including private ones, strips VM_MAYEXEC (or fails with -EPERM) on a noexec path, rejects growable file mappings, and finally calls memfd_check_seals_mmap() to enforce F_SEAL_WRITE and F_SEAL_FUTURE_WRITE. Note that sealing may not merely reject the mapping — it can also clear VM_MAYWRITE so that a later mprotect() cannot re-acquire write access.

One check in that list is new in spirit even though it looks routine: can_mmap_file() returns -ENODEV if the file's operations table provides neither mmap nor mmap_prepare, and WARN_ON_ONCE()s if it provides both. That is the gate for the hook rework described in a moment.

The anonymous switch is shorter. MAP_SHARED ignores the offset entirely and sets VM_SHARED | VM_MAYSHARE; MAP_PRIVATE sets vm_pgoff = addr >> PAGE_SHIFT so that the anonymous reverse-mapping code has a consistent interval notion. Between them sits MAP_DROPPABLE, added for memory whose contents the kernel is allowed to discard rather than swap. It is rejected outright on architectures where VM_DROPPABLE is VM_NONE, is incompatible with MAP_LOCKED, MAP_HUGETLB, and the growable flags, and implies VM_NORESERVE | VM_WIPEONFORK | VM_DONTDUMP — reserving, inheriting, or dumping pages that may vanish at any moment would all be meaningless.

The flags argument to mmap() controls placement, sharing semantics, and special behaviors. The kernel translates these user-visible flags into internal vm_flags via calc_vm_flag_bits() inside do_mmap(). Some flags like MAP_SHARED and MAP_ANONYMOUS are fundamental to every mapping; others like MAP_FIXED_NOREPLACE and MAP_SYNC are newer additions that reflect lessons learned from real-world bugs and persistent-memory use cases. The following table lists the most important flags in the 7.0 kernel:

Table 9-7. Important MAP_* Flags for mmap()
Flag Meaning Notes in Linux 7.0.12
MAP_SHAREDUpdates are shared with the underlying object.Pairs naturally with file mappings; validated against file mode and seals.
MAP_PRIVATEPrivate mapping with copy-on-write semantics.Common for executable images and anonymous mappings. Exactly one of MAP_SHARED, MAP_SHARED_VALIDATE, and MAP_PRIVATE must be given; the switch on flags & MAP_TYPE rejects anything else with -EINVAL.
MAP_SHARED_VALIDATELike MAP_SHARED, but unknown or unsupported flags are an error.Plain MAP_SHARED masks the request down to LEGACY_MAP_MASK and ignores the rest; this variant returns -EOPNOTSUPP instead. Required to use MAP_SYNC meaningfully.
MAP_ANONYMOUSNo backing file.Anonymous mappings derive their vm_pgoff from the virtual address.
MAP_FIXEDUse exactly the requested address, replacing existing mappings if needed.Dangerous because it can silently destroy overlapping VMAs.
MAP_FIXED_NOREPLACEUse exactly the requested address, but fail if anything overlaps.do_mmap() forces MAP_FIXED handling and then checks for intersections.
MAP_POPULATEPrefault the mapping.Sets the *populate out-parameter, so mm_populate() runs in vm_mmap_pgoff() after the write lock is dropped.
MAP_NONBLOCKHistorically "do not block on prefault".Now only meaningful as a modifier that suppresses MAP_POPULATE; the two together request no population at all.
MAP_LOCKEDLock pages into memory.Requires privilege or RLIMIT compliance; sets VMA locking state.
MAP_HUGETLBCreate a hugetlb mapping.Handled specially in ksys_mmap_pgoff().
MAP_GROWSDOWNStack-like growth semantics.Translated into VM_GROWSDOWN.
MAP_DENYWRITE, MAP_EXECUTABLEHistoric ETXTBSY-related flags.Completely ignored, as include/linux/mman.h states outright, but retained in LEGACY_MAP_MASK so old binaries that pass them still work.
MAP_NORESERVEAvoid strict reservation accounting where permitted.Interacts with overcommit policy and hugetlb.
MAP_STACKMapping is intended as a stack.Helps userspace request stack-appropriate placement.
MAP_SYNCSynchronous page-fault semantics for supported file mappings.Accepted only if f_op->fop_flags contains FOP_MMAP_SYNC, and only under MAP_SHARED_VALIDATE. Translated to VM_SYNC by calc_vm_flag_bits(). Used by DAX filesystems on persistent memory.
MAP_DROPPABLEPages may be discarded by the kernel under pressure instead of swapped.Sets VM_DROPPABLE, and implies VM_NORESERVE, VM_WIPEONFORK, and VM_DONTDUMP. Rejected with -ENOTSUPP where VM_DROPPABLE is VM_NONE, and incompatible with MAP_LOCKED, MAP_HUGETLB, and the growable flags.
MAP_UNINITIALIZEDSkip zeroing of anonymous pages.Honoured only on no-MMU builds with CONFIG_MMAP_ALLOW_UNINITIALIZED. Never active on x86_64 or ARM64 — zeroing anonymous pages is a security guarantee there.
MAP_32BIT, MAP_ABOVE4Gx86_64-only placement constraints.Handled in arch/x86/kernel/sys_x86_64.c. MAP_32BIT restricts the search to [0x40000000, 0x80000000) — 1 GiB of playground in the low 2 GiB, for small-code-model objects — and MAP_ABOVE4G raises the search floor to SZ_4G. Both are ignored for 32-bit syscalls. No ARM64 equivalent.

do_mmap() has three especially modern behaviors worth calling out. First, it treats MAP_FIXED_NOREPLACE as a distinct safe programming interface rather than expecting user space to probe manually and race. Second, it centralizes the transformation from POSIX-style prot/flags pairs to internal vm_flags. Third, it integrates with newer policy features such as execute-only pkeys, file sealing, MAP_DROPPABLE, and future-lock defaults.

The next stage, mmap_region() in mm/vma.c, is the insertion point where an already validated address and flag set become real address-space topology. Its job is deliberately narrow: ensure architecture and write-exec policy accept the flags, account for writable shared file mappings, and then delegate to the lower-level region constructor. This separation keeps the syscall-facing argument checks in do_mmap() distinct from the topology mutation logic in mm/vma.c.

/* mm/vma.c — front end of mmap_region() */
unsigned long mmap_region(struct file *file, unsigned long addr,
		  unsigned long len, vm_flags_t vm_flags,
		  unsigned long pgoff, struct list_head *uf)
{
	unsigned long ret;
	bool writable_file_mapping = false;

	mmap_assert_write_locked(current->mm);

	if (map_deny_write_exec(vm_flags, vm_flags))
		return -EACCES;
	if (!arch_validate_flags(vm_flags))
		return -EINVAL;
	if (file && is_shared_maywrite_vm_flags(vm_flags)) {
		int error = mapping_map_writable(file->f_mapping);
		if (error)
			return error;
		writable_file_mapping = true;
	}

	ret = __mmap_region(file, addr, len, vm_flags, pgoff, uf);
	...
	return ret;
}

The first of those three checks deserves a name. map_deny_write_exec(), in include/linux/mman.h, implements Memory-Deny-Write-Execute (MDWE), a per-process opt-in hardening policy enabled with prctl(PR_SET_MDWE, PR_MDWE_REFUSE_EXEC_GAIN, ...) and recorded as the MMF_HAS_MDWE bit in mm->flags. When it is set, the kernel refuses two things: creating a mapping that is writable and executable at the same time, and making a previously non-executable mapping executable. The function takes old and new flag sets, which is why mmap_region() passes vm_flags twice — a brand-new mapping has no previous state, so the "gaining execute permission" test collapses into the "was not executable" test. The same helper is called from mprotect(), where the two arguments genuinely differ. A companion flag, PR_MDWE_NO_INHERIT, causes the policy to be dropped rather than inherited whenever a fresh mm_struct is derived from the current one — mmf_init_legacy_flags() in include/linux/mm_types.h strips both bits, and that helper runs from mm_init(), so it covers fork() and execve() alike.

MDWE is the kernel-enforced form of the W^X discipline that JIT compilers already follow voluntarily: allocate a region writable, emit code into it, then flip it to executable with mprotect(). Under MDWE that last step fails, so JITs must instead map the same memory twice — once writable, once executable — typically through a memfd. That is a real compatibility cost, which is why the policy is opt-in per process rather than a global default.

Inside __mmap_region()

Where mmap_region() is a thin policy gate, __mmap_region() in mm/vma.c is the function that actually changes the address space. It is worth reading as a sequence of phases, because each phase is a separate static function and the error labels at the bottom unwind them in reverse.

All the state that the phases share lives in a struct mmap_state, built by the MMAP_STATE() macro from the arguments, plus a struct vm_area_desc that is only populated if the file provides an mmap_prepare hook. Bundling the state this way is what allows the phases to be separate functions without a long argument list, and it is also what makes the operation restartable: everything that must be undone on failure is reachable from one place.

  1. __mmap_setup() — reserve and clear the ground. It locates the first VMA overlapping the target range and, if there is one, initialises a detached maple tree and calls vms_gather_munmap_vmas() to collect everything that must be removed. This is how MAP_FIXED over an existing mapping works: the old VMAs are gathered but not yet freed, so the operation can still be aborted. It then checks may_expand_vm(), which enforces RLIMIT_AS against mm->total_vm and, for data mappings, RLIMIT_DATA against mm->data_vm; the latter is advisory unless the ignore_rlimit_data boot option says otherwise. For a private writable mapping it then charges the commit through security_vm_enough_memory_mm() and sets VM_ACCOUNT. Note that the charge is reduced by the pages the pending unmap will release, so replacing a mapping with one of the same size costs nothing. Finally vms_clean_up_area() tears down the PTEs of the doomed VMAs while they are still in the tree, so that reverse mapping cannot race with the later free.
  2. call_mmap_prepare() — ask the driver, if it uses the new hook. Described in the next subsection.
  3. Merge first, allocate second. If there is a previous or next VMA, vma_merge_new_range() is tried before anything is allocated. A successful merge means no new vm_area_struct and no new maple-tree node — which is why a program that mmaps a large arena in many adjacent chunks with identical flags does not accumulate thousands of VMAs.
  4. __mmap_new_vma() — build the VMA if the merge failed. It allocates the descriptor with vm_area_alloc(), sets its range and flags, and preallocates the maple-tree node before doing anything that is hard to undo. It then dispatches on the kind of mapping: file-backed goes to __mmap_new_file_vma(), which takes a reference on the file and calls the driver's legacy mmap hook (and on failure unmaps whatever the driver may have partially established); shared anonymous goes to shmem_zero_setup(), which quietly gives the mapping a tmpfs file; private anonymous just gets vma_set_anonymous(). Only once all of that has succeeded does it take the VMA write lock, store the VMA into the maple tree, increment map_count, and call vma_link_file() to insert it into the file's i_mmap interval tree.
  5. __mmap_complete() — commit and finish. This is where vms_complete_munmap_vmas() finally frees the VMAs that phase 1 gathered; up to this point the operation could still have been abandoned. It then updates mm->total_vm and the per-class counters through vm_stat_account(), adds to mm->locked_vm for VM_LOCKED mappings that actually support mlock, notifies perf_event_mmap() and uprobe_mmap(), sets VM_SOFTDIRTY where the page tables support it, and calls vma_set_page_prot() so the cached vm_page_prot matches the final flags.

The unwinding is equally instructive. The unacct_error label gives back the commit charge taken in phase 1; abort_munmap additionally calls vms_abort_munmap_vmas(), which reattaches the VMAs that phase 1 gathered for removal. This is the payoff of gathering rather than unmapping immediately: a failure late in the operation leaves the address space exactly as it was, so a failed MAP_FIXED does not destroy the mapping it was going to replace.

The one thing this ordering cannot undo is the PTE teardown done by vms_clean_up_area(). That is safe because clearing a PTE is not observable: a subsequent access simply faults and the page is re-established from the still-attached VMA. The distinction between "state that can be reconstructed by faulting" and "state that must be preserved" runs through the whole of Linux MM.

The mmap_prepare Hook

For most of Linux's history, a driver or filesystem participated in mmap() through a single callback:

int (*mmap)(struct file *, struct vm_area_struct *);

The hook received a fully constructed VMA that was already, at least conceptually, part of the address space, and was free to modify it. That flexibility was the problem. A driver could change vm_flags after the merge decision had been made, install a vm_ops table that invalidated assumptions the caller had already acted on, or fail after having mutated state that the caller then had to guess how to undo. Auditing the several hundred implementations in the tree for what they actually touched was, in practice, impossible.

Linux 7.0 introduces a replacement that inverts the relationship. Instead of handing the driver a VMA to mutate, the kernel hands it a description of the VMA it intends to create, and lets the driver adjust the description:

/* include/linux/fs.h — the new hook, alongside the old one */
struct file_operations {
	...
	int (*mmap)(struct file *, struct vm_area_struct *);
	int (*mmap_prepare)(struct vm_area_desc *);
	...
};

/* include/linux/mm_types.h */
struct vm_area_desc {
	/* Immutable state. */
	const struct mm_struct *const mm;
	struct file *const file; /* May vary from vm_file in stacked callers. */
	unsigned long start;
	unsigned long end;

	/* Mutable fields. Populated with initial state. */
	pgoff_t pgoff;
	struct file *vm_file;
	vma_flags_t vma_flags;
	pgprot_t page_prot;

	/* Write-only fields. */
	const struct vm_operations_struct *vm_ops;
	void *private_data;

	/* Take further action? */
	struct mmap_action action;
};

The comments in that structure are the specification. mm and file are const pointers to const data, so the compiler enforces that a hook cannot reach into the address space it is being consulted about. start and end are informational. Only pgoff, vm_file, vma_flags, and page_prot may be adjusted, and only vm_ops and private_data may be set. Everything a driver used to do by writing into a live VMA now has a declared slot, and anything not on the list is simply unreachable.

The two hooks are mutually exclusive, enforced at the point of use rather than by convention:

/* include/linux/fs.h */
static inline bool can_mmap_file(struct file *file)
{
	bool has_mmap = file->f_op->mmap;
	bool has_mmap_prepare = file->f_op->mmap_prepare;

	/* Hooks are mutually exclusive. */
	if (WARN_ON_ONCE(has_mmap && has_mmap_prepare))
		return false;
	if (!has_mmap && !has_mmap_prepare)
		return false;

	return true;
}

static inline int vfs_mmap(struct file *file, struct vm_area_struct *vma)
{
	if (file->f_op->mmap_prepare)
		return compat_vma_mmap(file, vma);

	return file->f_op->mmap(file, vma);
}

vfs_mmap() is the bridge that makes incremental conversion possible. Callers that still have only a VMA in hand — stacking filesystems, and code paths that map a file outside the normal mmap() flow — go through compat_vma_mmap() in mm/util.c, which synthesises a vm_area_desc from the VMA, calls the new hook, runs any requested action, and copies the permitted results back with set_vma_from_desc(). If the action fails it unmaps the range itself, since in this path the caller has no gathered-and-restorable state to fall back on. Converted and unconverted drivers therefore coexist, and no flag day is required.

Some drivers need to do more than describe the mapping; a device driver typically wants to establish PFN mappings immediately. Rather than let the hook do that itself — which would reintroduce the mutation problem — the description carries a request for the kernel to perform the action on the driver's behalf:

/* include/linux/mm_types.h */
enum mmap_action_type {
	MMAP_NOTHING,		/* Mapping is complete, no further action. */
	MMAP_REMAP_PFN,		/* Remap PFN range. */
	MMAP_IO_REMAP_PFN,	/* I/O remap PFN range. */
};

struct mmap_action {
	union {
		struct {
			unsigned long start;
			unsigned long start_pfn;
			unsigned long size;
			pgprot_t pgprot;
		} remap;
	};
	enum mmap_action_type type;

	int (*success_hook)(const struct vm_area_struct *vma);
	int (*error_hook)(int err);
	bool hide_from_rmap_until_complete :1;
};

A hook that wants remap_pfn_range() fills in action.remap and sets type; the kernel performs the remap at the right point in the sequence, with the right locks held, and reports failure through the driver's error_hook without the driver ever having touched the VMA. The hide_from_rmap_until_complete bit covers the rare case where the VMA must not be visible to reverse mapping until the action has finished.

Back in __mmap_region(), call_mmap_prepare() invokes the hook, runs the requested preparation, and then copies exactly the permitted fields out of the descriptor into the mmap_state. It notices if the hook substituted a different vm_file and records that the original reference must be released. Because all of this happens before the merge attempt, the flags the merge logic sees are the final ones — which is the concrete correctness win. With the old hook the driver ran after the VMA existed, so a driver that changed vm_flags could invalidate a merge decision that had already been made.

The same reasoning appears in can_set_ksm_flags_early(), which decides whether the KSM mergeable flag can be applied before the driver runs. It answers yes for anonymous mappings, for shmem, and — significantly — for any file whose operations provide mmap_prepare, precisely because such a driver cannot change the flags afterwards. New infrastructure earning better treatment than the compatibility path is a recurring pattern in Linux MM, and it is how a rework of this size eventually finishes.

Anonymous versus file-backed mappings

The single most important conceptual divide in Linux virtual memory is between anonymous and file-backed mappings. Almost every decision the kernel makes about a page — how to fault it in, where to find its data, how to reclaim it under memory pressure, how to track who maps it, and where to write it when it is dirty — depends on which side of this divide the page falls.

The conceptual split between anonymous and file-backed mappings is visible all the way down in the code:

  • for file-backed mappings, do_mmap() checks open modes, append-only and noexec constraints, support for special flags such as MAP_SYNC, and file seals; the VMA carries vm_file and a file-relative vm_pgoff;
  • for anonymous mappings, vm_file is NULL, the kernel may allocate or attach anonymous reverse-mapping state later, and the initial vm_pgoff is derived from the virtual address so that merge/split logic still has a consistent interval notion.

Anonymous mappings dominate heaps, thread stacks, JIT reservations, guard pages, and scratch arenas. File-backed mappings dominate executable text, shared libraries, memory-mapped files, and DAX-style persistent-memory use cases. But private executable segments blur the line: they start file-backed and become partly anonymous after copy-on-write faults. That is why vm_area_struct needs both vm_file and anon_vma; a MAP_PRIVATE file mapping may participate in both worlds over time.

MAP_SHARED versus MAP_PRIVATE

Orthogonal to the anonymous/file-backed distinction is the sharing mode. Every mapping is either MAP_SHARED or MAP_PRIVATE (passing neither is an error). This choice determines how write operations propagate: shared writes are visible to all processes mapping the same object and, for files, eventually reach the backing store; private writes create per-process copies that are invisible to other mappers and never written back to the file. The two modes interact with the anonymous/file-backed axis to produce four combinations, each with different kernel behavior:

Table 9-8. Mapping Mode Matrix
MAP_SHARED MAP_PRIVATE
File-backed Writes go to page cache and eventually to disk. All mappers see updates. Requires file write permission. Reads come from page cache; writes trigger CoW into anonymous pages. The file is never modified.
Anonymous Shared anonymous memory (e.g., shmem/tmpfs-backed). Used by IPC, memfd_create(), and shared anonymous mmap(). The most common case: heap, stack, JIT arenas. Pages are zero-filled on first fault. Reclaimed to swap if needed.

The visible user-level rule is simple: shared mappings propagate writes to the underlying object and to peer mappers; private mappings create per-process modifications through copy-on-write. The kernel rule is more precise. A shared writable file mapping must be validated against file mode, append restrictions, sealing, and sometimes filesystem-specific writeback requirements. A private mapping, by contrast, can often start with read-only page-cache-backed PTEs and rely on later write faults to instantiate anonymous copies.

That means MAP_PRIVATE is not “copied at mmap() time.” It is merely marked as potentially copy-on-write. The expensive duplication happens only if and when a process writes a page that still aliases shared backing storage. This deferred behavior is exactly why fork() and private executable images are practical.

Modern Linux also treats “shared” more carefully than Linux 2.6 did. For example, MAP_SHARED_VALIDATE exists so user space can request non-legacy flags such as MAP_SYNC and know the kernel will reject unsupported combinations rather than silently ignore them. The MM subsystem is no longer only preserving POSIX semantics; it is also preserving correctness for advanced persistent-memory and fault-handling use cases.

Address Space Layout and ASLR

When a process calls mmap() without MAP_FIXED, the kernel must choose where in the vast virtual address space to place the new mapping. This decision is not arbitrary — it is governed by a combination of layout policy (top-down vs. bottom-up allocation), alignment constraints (huge pages, cache coloring), stack-gap guards (to prevent stack-heap collisions), and Address Space Layout Randomization (ASLR), which deliberately perturbs the base addresses of the stack, mmap region, heap, and executable image to make exploitation harder.

ASLR is one of the kernel's most widely deployed security hardening features. By randomizing the starting positions of key memory regions, it forces an attacker who has found a memory corruption bug to also defeat address uncertainty before they can reliably redirect control flow. While not a complete defense — information leaks, brute-force, and side channels can reduce or eliminate the entropy — ASLR raises the practical cost of exploitation enough that disabling it is considered a serious misconfiguration on production systems.

Where should a new anonymous mapping go when the caller does not specify MAP_FIXED? That question is answered by a mixture of generic code, architecture layout policy, stack-limit heuristics, and ASLR randomization. On modern 64-bit Linux systems the default is usually a top-down layout: the mmap area grows downward from near the top of the user portion of the address space, leaving the traditional brk heap below.

On x86_64, arch/x86/mm/mmap.c implements arch_pick_mmap_layout(). The function decides whether the process gets legacy bottom-up placement or the modern top-down layout, sets or clears MMF_TOPDOWN, and computes randomized base addresses for 64-bit and compat syscalls. ARM64 often uses the generic helper path in mm/util.c, but the policy idea is the same: select a layout model, then perturb the result with architecture-dependent entropy.

/* arch/x86/mm/mmap.c — x86_64 layout selection */
void arch_pick_mmap_layout(struct mm_struct *mm, const struct rlimit *rlim_stack)
{
	if (mmap_is_legacy())
		mm_flags_clear(MMF_TOPDOWN, mm);
	else
		mm_flags_set(MMF_TOPDOWN, mm);

	arch_pick_mmap_base(&mm->mmap_base, &mm->mmap_legacy_base,
			arch_rnd(mmap64_rnd_bits), task_size_64bit(0),
			rlim_stack);
	...
}

/* mm/util.c — generic brk randomization helper */
unsigned long __weak arch_randomize_brk(struct mm_struct *mm)
{
	if (!IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_64BIT) || is_compat_task())
		return randomize_page(mm->brk, SZ_32M);

	return randomize_page(mm->brk, SZ_1G);
}

Several different values are randomized independently or semi-independently:

  • stack placement: randomized by architecture-specific stack masks such as STACK_RND_MASK;
  • mmap base: randomized by mmap_rnd_bits or mmap_rnd_compat_bits;
  • brk base: randomized when randomize_va_space == 2 and compatibility mode does not suppress it;
  • PIE executable placement: randomized because the executable itself is mapped like a relocatable object.

The sysctl /proc/sys/kernel/randomize_va_space selects the coarse policy level, documented in Documentation/admin-guide/sysctl/kernel.rst:

  • 0: no process-address-space randomization;
  • 1: randomize mmap base, stack, VDSO, and PIE load addresses;
  • 2: additionally randomize the heap (brk).

The default in mm/memory.c is 2 unless CONFIG_COMPAT_BRK forces compatibility-oriented behavior, in which case the default is 1. That default matters because very old binaries once assumed the heap began immediately after the static data segment. Modern toolchains and runtimes should assume full randomization.

Table 9-9. ASLR Entropy Sources on x86_64 and ARM64
Architecture mmap randomization bits Stack randomization mask brk randomization range
x86_64 ARCH_MMAP_RND_BITS_MIN = 28, MAX = 32 in arch/x86/Kconfig. __STACK_RND_MASK(...)=0x3fffff on 64-bit, roughly 1 GiB of virtual slack. arch_randomize_brk() randomizes within SZ_1G for 64-bit tasks.
ARM64 (4 KiB pages, common 48-bit VA build) ARCH_MMAP_RND_BITS_MIN = 18, MAX = 33 in arch/arm64/Kconfig. STACK_RND_MASK = 0x3ffff >> (PAGE_SHIFT - 12), about 1 GiB with 4 KiB pages. Usually uses the generic helper path; 64-bit generic code also allows up to SZ_1G.

Two caveats are worth teaching explicitly:

  1. “Entropy bits” are maxima or configurable bounds, not guarantees that every mapping enjoys the full theoretical spread after alignment, stack-gap constraints, reserved address windows, and PIE loader rules are accounted for.
  2. ASLR is layout hardening, not a permission system. It raises the cost of exploitation but does not replace W^X, page-table protections, or correct program logic.

From a kernel-implementation perspective, ASLR is interesting because it is not bolted on after the fact. The randomization inputs become fields in mm_struct such as mmap_base and indirectly affect the very first unmapped-area search. That means topology and security policy are intertwined from the first mapping onward.

ARM64 and x86_64 differ in the geometry beneath the abstraction. x86_64 commonly uses a 48-bit canonical user range unless five-level paging is active; ARM64 may be built for several VA widths and page sizes. Yet the VMA layer above them barely changes. That separation is one of the strengths of Linux MM design: architecture-specific code picks the search window and entropy, then the generic VMA machinery handles insertion, merging, splitting, and accounting.

Finding an Unmapped Gap

ASLR decides where the search starts. The search itself is __get_unmapped_area() in mm/mmap.c, and it is worth following because a surprising amount of policy is layered into what looks like a simple "find a hole" problem.

The first job is to pick a search function. A file whose operations table provides get_unmapped_area gets to choose its own placement — this is how hugetlbfs enforces huge-page alignment and how some drivers demand particular alignment for their hardware. A shared anonymous mapping borrows shmem_get_unmapped_area(), because mmap_region() will later turn it into a tmpfs mapping anyway and tmpfs may want huge-page alignment. An anonymous mapping with no hint whose length is a multiple of PMD_SIZE is routed through thp_get_unmapped_area_vmflags() so that large allocations land where a transparent huge page can actually be used — an alignment decision made at mmap() time that determines whether khugepaged can ever collapse the region later. Everything else goes to mm_get_unmapped_area_vmflags(), which is the one-line function that consults MMF_TOPDOWN and dispatches to the bottom-up or top-down variant.

Note also that pgoff is forced to zero for anonymous mappings before the search, and that the whole thing is bracketed by arch_mmap_check() at the start and security_mmap_addr() at the end. The latter is the LSM's opportunity to veto a low mapping; it is the hook behind the classic mmap_min_addr protection against NULL-pointer-dereference exploits.

The generic top-down search, generic_get_unmapped_area_topdown(), shows the structure that all of these variants share. ARM64 uses it directly; x86_64 defines HAVE_ARCH_UNMAPPED_AREA and HAVE_ARCH_UNMAPPED_AREA_TOPDOWN in arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_64.h and supplies its own versions in arch/x86/kernel/sys_x86_64.c, which follow the same shape with extra cases bolted on.

/* mm/mmap.c — the top-down search, abridged */
	/* requesting a specific address */
	if (addr) {
		addr = PAGE_ALIGN(addr);
		vma = find_vma_prev(mm, addr, &prev);
		if (mmap_end - len >= addr && addr >= mmap_min_addr &&
				(!vma || addr + len <= vm_start_gap(vma)) &&
				(!prev || addr >= vm_end_gap(prev)))
			return addr;
	}

	info.flags = VM_UNMAPPED_AREA_TOPDOWN;
	info.length = len;
	info.low_limit = PAGE_SIZE;
	info.high_limit = arch_get_mmap_base(addr, mm->mmap_base);
	info.start_gap = stack_guard_placement(vm_flags);
	if (filp && is_file_hugepages(filp))
		info.align_mask = huge_page_mask_align(filp);
	addr = vm_unmapped_area(&info);

	/*
	 * A failed mmap() very likely causes application failure,
	 * so fall back to the bottom-up function here. ...
	 */
	if (offset_in_page(addr)) {
		VM_BUG_ON(addr != -ENOMEM);
		info.flags = 0;
		info.low_limit = TASK_UNMAPPED_BASE;
		info.high_limit = mmap_end;
		addr = vm_unmapped_area(&info);
	}

Three things in that fragment matter. First, a caller-supplied hint is honoured if it happens to fit, which is why repeatedly mapping and unmapping at the same address usually succeeds without any search at all. Second, the ceiling is mm->mmap_base — the randomized value ASLR computed — passed through arch_get_mmap_base(), which is a no-op by default; ARM64 overrides it so that a hint above DEFAULT_MAP_WINDOW opts the mapping into the wider 52-bit window, and similarly overrides arch_get_mmap_end(). This is how ARM64 keeps 52-bit virtual addressing opt-in: a program that never passes a high hint never sees an address it cannot handle. Third, a top-down search that fails falls back to a bottom-up search over the whole address space rather than returning -ENOMEM. That fallback exists because a very large RLIMIT_STACK pushes mmap_base far down, leaving too little room above it; without the retry, raising the stack limit could make unrelated large allocations fail. x86_64 open-codes the same fallback, with a bottomup: label that tail-calls its bottom-up function.

The x86_64 versions add two things the generic code has no notion of. find_start_end() implements the MAP_32BIT window described in Table 9-7, complete with its own randomization, and MAP_ABOVE4G raises the floor to SZ_4G. And for file mappings both x86_64 functions set info.align_mask and info.align_offset from get_align_mask() and get_align_bits(). Those cover hugetlb alignment and, on processors that need it, randomize the low address bits rather than leaving them zero, to avoid instruction-cache aliasing on AMD family 15h. Both are gated on PF_RANDOMIZE, so a process running with ASLR disabled gets neither. This is the alignment machinery the generic search exposes but only uses for hugetlb.

Note the calling convention, flagged in the source as an "ugly calling convention alert": these functions return either an address or a small negative error value cast to unsigned long, distinguished by offset_in_page() — an error is never page-aligned, an address always is.

vm_unmapped_area() then dispatches to unmapped_area() or unmapped_area_topdown() in mm/vma.c. This is where the maple tree earns its place a second time. Both functions are built on mas_empty_area() and mas_empty_area_rev(), wrapped as vma_iter_area_lowest() and vma_iter_area_highest(), which find a hole of a given size within a range in a single descent. The old red-black tree could not do this: it had to maintain an augmented "largest gap in this subtree" field at every node and update it on every insertion and removal. The maple tree stores intervals natively, so the gap is simply the space between two adjacent entries and no augmentation is needed.

What the tree cannot express is the guard gap. A stack VMA must not have anything mapped immediately below it, or a deep recursion could grow the stack straight into an unrelated mapping instead of faulting. Linux reserves stack_guard_gap — 256 pages, 1 MiB with 4 KiB pages, declared in mm/mmap.c — below every VM_GROWSDOWN VMA, and one page below every VM_SHADOW_STACK VMA. The helpers vm_start_gap() and vm_end_gap() return a VMA's effective boundary including its gap, and VM_STARTGAP_FLAGS names the two flags that cause one.

Because the gap is not part of the interval stored in the tree, the search cannot account for it up front. Both functions therefore use a retry loop: ask the tree for a hole, apply the start gap and alignment, check the neighbouring VMA's gapped boundary with vm_start_gap() or vm_end_gap(), and if that boundary intrudes, move the limit past the offending VMA and search again. The loop terminates because each retry strictly shrinks the search range. The initial length is padded by align_mask + start_gap so that the tree does not return a hole that is guaranteed to be too small once alignment is applied.

This is a good example of a general principle in the VMA layer: the data structure answers the geometric question, and a thin loop above it applies the policy the data structure does not know about. Trying to teach the maple tree about guard gaps would make every insertion more expensive in order to speed up a search that happens far less often.

Releasing Address Intervals (munmap)

If mmap() is the constructor of process topology, munmap() is its destructor. The user-space interface is deceptively simple:

int munmap(void *addr, size_t length);

The caller specifies a starting address (which must be page-aligned) and a length (which the kernel rounds up to a page boundary). On success the kernel removes all mappings that fall within [addr, addr+length), making future accesses to those addresses generate a segfault. But behind this simple contract lies a surprisingly deep pipeline of work:

  • VMA splitting — if the range partially overlaps existing VMAs, the kernel must split them at the boundaries, preserving the surviving portions with correct offsets, permissions, reverse mappings, and policy.
  • Page-table teardown — every PTE, PMD, and higher-level entry within the range must be cleared, and the corresponding physical pages must be released or dirtied for writeback.
  • TLB invalidation — on SMP systems, stale TLB entries on remote CPUs must be shot down via inter-processor interrupts before the physical pages can be reused.
  • Reverse-mapping cleanup — file-backed pages must be unlinked from the file's i_mmap interval tree; anonymous pages must update their anon_vma chains.
  • Notification — userfaultfd observers, mmu notifiers (used by KVM and GPU drivers), and memory cgroup accounting must all be informed.
  • Metadata destruction — the VMA structures themselves are freed only after all the above work is complete and no concurrent reader can still reference them.

The operation also interacts with other syscalls in non-obvious ways. A MAP_FIXED mmap implicitly munmaps the target range before creating the new mapping. Process exit calls exit_mmap(), which is essentially a full-address-space munmap. And execve() tears down the old address space before building the new one. Understanding munmap() therefore means understanding the common teardown machinery that all these paths share.

Implementing this correctly requires the kernel to respect VMA boundaries, page-table teardown ordering, file and anon reverse mappings, userfaultfd notifications, lock ordering, and architecture TLB implications. The top-level system call in mm/mmap.c is intentionally tiny because almost all of the complexity lives underneath.

/* mm/mmap.c and mm/vma.c — the modern munmap path */
SYSCALL_DEFINE2(munmap, unsigned long, addr, size_t, len)
{
	addr = untagged_addr(addr);
	return __vm_munmap(addr, len, true);
}

int do_vmi_munmap(struct vma_iterator *vmi, struct mm_struct *mm,
		  unsigned long start, size_t len, struct list_head *uf,
		  bool unlock)
{
	unsigned long end;
	struct vm_area_struct *vma;

	...
	end = start + PAGE_ALIGN(len);
	...
	vma = vma_find(vmi, end);
	if (!vma) {
		if (unlock)
			mmap_write_unlock(mm);
		return 0;
	}

	return do_vmi_align_munmap(vmi, vma, mm, start, end, uf, unlock);
}

int do_vmi_align_munmap(struct vma_iterator *vmi, struct vm_area_struct *vma,
		struct mm_struct *mm, unsigned long start, unsigned long end,
		struct list_head *uf, bool unlock)
{
	...
	error = vms_gather_munmap_vmas(&vms, &mas_detach);
	...
	error = vma_iter_clear_gfp(vmi, start, end, GFP_KERNEL);
	...
	vms_complete_munmap_vmas(&vms, &mas_detach);
	return 0;
}

There are three entry points into that machinery, and the difference between them is only who holds the lock and who drops it. __vm_munmap() is the outermost: it takes mmap_write_lock_killable(), calls do_vmi_munmap(), and finishes with userfaultfd_unmap_complete() after the lock is released. The syscall reaches it through __vm_munmap(addr, len, true), where true asks the lower layers to drop the lock themselves on success; the in-kernel vm_munmap() — exported for drivers — passes false. do_munmap() is a third variant for callers that already hold the write lock. Note also the untagged_addr() in the syscall wrapper: on ARM64 with top-byte-ignore or MTE enabled, user pointers may carry a tag in their upper bits that must be stripped before the address is compared against VMA boundaries.

Two aspects of the error contract are worth stating explicitly, because they surprise people. First, do_vmi_munmap() rejects an unaligned start, a start beyond TASK_SIZE, a length that overflows past it, and a zero-length request, all with -EINVAL. Second, if the range is well formed but simply contains no mappings at all, vma_find() returns NULL and the call succeeds with 0. Unmapping something that was never mapped is not an error in POSIX, and Linux honours that — which is why munmap() is safe to call defensively but useless as a probe for whether an address is mapped.

The modern munmap() path is best understood as a pipeline:

  1. Validate and align: reject invalid ranges, page-align the length, and compute end.
  2. Find the first overlapping VMA: do_vmi_munmap() uses a VMA iterator and vma_find().
  3. Split boundary VMAs if necessary: the range to be removed must line up with VMA boundaries before final detachment.
  4. Detach from the maple tree: the affected VMAs are gathered and the interval is cleared from mm->mm_mt.
  5. Unmap page tables and reverse mappings: page-table teardown, TLB invalidation, file/anon unlinking, and userfaultfd handling occur in the completion phase.
  6. Free metadata: dead VMAs are finally destroyed after the core unmap work completes.

That split between gather, clear tree, and complete is one of the clearer signatures of the modern maple-tree-based implementation. The code first stages the affected VMAs in a detached temporary tree, then removes the interval from the live tree, then performs the irreversible completion steps. In other words, topology mutation and resource teardown are related but not identical phases.

Boundary splitting is unavoidable because user space can unmap arbitrary subranges. If a VMA spans 0x400000-0x500000 and the process calls munmap(0x440000, 0x8000), Linux cannot delete the entire VMA. It must preserve the left and right survivors with correct offsets, policy, userfaultfd context, anon naming, and reverse-mapping metadata. That is why split_vma() appears not only in mprotect() and mremap() logic, but also in unmap paths.

The Three Phases of munmap()

All of the shared state for an unmap lives in a struct vma_munmap_struct, declared in mm/vma.h. It is worth reading, because its fields are the algorithm:

/* mm/vma.h */
struct vma_munmap_struct {
	struct vma_iterator *vmi;
	struct vm_area_struct *vma;     /* The first vma to munmap */
	struct vm_area_struct *prev;    /* vma before the munmap area */
	struct vm_area_struct *next;    /* vma after the munmap area */
	struct list_head *uf;           /* Userfaultfd list_head */
	unsigned long start;            /* Aligned start addr (inclusive) */
	unsigned long end;              /* Aligned end addr (exclusive) */
	unsigned long unmap_start;      /* Unmap PTE start */
	unsigned long unmap_end;        /* Unmap PTE end */
	int vma_count;                  /* Number of vmas that will be removed */
	bool unlock;                    /* Unlock after the munmap */
	bool clear_ptes;                /* If there are outstanding PTE to be cleared */
	/* 2 byte hole */
	unsigned long nr_pages;         /* Number of pages being removed */
	unsigned long locked_vm;        /* Number of locked pages */
	unsigned long nr_accounted;     /* Number of VM_ACCOUNT pages */
	unsigned long exec_vm;
	unsigned long stack_vm;
	unsigned long data_vm;
};

The counters at the bottom exist because the statistics cannot be updated as the VMAs are visited. If the operation fails partway through, none of them must have been applied. So the gather phase accumulates what the removal would cost, and the completion phase subtracts it all at once. The same reasoning explains prev and next: they are captured during the gather, while the neighbours are still reachable through the live tree, and used afterwards, when the range in between has been erased from it.

Phase 1, vms_gather_munmap_vmas(). This does everything that can fail. It splits the first VMA if the range starts in the middle of one, then walks for_each_vma_range() splitting the last VMA if the range ends in the middle of one. For each VMA in between it takes the VMA write lock with vma_start_write(), stores the VMA into a second, stack-allocated maple tree through mas_store_gfp(), marks it detached with vma_mark_detached(), adds its pages to the accumulators, and calls userfaultfd_unmap_prep() if anyone is watching. At the end it records unmap_start and unmap_end from the surviving neighbours and sets clear_ptes.

Two checks in that loop are easy to miss. The first is the max_map_count guard, which applies only when the range falls strictly inside a single VMA — the case that produces two survivors and therefore a net increase of one mapping. Unmapping is the one operation that can fail with -ENOMEM for having too many mappings, and this is why. The comment in the source notes that the count is allowed to exceed the limit temporarily, since the point is to free resources. The second is vma_is_sealed(): a VMA sealed with mseal() refuses to be unmapped with -EPERM, and the check is deliberately made before the split, since splitting a VMA that cannot be unmapped anyway would be pure waste.

Phase 2, vma_iter_clear_gfp(). A single call that stores NULL over [start, end) in the live maple tree. This is the moment the range stops existing as far as any lookup is concerned. It is separated from the other two phases because it is the last step that can fail: storing a NULL over an interval may require the tree to allocate nodes, so it can return -ENOMEM.

Phase 3, vms_complete_munmap_vmas(). Labelled in the source with the comment /* Point of no return */, and it returns void because nothing in it is allowed to fail. It decrements map_count and locked_vm, optionally downgrades the lock, clears the PTEs, updates the high-water marks and the total_vm/exec_vm/stack_vm/data_vm counters from the accumulators, calls remove_vma() on every gathered VMA, returns the commit charge with vm_unacct_memory(), and destroys the temporary tree.

The failure paths make the design explicit. If phase 1 fails after detaching some VMAs, or phase 2 fails, reattach_vmas() walks the temporary tree calling vma_mark_attached() on each entry and throws the tree away. Nothing was ever removed from the live tree at that point, so the address space is exactly as it was. This is the same gather-and-maybe-reattach protocol that __mmap_region() uses for a MAP_FIXED replacement, and indeed it is literally the same code: __mmap_setup() calls vms_gather_munmap_vmas() and __mmap_complete() calls vms_complete_munmap_vmas().

The __split_vma() calls in phase 1 are not undone on failure, and the source says so plainly: the two splits leave the address space describing the same memory with the same permissions, just recorded as more VMAs than before. Splitting is semantically invisible to user space, so leaving a stray split behind on an error path is a cosmetic wart rather than a correctness bug. Detaching a VMA, by contrast, is very much visible, which is why that is undone.

Tearing Down Page Tables

Removing VMAs from a tree does not free a single page. That work is vms_clear_ptes(), which builds a struct unmap_desc and hands it to unmap_region():

/* mm/vma.c */
void unmap_region(struct unmap_desc *unmap)
{
	struct mm_struct *mm = unmap->first->vm_mm;
	struct mmu_gather tlb;

	tlb_gather_mmu(&tlb, mm);
	update_hiwater_rss(mm);
	unmap_vmas(&tlb, unmap);
	mas_set(unmap->mas, unmap->tree_reset);
	free_pgtables(&tlb, unmap);
	tlb_finish_mmu(&tlb);
}

The struct mmu_gather is the batching mechanism described in Chapter 2: rather than flushing the TLB after every PTE, the unmap accumulates freed pages and the address range touched, and issues one flush — and on SMP one round of shootdown IPIs — at tlb_finish_mmu(). This is why unmapping one large region is dramatically cheaper than unmapping many small ones: the expensive part is per-batch, not per-page.

unmap_vmas() clears the PTEs and drops the page references. free_pgtables() then reclaims the page-table pages themselves, and that is where unmap_start and unmap_end matter. A page-table page may be shared with a neighbouring VMA — a PMD covers 2 MiB, so a small mapping beside another small mapping very likely share one. The gather phase therefore recorded the surviving neighbours' boundaries as a floor and ceiling: prev->vm_end and next->vm_start, or FIRST_USER_ADDRESS and USER_PGTABLES_CEILING when there is no neighbour on that side. free_pgtables() frees only the table pages that lie wholly within that window, so a table still describing a live neighbour survives.

Note the ordering around update_hiwater_rss() and, in the caller, update_hiwater_vm(). Both are called before the counters are lowered, because VmHWM and VmPeak in /proc/<pid>/status must record the peak the process actually reached. Sampling the high-water mark after the decrement would silently lose it.

Finally, remove_vma() disposes of each descriptor:

/* mm/vma.c */
void remove_vma(struct vm_area_struct *vma)
{
	might_sleep();
	vma_close(vma);
	if (vma->vm_file)
		fput(vma->vm_file);
	mpol_put(vma_policy(vma));
	vm_area_free(vma);
}

Four distinct resources, in a required order: the driver's vm_ops->close callback runs first and may need to sleep, then the file reference is dropped — which is what finally allows a deleted file's inode to be reclaimed once the last mapping goes — then the NUMA policy reference, and only then the descriptor itself. vm_area_free() defers the actual kmem_cache_free() through RCU, so a lockless fault handler that is still holding a reference to this VMA cannot trip over freed memory.

Lock Downgrade and Detachment

mmap_lock is central here. Topology-changing operations such as mmap(), munmap(), and brk() take it for write. Read-mostly paths such as inspection and many fault operations use read locking or more specialized helpers. The use of a single process-wide lock may sound coarse, but keep in mind what it protects: not only a search tree, but a set of interval objects whose identity affects reverse mapping, file writeback, userfaultfd, KSM, NUMA policy, procfs reporting, and future merges. Per-VMA locks reduce contention for some fault-side readers, but they do not eliminate the need for a write-side authority on topology changes.

The old name mmap_sem tempted readers to think of this lock as only a legacy reader-writer semaphore around address lookups. The new name mmap_lock is conceptually better: it is the central lock governing the logical map of the process. In the maple-tree era, that map is a richer object than a simple linked list or binary tree of regions.

The unlock flag threaded through the whole path is what allows an optimisation that would otherwise be unsafe. When munmap() is called from the syscall — as opposed to internally from mmap() or brk() — the completion phase calls mmap_write_downgrade() before tearing down the page tables, and mmap_read_unlock() at the end. The expensive part of the operation, which can touch millions of PTEs and issue TLB shootdowns, therefore runs while other threads can take the lock for reading and service page faults elsewhere in the address space.

This is safe only because of what phase 1 did. The VMAs being torn down were removed from the live tree and marked detached, so no concurrent reader can find them; and each one had its own write lock taken with vma_start_write(), so no per-VMA-lock fault handler can be operating on one either. Isolation, not exclusion, is what makes the downgrade sound. The mm_wr_locked argument that vms_clear_ptes() passes down to free_pgtables() exists precisely to record which of the two states the caller is in, and the source comment says as much: page tables can be freed without the write lock because the VMAs were isolated before the downgrade.

The consequence for the reader is that munmap() is not one atomic step but two visible ones. Between the tree update and the completion, the range is already gone from every lookup while its pages are still mapped. Nothing can observe those pages through the VMA layer, so the intermediate state is not user-visible — but it is why the page-table walkers must never assume that a present PTE implies a findable VMA.

Cost and Correctness

brk() also shows the interplay between heap management and unmapping. When shrinking the heap, the syscall path in mm/mmap.c calls do_vmi_align_munmap() after locating the brk VMA with VMA iterators. In other words, even the “old Unix heap” is implemented today by the same generalized VMA topology machinery as every other modern mapping type.

From a performance standpoint, munmap() cost is dominated not by removing one record from a tree, but by the consequences of removal: page-table zaps, TLB shootdowns on SMP systems, reverse-mapping cleanup, and potential fragmentation of the remaining VMA topology. That is one reason high-performance runtimes often try to recycle arenas with madvise() or protection changes rather than constantly mapping and unmapping small pieces.

From a correctness standpoint, munmap() is where stale assumptions die. After a successful unmap, later accesses must fault; later lookups must not rediscover dead intervals; file mappings must stop advertising writable shared ranges; userfaultfd observers must see the removal; and merges must not accidentally cross the hole. The choreography in do_vmi_align_munmap() exists to make those postconditions explicit.

At this point in the chapter, we have the full topology story for the major constructor and destructor of process address space: mmap() inserts, optionally merges, and defers physical population; munmap() locates, splits, detaches, unmaps, and destroys. The next half of the chapter can build on this foundation to explain fault-time population, copy-on-write, brk() growth, page-table interactions, and more advanced interfaces such as userfaultfd in greater detail.

The Page Fault Handler

Everything in Sections 1 through 5 describes the shape of a process address space — the VMA topology, the maple tree index, the mmap/munmap lifecycle. The page fault handler is where that shape becomes executable reality. It is arguably the most performance-critical path in the entire kernel: on a system running thousands of processes, millions of page faults may occur per second, and every unnecessary instruction, cache miss, or lock contention in this path directly impacts application throughput.

When the CPU cannot complete a load, store, or instruction fetch because the translation is missing or the permissions do not allow the access, control transfers into architecture-specific exception code, which decodes the fault, finds the relevant VMA, and decides whether the kernel should populate a new page, break copy-on-write sharing, signal the task, or ask user space to resolve the fault through userfaultfd.

This is demand paging in its purest form. Chapter 2 introduced the hardware walk through multi-level page tables, and Chapter 8 explained where the physical folios come from. The fault path is the bridge between them: architecture entry code extracts the faulting address and access type, handle_mm_fault() walks the page-table levels, and specialized helpers such as do_anonymous_page(), do_fault(), do_wp_page(), and huge-page handlers decide what concrete mapping should appear.

The fault handler must answer several questions in sequence, and the answer to each determines the next step:

  1. Is the address valid? — Does a VMA exist that covers this address? If not, send SIGSEGV.
  2. Is the access permitted? — Does the VMA's vm_flags allow the type of access (read/write/execute) that was attempted? If not, send SIGSEGV.
  3. Is the translation present? — Is a page-table entry installed? If not, this is a demand paging fault — allocate or look up the backing folio and install a PTE.
  4. Is it a permission fault? — Is the PTE present but read-only on a write access? If so, this is likely a copy-on-write fault — decide whether to reuse the folio or copy it.
  5. Is it a special entry? — Does the PTE slot contain a swap entry, migration entry, device-private entry, or NUMA hint? Each has its own resolution path.
  6. Should user space handle it? — Is the VMA registered with userfaultfd? If so, wake the monitor and wait for its resolution.

Because page faults are extraordinarily frequent on real systems, this code is also where Linux invests heavily in concurrency work. The classic model was simple but blunt: take the process-wide mmap_lock in read mode, look up the VMA, walk page tables, and resolve the fault. Modern kernels still fall back to that path, but optional CONFIG_PER_VMA_LOCK adds a faster route that can avoid the global lock for eligible faults. That design change mirrors the broader evolution of MM in this book: more interval-aware data structures, more fine-grained sequencing, and a stronger separation between topology updates and read-mostly fault handling.

The following figure shows the high-level architecture of the page fault handler, from hardware exception to final PTE installation:

Architecture Entry Points

The page fault handler has a clear two-layer design: architecture-specific entry code at the top and generic MM code below. This separation is essential because different processor architectures report page faults through fundamentally different mechanisms — different exception vectors, different registers for the faulting address, different encodings of the fault reason — but the kernel's response (find the VMA, walk page tables, install a mapping) is the same regardless of architecture. The architecture layer's job is to translate the hardware's raw exception data into a normalized form that the generic layer can consume.

A page fault begins as a hardware exception, so the first code that runs is architecture-specific. On x86_64 the main entry path lives in arch/x86/mm/fault.c. The low-level handler records the fault address from CR2, inspects the x86 page-fault error code, and then routes user addresses to do_user_addr_fault(). Kernel-mode faults take a different path because they may represent either a kernel bug or a recoverable access such as a copy-to-user helper probing a user mapping. 

/* arch/x86/mm/fault.c — x86_64 entry, abbreviated */
DEFINE_IDTENTRY_RAW_ERRORCODE(exc_page_fault)
{
	irqentry_state_t state;
	unsigned long address;

	address = cpu_feature_enabled(X86_FEATURE_FRED) ? fred_event_data(regs) : read_cr2();

	if (kvm_handle_async_pf(regs, (u32)address))
		return;

	state = irqentry_enter(regs);

	instrumentation_begin();
	handle_page_fault(regs, error_code, address);
	instrumentation_end();

	irqentry_exit(regs, state);
}

static __always_inline void
handle_page_fault(struct pt_regs *regs, unsigned long error_code,
			      unsigned long address)
{
	trace_page_fault_entries(regs, error_code, address);

	if (unlikely(kmmio_fault(regs, address)))
		return;

	/* Was the fault on kernel-controlled part of the address space? */
	if (unlikely(fault_in_kernel_space(address))) {
		do_kern_addr_fault(regs, error_code, address);
	} else {
		do_user_addr_fault(regs, error_code, address);
	}
	/*
	 * page fault handling might have reenabled interrupts,
	 * make sure to disable them again.
	 */
	local_irq_disable();
}

Three details in that short function are worth naming. The faulting address no longer comes unconditionally from CR2: on CPUs with FRED (Flexible Return and Event Delivery) the hardware delivers it in the event-data field of the exception frame, and fred_event_data() reads it from pt_regs instead. The kvm_handle_async_pf() call intercepts the fault before anything else, because a KVM guest receives asynchronous page fault notifications from the host through the same #PF vector — the host is telling the guest that a page it believes is present is actually swapped out on the host side, and the guest should schedule something else rather than spin. And kmmio_fault() gives the mmiotrace debugging facility first refusal on faults it deliberately provoked by unmapping MMIO pages. Only what survives all three checks is a real page fault.

ARM64 expresses the same ideas with different registers and a different dispatch structure. The fault address is reported through FAR_EL1, and the exception syndrome register ESR_ELx carries both the exception class and a data-fault status code (DFSC) describing exactly which stage of translation failed. Rather than one handler that decodes everything, arch/arm64/mm/fault.c uses the DFSC as an index into a table of handlers, so the hardware's own classification chooses the software path directly: 

/* arch/arm64/mm/fault.c — syndrome-driven dispatch, abbreviated */
static const struct fault_info fault_info[] = {
	...
	{ do_translation_fault,	SIGSEGV, SEGV_MAPERR,	"level 0 translation fault"	},
	{ do_translation_fault,	SIGSEGV, SEGV_MAPERR,	"level 1 translation fault"	},
	{ do_translation_fault,	SIGSEGV, SEGV_MAPERR,	"level 2 translation fault"	},
	{ do_translation_fault,	SIGSEGV, SEGV_MAPERR,	"level 3 translation fault"	},
	{ do_page_fault,	SIGSEGV, SEGV_ACCERR,	"level 0 access flag fault"	},
	...
	{ do_page_fault,	SIGSEGV, SEGV_ACCERR,	"level 1 permission fault"	},
	{ do_page_fault,	SIGSEGV, SEGV_ACCERR,	"level 2 permission fault"	},
	{ do_page_fault,	SIGSEGV, SEGV_ACCERR,	"level 3 permission fault"	},
	...
};

void do_mem_abort(unsigned long far, unsigned long esr, struct pt_regs *regs)
{
	const struct fault_info *inf = esr_to_fault_info(esr);
	...
	if (!inf->fn(far, esr, regs))
		return;
	...
}

Note what this buys. The table already carries the signal and si_code that a hopeless fault should produce — SEGV_MAPERR for a translation fault (nothing was mapped) and SEGV_ACCERR for a permission or access-flag fault (something was mapped but the access was not allowed) — so the distinction that x86 recovers by testing X86_PF_PROT is handed to ARM64 for free. Entries for other abort types never reach the MM layer: a synchronous external abort, for instance, goes to do_sea(), which hands memory errors to the firmware-first error-handling code and from there to memory_failure() (see Memory Failure and HWPoison). do_translation_fault() forwards user addresses to do_page_fault() and treats kernel addresses as fatal; do_page_fault() is the one that actually consults the VMA topology. 

/* arch/arm64/mm/fault.c — deriving the generic flags, abbreviated */
static int __kprobes do_page_fault(unsigned long far, unsigned long esr,
				   struct pt_regs *regs)
{
	struct mm_struct *mm = current->mm;
	unsigned int mm_flags = FAULT_FLAG_DEFAULT;
	unsigned long addr = untagged_addr(far);
	vm_flags_t vm_flags;
	...
	if (user_mode(regs))
		mm_flags |= FAULT_FLAG_USER;

	if (is_el0_instruction_abort(esr)) {
		/* It was exec fault */
		vm_flags = VM_EXEC;
		mm_flags |= FAULT_FLAG_INSTRUCTION;
	} else if (is_gcs_fault(esr)) {
		/*
		 * The GCS permission on a page implies both read and
		 * write so always handle any GCS fault as a write fault,
		 * we need to trigger CoW even for GCS reads.
		 */
		vm_flags = VM_WRITE;
		mm_flags |= FAULT_FLAG_WRITE;
	} else if (is_write_abort(esr)) {
		/* It was write fault */
		vm_flags = VM_WRITE;
		mm_flags |= FAULT_FLAG_WRITE;
	} else {
		/* It was read fault */
		vm_flags = VM_READ;
		/* Write implies read */
		vm_flags |= VM_WRITE;
		/* If EPAN is absent then exec implies read */
		if (!alternative_has_cap_unlikely(ARM64_HAS_EPAN))
			vm_flags |= VM_EXEC;
	}
	...
}

The local vm_flags variable is the ARM64 analogue of the x86 access_error() test discussed below: it records the bit that the VMA must have for the access to be legitimate, and every path that finds !(vma->vm_flags & vm_flags) jumps straight to SIGSEGV with SEGV_ACCERR. Two of the branches encode recent architecture extensions. GCS is the Guarded Control Stack, ARM64's hardware shadow stack; because a GCS page grants read and write together, even a GCS read has to be treated as a write so that copy-on-write is triggered on a page shared after fork(). EPAN is Enhanced Privileged Access Never, which makes execute-only user mappings possible; without it, execute permission implies read, and the fallback in the last branch says so. Also note untagged_addr(far): the same tag stripping that munmap() needed applies to the faulting address.

The big conceptual difference is not that one architecture “has more information” than the other, but that they encode it differently. x86 supplies a compact page-fault error code whose bits describe presence, write versus read, user versus supervisor, and a small set of fault qualifiers. ARM64 instead embeds the reason in the syndrome register and decodes it through helpers such as is_permission_fault(), is_translation_fault(), and is_write_abort(). Linux normalizes both styles into the generic FAULT_FLAG_* vocabulary before calling the common MM layer.

Table 9-10. x86_64 Page-Fault Error Code Bits (arch/x86/include/asm/trap_pf.h)
Bit Name Meaning when set
0X86_PF_PROTThe fault was caused by a protection violation rather than a not-present translation.
1X86_PF_WRITEThe access was a write; if clear, the access was a read.
2X86_PF_USERThe fault originated in user mode.
3X86_PF_RSVDA reserved bit was set in a paging-structure entry. Always a kernel bug or corrupted page table; never resolvable by MM.
4X86_PF_INSTRThe fault came from an instruction fetch.
5X86_PF_PKA protection key blocked the access. Unconditionally an error — no CoW or demand-paging action can resolve it.
6X86_PF_SHSTKA shadow-stack access faulted. Only legal against a VM_SHADOW_STACK VMA.
15X86_PF_SGXSGX hardware blocked the access, typically because enclave memory was destroyed across a suspend/resume cycle. Reported to user space so the enclave can be rebuilt.
31X86_PF_RMPAn AMD SEV-SNP Reverse Map Table violation.

Those bits matter because they distinguish the two classic cases: missing translation and protection fault. A missing translation usually invites demand paging. A protection fault may instead require copy-on-write, a signal such as SIGSEGV, or architecture-specific handling for pkeys, shadow stacks, or execute permissions. By the time the generic code runs, it needs a normalized description of the attempted access, not just the raw hardware register dump.

On x86 that classification is concentrated in one function, access_error(), which the fault path calls once it has a VMA in hand. It is worth reading as a statement of what the hardware bits mean to MM: 

/* arch/x86/mm/fault.c — is this access legal against this VMA? */
	if (error_code & X86_PF_PK)
		return 1;

	if (unlikely(error_code & X86_PF_SGX))
		return 1;

	if (!arch_vma_access_permitted(vma, (error_code & X86_PF_WRITE),
				       (error_code & X86_PF_INSTR), foreign))
		return 1;

	/*
	 * Shadow stack accesses (PF_SHSTK=1) are only permitted to
	 * shadow stack VMAs. All other accesses result in an error.
	 */
	if (error_code & X86_PF_SHSTK) {
		if (unlikely(!(vma->vm_flags & VM_SHADOW_STACK)))
			return 1;
		if (unlikely(!(vma->vm_flags & VM_WRITE)))
			return 1;
		return 0;
	}

	if (error_code & X86_PF_WRITE) {
		/* write, present and write, not present: */
		if (unlikely(vma->vm_flags & VM_SHADOW_STACK))
			return 1;
		if (unlikely(!(vma->vm_flags & VM_WRITE)))
			return 1;
		return 0;
	}

	/* read, present: */
	if (unlikely(error_code & X86_PF_PROT))
		return 1;

	/* read, not present: */
	if (unlikely(!vma_is_accessible(vma)))
		return 1;

	return 0;

The two comments at the bottom compress the whole distinction. A read on a present page that still faulted cannot be fixed by mapping anything — something denied it, so it is an error. A read on a non-present page is exactly the demand-paging case, and is allowed as long as the VMA is accessible at all. Note also the symmetry of the two shadow-stack tests: a shadow-stack access to a normal VMA is illegal, and an ordinary write to a shadow-stack VMA is equally illegal. Shadow-stack memory is writable only by the specific hardware instructions that maintain it.

Despite the differing decode styles, the two architectures then run structurally identical code, and it is worth learning the shape once:

  1. Try the fast path. If the fault came from user mode, call lock_vma_under_rcu(). On success, check the access against the VMA, then call handle_mm_fault() with FAULT_FLAG_VMA_LOCK added, and release the VMA with vma_end_read() unless the result was VM_FAULT_RETRY or VM_FAULT_COMPLETED.
  2. Fall back. If the fast path could not be used or returned VM_FAULT_RETRY, jump to a retry: label and call lock_mm_and_find_vma(), which takes mmap_lock for reading, looks up the VMA, and — crucially — grows a stack VMA downward if the address falls in its guard region.
  3. Re-check and re-enter. Validate the access again and call handle_mm_fault() without FAULT_FLAG_VMA_LOCK.
  4. Handle the result. fault_signal_pending() bails out early if a fatal signal arrived while the handler slept. VM_FAULT_COMPLETED returns immediately. VM_FAULT_RETRY sets FAULT_FLAG_TRIED and loops back to retry: — once, because the flag suppresses further retries. Anything in VM_FAULT_ERROR becomes SIGBUS, SIGSEGV, or OOM handling.
  5. Or give up. Every failed lookup or permission check jumps to a bad_area path that delivers SIGSEGV with SEGV_MAPERR, SEGV_ACCERR, or SEGV_PKUERR — or, if the fault came from kernel mode, tries an exception fixup first.

The lock_mm_and_find_vma() helper deserves a note of its own, because it absorbs work that older kernels handled elsewhere. Stack expansion used to be handled by the fault code noticing that the address lay just below a VM_GROWSDOWN VMA and calling expand_stack() inline. That pattern is gone: growing the stack needs the write lock, but the fault path holds the read lock, so the modern helper encapsulates the whole upgrade-and-retry dance in one place shared by every architecture. A fault a few bytes below the stack pointer therefore silently becomes a larger stack, while a fault below the guard gap becomes SIGSEGV — and neither case is visible in the architecture entry code any more. Internally it tries mmap_read_trylock(), attempts to upgrade to the write lock, calls expand_stack_locked(), and finally mmap_write_downgrade()s back to a read lock before returning, so the caller always gets the same lock state regardless of which path ran. One further precaution applies only to kernel-mode faults: before blocking on mmap_lock at all, the helper calls search_exception_tables() on the faulting instruction pointer. If the instruction is not one that is expected to fault, the fault is a kernel bug, and waiting on the lock could deadlock against whichever writer holds it.

The fault address itself comes from different architectural sources, but conceptually it plays the same role everywhere. On x86 the handler reads CR2. On ARM64 the exception entry code saves FAR_EL1. Either way, Linux receives a virtual address that must be interpreted against the process's VMA topology before any page-table update is possible.

This architecture front end is also where Linux decides whether the fault is even eligible for normal MM handling. Reserved-bit violations, kernel-space faults on obviously bad addresses, or faults taken in contexts that cannot sleep may bypass the generic path and terminate the task or panic the kernel. Only after those quick exclusions does the code translate the architecture syndrome into the generic MM vocabulary and descend into handle_mm_fault().

The Generic Fault Path

Once architecture code has normalized the fault into a FAULT_FLAG_* vocabulary and located the relevant VMA, control passes to the architecture-independent generic fault path. This is the core of the page-fault handler — the layer that makes Linux's VM system portable across dozens of architectures. Every architecture entry point eventually funnels into the same handle_mm_fault() function, regardless of whether the CPU speaks x86 page-fault error codes, ARM ESR fields, or RISC-V SCAUSE values.
