The generic fault path performs three essential jobs: (1) it walks the page-table hierarchy (PGD → P4D → PUD → PMD → PTE), allocating intermediate tables as needed; (2) it classifies the PTE state at each level to determine what kind of fault this is (missing entry, swap entry, NUMA migration entry, write-protection fault, or huge-page-level mapping); and (3) it dispatches to the appropriate leaf handler (demand paging, copy-on-write, swap-in, etc.) based on that classification. The entire flow is organized around the struct vm_fault, a transient per-fault structure that accumulates state as the handler descends through the levels.

The architecture-independent heart of the fault path lives in mm/memory.c. Its public entry is handle_mm_fault(), which receives a VMA, the faulting address, and a set of generic fault flags. That function is not a single monolithic resolver. Instead, it is a coordinator that sets up accounting, delegates to __handle_mm_fault(), and converts internal events into the VM_FAULT_* result bits that the architecture code interprets. 

/* include/linux/mm.h — comments abridged */
struct vm_fault {
	const struct {
		struct vm_area_struct *vma;	/* Target VMA */
		gfp_t gfp_mask;			/* gfp mask to be used for allocations */
		pgoff_t pgoff;			/* Logical page offset based on vma */
		unsigned long address;		/* Faulting virtual address - masked */
		unsigned long real_address;	/* Faulting virtual address - unmasked */
	};
	enum fault_flag flags;		/* FAULT_FLAG_xxx flags */
	pmd_t *pmd;			/* Pointer to pmd entry matching the 'address' */
	pud_t *pud;			/* Pointer to pud entry matching the 'address' */
	union {
		pte_t orig_pte;		/* Value of PTE at the time of fault */
		pmd_t orig_pmd;		/* Value of PMD at the time of fault,
					 * used by PMD fault only.
					 */
	};

	struct page *cow_page;		/* Page handler may use for COW fault */
	struct page *page;		/* ->fault handlers should return a
					 * page here, unless VM_FAULT_NOPAGE
					 * is set (which is also implied by
					 * VM_FAULT_ERROR).
					 */
	/* These three entries are valid only while holding ptl lock */
	pte_t *pte;			/* Pointer to pte entry matching
					 * the 'address'. NULL if the page
					 * table hasn't been allocated.
					 */
	spinlock_t *ptl;		/* Page table lock. */
	pgtable_t prealloc_pte;		/* Pre-allocated pte page table. */
};

struct vm_fault is the transient packet of state that carries one fault through the resolver. It records the target VMA, the aligned address, the page offset into the mapping, flags describing the access, and the page-table level pointers discovered while walking the hierarchy. As the resolver goes deeper, the structure accumulates more concrete detail: which pmd was found, what the original PTE contained, which folio was allocated or looked up, and whether the path is handling a copy-on-write case.

Three structural details reward a second look. The first five fields are wrapped in an anonymous const struct, which is the compiler enforcing that no leaf handler may retarget a fault at a different VMA or address partway through. address is page-aligned while real_address keeps the byte the instruction actually touched — the alignment is what the page-table code needs, the exact address is what a SIGBUS or userfaultfd report must carry. And orig_pte shares storage with orig_pmd in a union, because a fault is resolved at exactly one level: either the PMD is the leaf or the PTE is, never both.

The ptl comment marks the most important invariant in the whole structure. pte, ptl, and the value in orig_pte are only meaningful while the page-table lock is held. Leaf handlers routinely drop that lock — to allocate, to do I/O, to hand off to userfaultfd — and when they take it again they must re-read the PTE and compare it against orig_pte, because another CPU may have resolved the same fault in the meantime. The helper vmf_pte_changed() exists for exactly this re-validation, and nearly every successful path calls it before committing.

The most important generic flags are the ones that summarize the attempted access and locking context: FAULT_FLAG_WRITE means the access intends to write, FAULT_FLAG_USER means the fault came from user mode, FAULT_FLAG_REMOTE marks faults taken on behalf of another task, and FAULT_FLAG_VMA_LOCK records that the fast path entered under a per-VMA read lock rather than the process-wide mmap_lock. Other bits describe instruction fetches, retry state, killable waits, or “already tried” retries. The fault handler uses this flag set both to choose a subpath and to decide which locks or retries are legal.

Table 9-11. FAULT_FLAG_* Values (include/linux/mm_types.h)
Flag Set by Meaning
FAULT_FLAG_WRITEArchitecture entryThe access was a write. Selects CoW handling on a present read-only PTE.
FAULT_FLAG_MKWRITEInternalThe fault is making an existing mapping writable rather than instantiating one.
FAULT_FLAG_ALLOW_RETRYArchitecture entry (via FAULT_FLAG_DEFAULT)The handler is permitted to drop the lock and return VM_FAULT_RETRY rather than blocking.
FAULT_FLAG_RETRY_NOWAITGUP and KVMDo not block at all; return VM_FAULT_RETRY instead of sleeping. Incompatible with FAULT_FLAG_VMA_LOCK.
FAULT_FLAG_KILLABLEArchitecture entry (via FAULT_FLAG_DEFAULT)Waits inside the handler may be interrupted by a fatal signal.
FAULT_FLAG_TRIEDArchitecture entry, on the second passThis is a retry. Suppresses a further VM_FAULT_RETRY, bounding the loop at one repetition.
FAULT_FLAG_USERArchitecture entryThe fault came from user mode. Enables memcg OOM handling and permits SIGSEGV delivery.
FAULT_FLAG_REMOTEget_user_pages_remote()The fault is being taken on behalf of another task, so current's credentials and pkey register do not apply.
FAULT_FLAG_INSTRUCTIONArchitecture entryThe access was an instruction fetch.
FAULT_FLAG_INTERRUPTIBLEArchitecture entry (via FAULT_FLAG_DEFAULT)Waits may be interrupted by any signal, not only a fatal one — required for userfaultfd, whose monitor may never answer.
FAULT_FLAG_UNSHAREGUPNot a write: break sharing so a long-term read-only pin cannot observe a later CoW by another process. Never combined with FAULT_FLAG_WRITE.
FAULT_FLAG_ORIG_PTE_VALIDhandle_pte_fault()vmf->orig_pte holds a real snapshot; without it the PTE page did not exist.
FAULT_FLAG_VMA_LOCKArchitecture entry, fast pathThe fault holds only the per-VMA read lock, not mmap_lock. Handlers that need the wider lock bail out with VM_FAULT_RETRY.

FAULT_FLAG_DEFAULT is simply FAULT_FLAG_ALLOW_RETRY | FAULT_FLAG_KILLABLE | FAULT_FLAG_INTERRUPTIBLE, which is what both architecture entry points start from before adding the access-specific bits. FAULT_FLAG_UNSHARE is the least intuitive of the set and the most recent in origin: it exists because a process that takes a long-term read-only pin on a page, then forks, could otherwise find the child’s later write silently redirected to a copy while the pin still points at the original. Forcing the unshare at pin time removes the divergence. 

/* mm/memory.c — generic fault entry, abbreviated */
vm_fault_t handle_mm_fault(struct vm_area_struct *vma, unsigned long address,
			   unsigned int flags, struct pt_regs *regs)
{
	/* If the fault handler drops the mmap_lock, vma may be freed */
	struct mm_struct *mm = vma->vm_mm;
	vm_fault_t ret;
	bool is_droppable;

	__set_current_state(TASK_RUNNING);

	ret = sanitize_fault_flags(vma, &flags);
	if (ret)
		goto out;

	if (!arch_vma_access_permitted(vma, flags & FAULT_FLAG_WRITE,
					    flags & FAULT_FLAG_INSTRUCTION,
					    flags & FAULT_FLAG_REMOTE)) {
		ret = VM_FAULT_SIGSEGV;
		goto out;
	}

	is_droppable = !!(vma->vm_flags & VM_DROPPABLE);

	/*
	 * Enable the memcg OOM handling for faults triggered in user
	 * space.  Kernel faults are handled more gracefully.
	 */
	if (flags & FAULT_FLAG_USER)
		mem_cgroup_enter_user_fault();

	lru_gen_enter_fault(vma);

	if (unlikely(is_vm_hugetlb_page(vma)))
		ret = hugetlb_fault(vma->vm_mm, vma, address, flags);
	else
		ret = __handle_mm_fault(vma, address, flags);

	/*
	 * Warning: It is no longer safe to dereference vma-> after this point,
	 * because mmap_lock might have been dropped by __handle_mm_fault(), so
	 * vma might be destroyed from underneath us.
	 */

	lru_gen_exit_fault();

	/* If the mapping is droppable, then errors due to OOM aren't fatal. */
	if (is_droppable)
		ret &= ~VM_FAULT_OOM;

	if (flags & FAULT_FLAG_USER) {
		mem_cgroup_exit_user_fault();
		if (task_in_memcg_oom(current) && !(ret & VM_FAULT_OOM))
			mem_cgroup_oom_synchronize(false);
	}
out:
	mm_account_fault(mm, regs, address, flags, ret);

	return ret;
}

This wrapper is almost entirely bracketing, and each bracket is worth naming. sanitize_fault_flags() is the gatekeeper, and every one of its checks is a WARN_ON_ONCE returning VM_FAULT_SIGSEGV — these are conditions the architecture code should already have excluded, so reaching them means a caller is buggy. It rejects a write fault on a VMA lacking VM_MAYWRITE, rejects FAULT_FLAG_UNSHARE combined with FAULT_FLAG_WRITE, and rejects FAULT_FLAG_VMA_LOCK combined with FAULT_FLAG_RETRY_NOWAIT, since the per-VMA lock protocol assumes the lock is dropped on VM_FAULT_RETRY. Its one non-fatal action is to quietly clear FAULT_FLAG_UNSHARE on a non-CoW mapping, downgrading the request to an ordinary read fault. mem_cgroup_enter_user_fault() arms memcg OOM handling for the duration, so that a cgroup that hits its limit can kill the faulting task rather than failing the allocation; kernel faults deliberately opt out and are expected to cope with VM_FAULT_OOM themselves. lru_gen_enter_fault() records the VMA's executable-ness for MGLRU, which ages code pages differently from data. And mm_account_fault() is where the PGFAULT/PGMAJFAULT counters, the min_flt/maj_flt task fields, and the PERF_COUNT_SW_PAGE_FAULTS perf events are all updated — which is why every exit path funnels through the out: label rather than returning directly.

The is_droppable local looks redundant next to a later vma->vm_flags read, but it is not: the comment above the call makes the reason explicit. __handle_mm_fault() may drop mmap_lock, after which the VMA can be freed by a concurrent munmap(). The flag therefore has to be copied out before the call. The same hazard is why the architecture code stops touching vma once it sees VM_FAULT_RETRY. 

/* mm/memory.c — the page-table walk, abbreviated */
static vm_fault_t __handle_mm_fault(struct vm_area_struct *vma,
		unsigned long address, unsigned int flags)
{
	struct vm_fault vmf = {
		.vma = vma,
		.address = address & PAGE_MASK,
		.real_address = address,
		.flags = flags,
		.pgoff = linear_page_index(vma, address),
		.gfp_mask = __get_fault_gfp_mask(vma),
	};
	struct mm_struct *mm = vma->vm_mm;
	pgd_t *pgd;
	p4d_t *p4d;

	pgd = pgd_offset(mm, address);
	p4d = p4d_alloc(mm, pgd, address);
	if (!p4d)
		return VM_FAULT_OOM;

	vmf.pud = pud_alloc(mm, p4d, address);
	if (!vmf.pud)
		return VM_FAULT_OOM;
	...
	return handle_pte_fault(&vmf);
}

The _alloc suffix carries the whole idea of the walk. p4d_alloc() and pud_alloc() are not lookups that can fail benignly: they return the existing table if one is present and allocate a new one if it is not. Page tables are built lazily, on the way down, by the first fault that needs each level. This is the counterpart to the sparseness the chapter has been describing all along — a process may hold a terabyte-wide VMA and still own only a handful of page-table pages, because only the levels covering addresses that were actually touched have ever been materialized.

The classification the section keeps promising happens in one function, handle_pte_fault(), and it is short enough to read whole: 

/* mm/memory.c — the leaf dispatcher, abbreviated */
	if (!vmf->pte)
		return do_pte_missing(vmf);

	if (!pte_present(vmf->orig_pte))
		return do_swap_page(vmf);

	if (pte_protnone(vmf->orig_pte) && vma_is_accessible(vmf->vma))
		return do_numa_page(vmf);

	spin_lock(vmf->ptl);
	entry = vmf->orig_pte;
	if (unlikely(!pte_same(ptep_get(vmf->pte), entry))) {
		update_mmu_tlb(vmf->vma, vmf->address, vmf->pte);
		goto unlock;
	}
	if (vmf->flags & (FAULT_FLAG_WRITE|FAULT_FLAG_UNSHARE)) {
		if (!pte_write(entry))
			return do_wp_page(vmf);
		else if (likely(vmf->flags & FAULT_FLAG_WRITE))
			entry = pte_mkdirty(entry);
	}
	entry = pte_mkyoung(entry);
	if (ptep_set_access_flags(vmf->vma, vmf->address, vmf->pte, entry,
				vmf->flags & FAULT_FLAG_WRITE))
		update_mmu_cache_range(vmf, vmf->vma, vmf->address,
				vmf->pte, 1);
	else
		fix_spurious_fault(vmf, PGTABLE_LEVEL_PTE);
unlock:
	pte_unmap_unlock(vmf->pte, vmf->ptl);
	return 0;

Four states, four destinations. A null vmf->pte means the PTE page itself does not exist yet, and do_pte_missing() splits on vma_is_anonymous() to reach either do_anonymous_page() or do_fault(). A non-present PTE that is nevertheless non-zero is a swap entry — which in modern Linux also covers migration entries, device-private entries, hardware-poison entries, and userfaultfd markers, all of which do_swap_page() demultiplexes. A PROT_NONE PTE inside an accessible VMA is a NUMA balancing hint, deliberately unmapped by the scheduler to sample which node touches the page, and do_numa_page() restores the mapping and possibly migrates the folio. Only a present PTE reaches the write-protection test and do_wp_page().

The tail of the function is the case the earlier prose glosses over: the fault that needs nothing done. Two CPUs can fault on the same address simultaneously; the loser finds the PTE already installed, fails the pte_same() check, and returns 0 having only flushed a stale TLB entry. Or the fault may be entirely spurious — the PTE already permits the access, and the exception was raised because a remote CPU had not yet observed a TLB invalidation. That is what the fix_spurious_fault() branch handles. Meanwhile ptep_set_access_flags() quietly does the accessed- and dirty-bit maintenance for architectures whose hardware does not manage those bits itself, which is why a read fault on an already-mapped page is not simply a no-op.

The essential architectural independence appears inside __handle_mm_fault(). After validating the VMA and establishing the vm_fault structure, it descends the page-table hierarchy: PGD, P4D, PUD, PMD, and finally PTE. Chapter 2 described those levels as hardware translation steps. Here they reappear as software checkpoints. At each level the kernel can discover that a lower-level table page is absent, that a huge-page mapping already exists, that a migration or device-exclusive entry needs special handling, or that the final leaf PTE is present but write-protected.

The common implementation is careful to separate page-table walk mechanics from fault semantics. Walking down to a missing PTE page is one question. Deciding whether that absence means “install the shared zero page,” “allocate an anonymous folio,” “ask the filesystem for a cache folio,” “finish a userfaultfd protocol,” or “send SIGBUS” is another. That is why the fault dispatcher has many return edges and many specialized helpers. The generic path is unified, but the leaf actions are not.

One subtlety in this layer is that a “minor fault” is usually represented by no special result bit at all. Linux reports VM_FAULT_MAJOR when I/O or other heavyweight work makes the fault major. The absence of that bit is what user-space accounting interprets as a minor fault. That asymmetry explains why the result table below lists “minor” as an implied success state rather than a standalone flag.

Another modern wrinkle is that not every page-table walk ends at a normal PTE. The PMD might already contain a transparent huge page, a migration entry, a swap entry, or a device-private encoding. The generic path therefore treats “walk down to PTE level” as the common case, not the only case. The walk is best understood as a hierarchy of dispatch points, not a fixed tunnel with a single leaf shape.

Per-VMA Locks

The single biggest scalability improvement to the page-fault path in recent kernel history is per-VMA locking, introduced in Linux 6.4 and enabled by CONFIG_PER_VMA_LOCK. To understand why this matters, consider a multi-threaded application with 128 threads all faulting on private anonymous pages in different VMAs simultaneously. Under the classic locking model, every one of those 128 faults must acquire mmap_lock in read mode — and while read locks do not exclude each other, the shared cacheline bouncing for the rwlock counter still creates severe contention on NUMA systems. Per-VMA locks eliminate that bottleneck for the common case by allowing each fault to lock only the specific VMA it needs, bypassing mmap_lock entirely.

On a many-core machine, page faults can become bottlenecked not by allocation or I/O, but by contention on the process-wide mmap_lock. That lock protects the whole VMA topology, so even unrelated faults in disjoint regions used to serialize on a shared reader side. CONFIG_PER_VMA_LOCK addresses this by giving each VMA lightweight read-side lock state and by associating the whole mm_struct with a sequence counter, mm_lock_seq, that writers bump when they perform topology changes.

The high-level idea is simple. A fault first tries lock_vma_under_rcu(). That helper walks the maple tree under RCU, grabs a per-VMA read lock, and then verifies that the VMA's lock sequence still matches the current mm_lock_seq state. If all checks pass, the fault can proceed without taking the process-wide mmap_lock. If anything looks unstable, Linux drops back to the classic slow path and acquires mmap_lock in read mode. Correctness does not change; only the fast path does. 

/* mm/mmap_lock.c — the fast-path lookup, abbreviated */
struct vm_area_struct *lock_vma_under_rcu(struct mm_struct *mm,
					  unsigned long address)
{
	MA_STATE(mas, &mm->mm_mt, address, address);
	struct vm_area_struct *vma;

retry:
	rcu_read_lock();
	vma = mas_walk(&mas);
	if (!vma) {
		rcu_read_unlock();
		goto inval;
	}

	vma = vma_start_read(mm, vma);
	if (IS_ERR_OR_NULL(vma)) {
		/* Check if the VMA got isolated after we found it */
		if (PTR_ERR(vma) == -EAGAIN) {
			count_vm_vma_lock_event(VMA_LOCK_MISS);
			/* The area was replaced with another one */
			mas_set(&mas, address);
			goto retry;
		}

		/* Failed to lock the VMA */
		goto inval;
	}
	/*
	 * At this point, we have a stable reference to a VMA: The VMA is
	 * locked and we know it hasn't already been isolated.
	 */
	rcu_read_unlock();

	/* Check if the vma we locked is the right one. */
	if (unlikely(address < vma->vm_start || address >= vma->vm_end)) {
		vma_end_read(vma);
		goto inval;
	}

	return vma;

inval:
	count_vm_vma_lock_event(VMA_LOCK_ABORT);
	return NULL;
}

The re-check of vm_start and vm_end after the lock is taken is the crux. The maple-tree walk happened under RCU, so the VMA it returned may have been split, merged, or freed between the lookup and the lock. Taking the lock makes the object stable; only then is it worth asking whether it is still the right object.

Under the hood the mechanism has changed substantially since the feature first appeared in Linux 6.4. A per-VMA read lock was originally an rw_semaphore embedded in every vm_area_struct, which was correct but expensive: it inflated the structure and added a second write-lock acquisition to every VMA modification. Linux 7.0 replaces it with a plain refcount_t vm_refcnt, cacheline-aligned at the end of the structure, and folds the “write-locked” state into the sequence numbers instead: 

/* mm/mmap_lock.c — taking a per-VMA read lock, abbreviated */
static inline struct vm_area_struct *vma_start_read(struct mm_struct *mm,
						    struct vm_area_struct *vma)
{
	int oldcnt;

	RCU_LOCKDEP_WARN(!rcu_read_lock_held(), "no rcu lock held");
	/* Check before locking. A race might cause false locked result. */
	if (READ_ONCE(vma->vm_lock_seq) == READ_ONCE(mm->mm_lock_seq.sequence)) {
		vma = NULL;
		goto err;
	}

	if (unlikely(!__refcount_inc_not_zero_limited_acquire(&vma->vm_refcnt, &oldcnt,
							      VM_REFCNT_LIMIT))) {
		/* return EAGAIN if vma got detached from under us */
		vma = oldcnt ? NULL : ERR_PTR(-EAGAIN);
		goto err;
	}
	...
	if (unlikely(vma->vm_lock_seq == raw_read_seqcount(&mm->mm_lock_seq))) {
		vma_refcount_put(vma);
		vma = NULL;
		goto err;
	}

	return vma;
	...
}

Three distinct failure modes are distinguished, and the difference matters to the caller. A zero refcount means the VMA has been detached — recall that vms_gather_munmap_vmas() calls vma_mark_detached(), which is implemented as dropping the count to zero. That returns -EAGAIN, and lock_vma_under_rcu() re-walks the tree rather than giving up, because a detached VMA is very often being replaced by another one covering the same address (a MAP_FIXED overmap, or the surviving half of a split). A refcount that has hit VM_REFCNT_LIMIT means a writer has excluded readers. And a matching sequence number means a topology change is in flight. Only the first is worth retrying.

The sequence check appears twice, before and after the refcount increment, and neither is redundant. The first is a cheap early-out with no ordering requirement, as its comment notes. The second is the real test and must have acquire semantics, pairing with the release in vma_end_write_all() — the operation that a topology writer uses to invalidate every outstanding per-VMA read lock in the address space at once by bumping a single seqcount. That single-counter design is what keeps the write side cheap: munmap() does not have to walk the VMAs it is about to remove and unlock each one.

The whole mechanism is instrumented. With CONFIG_PER_VMA_LOCK_STATS enabled, /proc/vmstat carries vma_lock_success, vma_lock_abort, vma_lock_retry, and vma_lock_miss, so the fast-path hit rate for a workload is directly measurable rather than a matter of speculation.

The sequence numbers are the crucial correctness device. A topology writer that splits, merges, deletes, or otherwise reconfigures VMAs increments the mm-wide sequence and updates the relevant VMA-side lock sequence fields. A read-side fast-path fault is only valid if it sees a coherent snapshot: the VMA still covers the address, it was not concurrently recycled, and its lock sequence proves that no conflicting write-side change slipped by. If the snapshot is stale, fallback is mandatory.

Which faults can use this fast path? Read faults are the most natural fit because they often need only stable VMA metadata and page-table installation, not topology mutation. Some write faults can also proceed, especially when the VMA metadata itself is unchanged and the work is confined to page-table or folio updates. By contrast, paths that may need to expand a stack VMA, reconfigure userfaultfd state, retry after dropping locks for I/O, or perform operations whose invariants are still expressed in terms of mmap_lock fall back to the slow path. Eligibility is therefore about what must remain stable, not simply about whether the access was a read or a write.

Per-VMA locking does not replace the process-wide topology lock. It narrows the read-mostly fast path for page faults. All operations that materially reshape the VMA tree — mmap(), munmap(), mprotect(), mremap(), heap growth, and address-space teardown — still require a write-side authority spanning the whole map. The new design scales fault resolution without weakening that fundamental rule.

This feature also illustrates how the maple tree and per-VMA locks complement each other. The maple tree gives fast interval lookup under RCU-friendly rules, while the per-VMA lock sequence verifies that the returned object is stable enough for a fault resolver. A linked list would be a poor substrate for this pattern, and the old rb-tree plus list arrangement carried more structural baggage for lockless readers. The data-structure evolution and the locking evolution are therefore parts of one scaling story, not isolated changes.

Demand Paging

Demand paging is the fundamental reason page faults exist. When a process calls mmap() or when the kernel sets up the initial text/data/bss segments, no physical memory is actually allocated — the kernel merely records the promise of a mapping in the VMA. The first actual access to any page within that range triggers a demand-paging fault, and only then does the kernel allocate a physical folio and install a PTE. This lazy approach is enormously efficient: a process that maps a 1 GB file but only reads the first 4 KB pays for exactly one physical page, not 262,144.

Demand paging is split into two main sub-paths based on whether the VMA is anonymous or file-backed. Anonymous faults are handled by do_anonymous_page() in mm/memory.c, and file-backed faults are handled by do_fault(), which delegates to the filesystem's vm_operations_struct.fault callback. Within file-backed faults, the path further branches for read faults (do_read_fault), copy-on-write faults on private mappings (do_cow_fault), and shared-write faults (do_shared_fault).

The commonest successful fault is a demand-paging fault: a process accesses an address inside a valid VMA, but the PTE is not yet populated. The resolver's job is to instantiate exactly the mapping policy that the VMA promised. For an anonymous private mapping, that usually means a zero-filled page or the shared read-only zero page. For a file mapping, it means asking the filesystem or page cache to supply the backing folio. For a shared write mapping, it may mean ensuring dirty-tracking and writeback metadata are ready.

Anonymous faults are handled by do_anonymous_page(). Its logic is a concise statement of Linux's lazy-allocation model. A pure read fault on anonymous memory can map the globally shared zero page rather than allocating a private folio immediately. A write fault, or a fault on a VMA whose semantics require an actual page, allocates a fresh anonymous folio, zeros it, charges it to the right accounting domains, and then installs a writable PTE. 

/* mm/memory.c — anonymous demand paging, abbreviated */
static vm_fault_t do_anonymous_page(struct vm_fault *vmf)
{
	struct vm_area_struct *vma = vmf->vma;
	unsigned long addr = vmf->address;
	struct folio *folio;
	vm_fault_t ret = 0;
	int nr_pages = 1;
	pte_t entry;

	/* File mapping without ->vm_ops ? */
	if (vma->vm_flags & VM_SHARED)
		return VM_FAULT_SIGBUS;

	if (pte_alloc(vma->vm_mm, vmf->pmd))
		return VM_FAULT_OOM;

	/* Use the zero-page for reads */
	if (!(vmf->flags & FAULT_FLAG_WRITE) &&
			!mm_forbids_zeropage(vma->vm_mm)) {
		entry = pte_mkspecial(pfn_pte(my_zero_pfn(vmf->address),
						vma->vm_page_prot));
		vmf->pte = pte_offset_map_lock(vma->vm_mm, vmf->pmd,
				vmf->address, &vmf->ptl);
		if (!vmf->pte)
			goto unlock;
		if (vmf_pte_changed(vmf)) {
			update_mmu_tlb(vma, vmf->address, vmf->pte);
			goto unlock;
		}
		/* Deliver the page fault to userland, check inside PT lock */
		if (userfaultfd_missing(vma)) {
			pte_unmap_unlock(vmf->pte, vmf->ptl);
			return handle_userfault(vmf, VM_UFFD_MISSING);
		}
		goto setpte;
	}

	/* Allocate our own private page. */
	ret = vmf_anon_prepare(vmf);
	if (ret)
		return ret;
	/* Returns NULL on OOM or ERR_PTR(-EAGAIN) if we must retry the fault */
	folio = alloc_anon_folio(vmf);
	...
	nr_pages = folio_nr_pages(folio);
	addr = ALIGN_DOWN(vmf->address, nr_pages * PAGE_SIZE);

	/*
	 * The memory barrier inside __folio_mark_uptodate makes sure that
	 * preceding stores to the page contents become visible before
	 * the set_pte_at() write.
	 */
	__folio_mark_uptodate(folio);

	entry = folio_mk_pte(folio, vma->vm_page_prot);
	entry = pte_sw_mkyoung(entry);
	if (vma->vm_flags & VM_WRITE)
		entry = pte_mkwrite(pte_mkdirty(entry), vma);
	...
	folio_ref_add(folio, nr_pages - 1);
	add_mm_counter(vma->vm_mm, MM_ANONPAGES, nr_pages);
	folio_add_new_anon_rmap(folio, vma, addr, RMAP_EXCLUSIVE);
	folio_add_lru_vma(folio, vma);
setpte:
	if (vmf_orig_pte_uffd_wp(vmf))
		entry = pte_mkuffd_wp(entry);
	set_ptes(vma->vm_mm, addr, vmf->pte, entry, nr_pages);

	/* No need to invalidate - it was non-present before */
	update_mmu_cache_range(vmf, vma, addr, vmf->pte, nr_pages);
unlock:
	if (vmf->pte)
		pte_unmap_unlock(vmf->pte, vmf->ptl);
	return ret;
}

Compared with the Linux 2.6 do_anonymous_page(), three things have changed and each is characteristic of modern MM.

First, the function no longer allocates a page; it allocates a folio, and not necessarily an order-0 one. alloc_anon_folio() consults the multi-size THP (mTHP) settings under /sys/kernel/mm/transparent_hugepage/ and tries progressively smaller orders until one fits inside the VMA, is naturally aligned, and covers only empty PTEs. That is why addr is re-aligned downward by ALIGN_DOWN() and why the commit is set_ptes(..., nr_pages) rather than set_pte_at(): a single anonymous fault may install 16 PTEs at once. The intermediate sizes matter because a 64 KiB folio is far easier to obtain under fragmentation than a 2 MiB one, while still cutting the fault count and the rmap overhead by an order of magnitude.

Second, both branches check userfaultfd_missing() after taking the page-table lock, with the comment saying so explicitly. Checking earlier would race with a monitor registering the region; checking under the lock guarantees that either the PTE is installed or the monitor is notified, never both and never neither.

Third, the ordering at the end is a memory-model argument, not a stylistic one. __folio_mark_uptodate() carries a barrier so that the zeroing of the folio is visible to every CPU before the PTE that publishes it becomes visible. Without that ordering another CPU could follow the new translation and read stale, uninitialized memory — which for anonymous memory would be an information leak, not merely a bug. Note also RMAP_EXCLUSIVE in the folio_add_new_anon_rmap() call: the folio is born exclusively owned, which is what lets a later write fault reuse it instead of copying.

The shared zero page is a small but elegant optimization. Many processes read freshly reserved anonymous memory before they ever write it. Returning a read-only mapping of a global zero-filled page avoids allocation, zeroing, and anonymous reverse-mapping setup until a write actually occurs. Later, when code wants to know whether a mapping refers to a normal LRU-managed page, helpers such as vm_normal_page() can distinguish special cases like the zero page and return NULL rather than pretending it is an ordinary anonymous page.

File-backed faults are dispatched through do_fault(). This is where the generic MM layer stops pretending that every backing store behaves alike. The VMA may carry a vm_ops->fault() callback supplied by a filesystem, a DAX implementation, a driver, or a special mapping type. The generic code decides whether the fault is a read fault, a copy-on-write fault, or a shared writable fault, and then chooses helpers such as do_read_fault(), do_cow_fault(), or do_shared_fault(). 

/* mm/memory.c — file-backed dispatch, abbreviated */
static vm_fault_t do_fault(struct vm_fault *vmf)
{
	struct vm_area_struct *vma = vmf->vma;
	struct mm_struct *vm_mm = vma->vm_mm;
	vm_fault_t ret;

	/*
	 * The VMA was not fully populated on mmap() or missing VM_DONTEXPAND
	 */
	if (!vma->vm_ops->fault) {
		vmf->pte = pte_offset_map_lock(vmf->vma->vm_mm, vmf->pmd,
					       vmf->address, &vmf->ptl);
		if (unlikely(!vmf->pte))
			ret = VM_FAULT_SIGBUS;
		else {
			/*
			 * Make sure this is not a temporary clearing of pte
			 * by holding ptl and checking again.
			 */
			if (unlikely(pte_none(ptep_get(vmf->pte))))
				ret = VM_FAULT_SIGBUS;
			else
				ret = VM_FAULT_NOPAGE;

			pte_unmap_unlock(vmf->pte, vmf->ptl);
		}
	} else if (!(vmf->flags & FAULT_FLAG_WRITE))
		ret = do_read_fault(vmf);
	else if (!(vma->vm_flags & VM_SHARED))
		ret = do_cow_fault(vmf);
	else
		ret = do_shared_fault(vmf);

	/* preallocated pagetable is unused: free it */
	if (vmf->prealloc_pte) {
		pte_free(vm_mm, vmf->prealloc_pte);
		vmf->prealloc_pte = NULL;
	}
	return ret;
}

The three-way branch is the whole of file-backed policy, and it maps exactly onto Table 9-8's mapping-mode matrix: a read fault needs only the page-cache folio; a private write fault must not touch the page cache at all, so it copies; a shared write fault must dirty the cache folio and arrange for writeback. The !vm_ops->fault arm at the top is the odd one out and is really an error path: a VMA with no fault handler was supposed to have been fully populated at mmap() time (a VM_PFNMAP device mapping, say), so a fault reaching here means a hole, and the answer is SIGBUS — unless the PTE turns out to be present after all, which is the two-CPU race the comment describes.

The prealloc_pte cleanup at the bottom pays for a subtlety earlier in the path. __do_fault() allocates a PTE page before calling vm_ops->fault, because the filesystem may take the folio lock and a later allocation could then deadlock against memcg reclaim waiting on writeback of a folio that this task already holds locked. Allocating up front and freeing the leftover is cheaper than reasoning about that cycle.

Read faults have one further optimization that is easy to miss and accounts for a large share of real workloads' fault reduction: fault-around. Before do_read_fault() does anything else, it asks should_fault_around(), and if the answer is yes it calls the VMA's vm_ops->map_pages() hook — filemap_map_pages() for ordinary filesystems — to install PTEs for every folio already resident in the page cache in a window around the faulting address. Nothing is read from disk; the point is purely to avoid taking a separate fault for each of the neighbouring pages that are demonstrably there already. 

/* mm/memory.c — fault-around */
static unsigned long fault_around_pages __read_mostly =
	65536 >> PAGE_SHIFT;

static inline bool should_fault_around(struct vm_fault *vmf)
{
	/* No ->map_pages?  No way to fault around... */
	if (!vmf->vma->vm_ops->map_pages)
		return false;

	if (uffd_disable_fault_around(vmf->vma))
		return false;

	/* A single page implies no faulting 'around' at all. */
	return fault_around_pages > 1;
}

The default window is 64 KiB, tunable through /sys/kernel/debug/fault_around_bytes (the value is rounded down to a power-of-two page count). do_fault_around() then clamps that window three ways — to the enclosing PTE table, to the VMA, and to power-of-two alignment — so it can never allocate a second page-table page or map anything outside the mapping. Two exclusions in should_fault_around() are worth noting: a VMA registered with userfaultfd is excluded because the monitor must see every page individually, and setting the tunable to a single page disables the mechanism entirely, which is how the behaviour can be measured.

Whatever produced the folio — map_pages() or the slower vm_ops->fault() — the PTE is finally installed by finish_fault(), which re-takes the page-table lock, re-validates against orig_pte, and calls set_pte_range(). This is also the point at which a private file mapping installs its copy rather than the cache folio, and where the cache folio is added to the rmap so that reclaim and truncation can find every mapping of it later.

The interplay between VMA semantics and leaf PTE construction is visible in the last few lines of every successful path. Anonymous and file-backed faults eventually build an entry with helpers such as mk_pte(), pte_mkwrite(), pte_mkdirty(), pte_mkold(), or architecture-specific wrappers, and then commit it with set_pte_at(). That is the software moment when a conceptual VMA policy becomes a hardware-visible translation. The next access to the same address will usually hit a present PTE and never re-enter the high-level fault machinery.

Demand paging is also where the chapter's earlier topology work proves its value. The fault handler never asks “what object is this address inside?” by scanning page tables alone. It first asks the maple tree for the VMA, verifies permissions against vm_flags, and only then decides what concrete page-table entry to materialize. That layering is why Linux can keep page tables sparse and still remember rich policy across enormous virtual holes.

Copy-on-Write

Copy-on-write (CoW) is one of the most elegant optimizations in Unix history, and modern Linux has refined the original concept through several painful lessons — most notably the Dirty CoW vulnerability (CVE-2016-5195), which exposed a race between the write-protection fault path and madvise(MADV_DONTNEED) that had been latent for over a decade. The current implementation in Linux 7.0 is substantially more careful about folio reuse decisions, GUP (get_user_pages) pinning interactions, and the ordering of PTE updates relative to TLB invalidation.

A copy-on-write fault is a protection fault with a very specific meaning: the VMA allows writing, but the current PTE is intentionally read-only because the page is being shared. This is the mechanism that makes fork() practical. Instead of eagerly copying every private page into the child, Linux maps the same physical folios into both parent and child, clears the writable bit, and waits. Only a later write fault forces a real copy.

The principal handler is do_wp_page() in mm/memory.c. Its job is to decide whether the faulted folio can be reused or must be copied. If the mapping is now effectively exclusive, the kernel can often make the PTE writable again without allocating new memory. If other mappings still reference the folio, Linux allocates a replacement, copies the contents, and retargets the faulting PTE to the new private folio. 

/* mm/memory.c — write-protect fault handling, abbreviated */
static vm_fault_t do_wp_page(struct vm_fault *vmf)
	__releases(vmf->ptl)
{
	const bool unshare = vmf->flags & FAULT_FLAG_UNSHARE;
	struct vm_area_struct *vma = vmf->vma;
	struct folio *folio = NULL;

	if (likely(!unshare)) {
		if (userfaultfd_pte_wp(vma, ptep_get(vmf->pte))) {
			if (!userfaultfd_wp_async(vma)) {
				pte_unmap_unlock(vmf->pte, vmf->ptl);
				return handle_userfault(vmf, VM_UFFD_WP);
			}
			...
		}

		/*
		 * Userfaultfd write-protect can defer flushes. Ensure the TLB
		 * is flushed in this case before copying.
		 */
		if (unlikely(userfaultfd_wp(vmf->vma) &&
			     mm_tlb_flush_pending(vmf->vma->vm_mm)))
			flush_tlb_page(vmf->vma, vmf->address);
	}

	vmf->page = vm_normal_page(vma, vmf->address, vmf->orig_pte);

	if (vmf->page)
		folio = page_folio(vmf->page);

	/*
	 * Shared mapping: we are guaranteed to have VM_WRITE and
	 * FAULT_FLAG_WRITE set at this point.
	 */
	if (vma->vm_flags & (VM_SHARED | VM_MAYSHARE)) {
		if (!vmf->page || is_fsdax_page(vmf->page)) {
			vmf->page = NULL;
			return wp_pfn_shared(vmf);
		}
		return wp_page_shared(vmf, folio);
	}

	/*
	 * Private mapping: create an exclusive anonymous page copy if reuse
	 * is impossible. We might miss VM_WRITE for FOLL_FORCE handling.
	 *
	 * If we encounter a page that is marked exclusive, we must reuse
	 * the page without further checks.
	 */
	if (folio && folio_test_anon(folio) &&
	    (PageAnonExclusive(vmf->page) || wp_can_reuse_anon_folio(folio, vma))) {
		if (!PageAnonExclusive(vmf->page))
			SetPageAnonExclusive(vmf->page);
		if (unlikely(unshare)) {
			pte_unmap_unlock(vmf->pte, vmf->ptl);
			return 0;
		}
		wp_page_reuse(vmf, folio);
		return 0;
	}
	/*
	 * Ok, we need to copy. Oh, well..
	 */
	if (folio)
		folio_get(folio);

	pte_unmap_unlock(vmf->pte, vmf->ptl);
	return wp_page_copy(vmf);
}

The function is bigger than it was in Linux 2.6 because a write-protected PTE has stopped meaning only one thing. It is now the encoding for at least four distinct situations, and do_wp_page() separates them before it reaches the copy decision: a userfaultfd write-protected page, a shared mapping whose PTE was cleared so the filesystem could be told about the first write, a raw PFN or DAX mapping with no struct page at all, and the classic post-fork() private anonymous page. Only the last is copy-on-write in the textbook sense.

The reuse decision now hinges on a single per-page bit, PageAnonExclusive, and the comment above the test states the rule flatly: an exclusive page is reused without further checks. That bit is set when an anonymous folio is first allocated (the RMAP_EXCLUSIVE seen earlier), cleared when fork() shares the page, and re-established here once the kernel has proved sole ownership. Making exclusivity an explicit, maintained property rather than something re-derived from reference counts on every fault is the single most important correction the CoW path has received since Linux 2.6, and it is what closes the class of bugs Dirty CoW belonged to. 

/* mm/memory.c — can we avoid the copy? */
static bool wp_can_reuse_anon_folio(struct folio *folio,
				    struct vm_area_struct *vma)
{
	if (IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_TRANSPARENT_HUGEPAGE) && folio_test_large(folio))
		return __wp_can_reuse_large_anon_folio(folio, vma);

	/*
	 * We have to verify under folio lock: these early checks are
	 * just an optimization to avoid locking the folio and freeing
	 * the swapcache if there is little hope that we can reuse.
	 *
	 * KSM doesn't necessarily raise the folio refcount.
	 */
	if (folio_test_ksm(folio) || folio_ref_count(folio) > 3)
		return false;
	if (!folio_test_lru(folio))
		/*
		 * We cannot easily detect+handle references from
		 * remote LRU caches or references to LRU folios.
		 */
		lru_add_drain();
	if (folio_ref_count(folio) > 1 + folio_test_swapcache(folio))
		return false;
	if (!folio_trylock(folio))
		return false;
	if (folio_test_swapcache(folio))
		folio_free_swap(folio);
	if (folio_test_ksm(folio) || folio_ref_count(folio) != 1) {
		folio_unlock(folio);
		return false;
	}
	/*
	 * Ok, we've got the only folio reference from our mapping
	 * and the folio is locked, it's dark out, and we're wearing
	 * sunglasses. Hit it.
	 */
	folio_move_anon_rmap(folio, vma);
	folio_unlock(folio);
	return true;
}

This is the fallback used only when the exclusive bit is not set, and the shape of it is instructive. Every early return is an optimization; the only authoritative test is the final folio_ref_count(folio) != 1 performed under the folio lock, exactly as the first comment says. Reference counts alone are not a safe basis for the decision because they can be raised transiently by LRU batching (hence the lru_add_drain()), by the swap cache (hence the folio_free_swap()), and by GUP pins that must never be silently detached from the page the pinner believes it holds. A KSM folio is refused outright, since KSM deduplicates identical pages across unrelated processes without raising the count.

Two further points about the excerpt above. folio_move_anon_rmap() at the end is not bookkeeping trivia: the folio may still be attached to the parent's anon_vma, and reusing it means re-parenting it to this VMA's. And FAULT_FLAG_UNSHARE takes an early exit — once exclusivity is established, an unshare request is complete, because it never wanted write access in the first place.

wp_page_copy() is the expensive branch. It allocates a new folio, copies the old contents into it, updates reverse-mapping metadata, builds a writable PTE for the replacement, and finally drops the shared read-only mapping. The old folio remains mapped in the sibling processes that still share it. The faulting task receives a private writable copy, which preserves the illusion that it always owned independent writable memory.

wp_page_reuse() is the happy fast path. If the folio is already exclusive in the relevant sense, copying would be wasted work. Linux instead upgrades permissions in place and accounts the fault as a write-protect resolution rather than a full duplication. Modern code uses folio-level helpers such as folio_ref_count() and folio_mapcount() to reason about exclusivity. The conditions are subtle because GUP pins, large folios, and temporary references all matter, but the principle is simple: copy only when real sharing remains.

Kernel Samepage Merging (KSM) adds another wrinkle. A page that originated as anonymous private memory may later have been merged with an identical peer page and marked specially. A write-protect fault on such a page must break the sharing relationship correctly, not merely flip a writable bit. That is why CoW code is careful about page identity, reverse mapping, and special sharing states rather than assuming that every anonymous read-only page came only from fork().

CoW also explains why Chapter 8's folio accounting matters here. Allocating the replacement folio is just the visible tip. The path interacts with memcg charging, NUMA placement, LRU state, dirty tracking, and TLB invalidation. Yet from user space the semantics remain deceptively simple: a store to private memory succeeds and later reads see the new value. The page-fault path hides the machinery required to preserve that illusion efficiently.

Huge Page Faults

Chapter 2 introduced the hardware support for large pages (2 MB on x86_64, 2 MB or 1 GB on ARM64 with contiguous hints). Huge page faults are the demand-paging mechanism that puts those large mappings into practice. Instead of populating a single 4 KB PTE, the fault handler can install a mapping at the PMD level (2 MB) or even the PUD level (1 GB for hugetlbfs), covering an entire large page with a single page-table entry. The payoff is dramatic: a 2 MB THP requires only one TLB entry instead of 512, and the TLB miss rate for sequential or large-working-set access patterns drops correspondingly.

Linux provides two distinct huge-page mechanisms, and both have their own fault paths. Transparent Huge Pages (THP) operate silently — the kernel decides at fault time whether to attempt a huge-page allocation, and falls back to base pages if memory is fragmented. Hugetlbfs pages are explicitly reserved by the administrator and allocated from a separate pool; their faults follow a different code path through hugetlb_fault(), dispatched from handle_mm_fault() before the generic page-table walk even begins. Table 9-12, later in this section, summarizes the key differences.

Not all faults populate base-page PTEs. When Transparent Huge Pages (THP) are enabled and policy permits, Linux may satisfy an anonymous fault with a PMD-sized mapping instead. The main anonymous THP allocation path uses helpers such as do_huge_pmd_anonymous_page() and create_huge_pmd(). If allocation succeeds, a single PMD entry can cover a large contiguous range, reducing TLB pressure and amortizing fault overhead. 

/* mm/huge_memory.c — THP fault handling, abbreviated */
vm_fault_t do_huge_pmd_anonymous_page(struct vm_fault *vmf)
{
	struct vm_area_struct *vma = vmf->vma;
	unsigned long haddr = vmf->address & HPAGE_PMD_MASK;
	vm_fault_t ret;

	if (!thp_vma_suitable_order(vma, haddr, PMD_ORDER))
		return VM_FAULT_FALLBACK;
	ret = vmf_anon_prepare(vmf);
	if (ret)
		return ret;
	khugepaged_enter_vma(vma, vma->vm_flags);

	if (!(vmf->flags & FAULT_FLAG_WRITE) &&
			!mm_forbids_zeropage(vma->vm_mm) &&
			transparent_hugepage_use_zero_page()) {
		pgtable_t pgtable;
		struct folio *zero_folio;

		pgtable = pte_alloc_one(vma->vm_mm);
		if (unlikely(!pgtable))
			return VM_FAULT_OOM;
		zero_folio = mm_get_huge_zero_folio(vma->vm_mm);
		if (unlikely(!zero_folio)) {
			pte_free(vma->vm_mm, pgtable);
			count_vm_event(THP_FAULT_FALLBACK);
			return VM_FAULT_FALLBACK;
		}
		vmf->ptl = pmd_lock(vma->vm_mm, vmf->pmd);
		...
				set_huge_zero_folio(pgtable, vma->vm_mm, vma,
						   haddr, vmf->pmd, zero_folio);
				update_mmu_cache_pmd(vma, vmf->address, vmf->pmd);
			spin_unlock(vmf->ptl);
		return ret;
	}

	return __do_huge_pmd_anonymous_page(vmf);
}

Three things in that function generalize the base-page story rather than repeating it. thp_vma_suitable_order() is the first gate and it is purely geometric: a PMD mapping must be 2 MiB aligned and lie wholly inside the VMA, so a huge fault is impossible near either end of a small mapping regardless of how much memory is free. khugepaged_enter_vma() registers the VMA with the background collapse thread, which is the acknowledgement that a fault-time failure is not final — if the huge folio cannot be had now, khugepaged may assemble one later from the base pages this fault is about to install. And the read-fault branch has a huge counterpart to the shared zero page: mm_get_huge_zero_folio() returns a per-mm reference to a global 2 MiB zero folio, so a process that reads a gigabyte of freshly mapped anonymous memory without writing it consumes 512 PMD entries and no data pages at all.

The two VM_FAULT_FALLBACK returns are the whole design in miniature. Neither is an error: they mean “not at this granularity”, and __handle_mm_fault() responds by descending one more level and letting handle_pte_fault() install base pages instead. That is why THP can be enabled by default without a correctness argument — the fallback is always available, and it is also why the thp_fault_fallback counter in /proc/vmstat is the number to watch when THP fails to deliver on a fragmented system.

Table 9-12. Transparent Huge Pages versus hugetlbfs
Property Transparent Huge Pages hugetlbfs
Fault entrycreate_huge_pmd() from __handle_mm_fault(), or alloc_anon_folio() for sub-PMD ordershugetlb_fault(), dispatched from handle_mm_fault() before the generic walk begins
Memory sourceThe ordinary buddy allocator, at the requested orderA pre-reserved pool sized through nr_hugepages
Failure behaviourVM_FAULT_FALLBACK — silently uses base pagesSIGBUS — the reservation was the contract
SizesPMD size, plus any enabled smaller order (mTHP)Every size the hardware supports, including PUD size (1 GiB)
ReclaimableYes — can be split and swappedNo — pinned in the pool for its lifetime
Opt-inSystem-wide policy plus madvise(MADV_HUGEPAGE)Explicit: a hugetlbfs mount or MAP_HUGETLB
Background promotionkhugepaged collapses base pages after the factNone
VMA markerNone — an ordinary anonymous or shmem VMAVM_HUGETLB, with its own vm_operations_struct

The key design point is that THP faults are opportunistic. Failure to allocate a huge folio is not usually fatal. The handler can return VM_FAULT_FALLBACK or equivalent state and let the generic code instantiate ordinary base pages instead. This preserves correctness even under fragmentation, memcg pressure, or policy constraints. THP therefore improves the common case without changing the VMA-level contract exposed to user space.

Write-protect faults on huge PMDs have their own helper, wp_huge_pmd(), because the kernel must decide whether to preserve the huge mapping, split it, or copy at large granularity. Those decisions are shaped by mapcount, sharing, NUMA migration, and the cost of fragmentation. The same copy-on-write principles still apply, but the bookkeeping is larger and the fallback options include splitting the PMD mapping into base PTEs when necessary.

Hugetlbfs mappings are a separate world. They are not just THP with a different allocator. They use reserved huge pages, distinct VMA flags, and dedicated fault handling such as hugetlb_fault(). Where THP is best-effort and transparent, hugetlb mappings are explicit and policy-heavy. The fault path reflects that difference: a hugetlb VMA takes the specialized hugetlb code from the start, not the anonymous or generic file-backed base-page logic. The pool those faults draw from — how it is sized, how mmap()-time reservations are counted, and where gigantic pages come from — is the subject of Chapter 8, Section 4.4.

The boundary between base-page, THP, and hugetlb fault handling is a good reminder that a VMA describes policy, not page-table granularity. The same logical address range can be backed by different leaf sizes over time, and the fault handlers are free to choose the best translation shape that still honors the mapping's contract.

Fault Return Codes

Every leaf handler in the page-fault path must communicate its outcome back to the architecture layer, and a simple success/failure boolean is not enough. The fault handler might have resolved the translation but also discovered that I/O was required (a major fault for accounting purposes). It might need the caller to retry the entire operation because a lock was dropped and the VMA topology changed. It might need to tell the architecture code to deliver SIGBUS rather than SIGSEGV because the address is valid but the backing store cannot supply data (e.g., a file was truncated). All of this information is encoded in the vm_fault_t bitmask — a typedef unsigned int where individual bits carry orthogonal meanings that can be combined.

The generic fault path returns a vm_fault_t bitmask, not a single integer status. That bitmask lets the resolver communicate both success and important side conditions to the architecture layer. The arch code then decides whether to retry, return to user mode, or deliver a signal such as SIGSEGV or SIGBUS. Reading these bits correctly is essential, because the fault handler often resolves the page-table problem and still needs the caller to perform follow-up work such as retries or synchronous fault completion.

Table 9-13. Important VM_FAULT_* Return Codes
Return code Meaning Typical architecture response
0 (minor fault implied)The fault was handled without a major-fault indication.Resume execution normally.
VM_FAULT_MAJORThe fault required heavyweight work such as I/O and should count as major.Resume execution, but account a major fault.
VM_FAULT_OOMMemory allocation failed in a non-droppable fault path.Usually invoke OOM handling or deliver a fatal outcome.
VM_FAULT_SIGBUSThe access was within a VMA but cannot be satisfied, often due to file truncation or unsupported offset.Deliver SIGBUS.
VM_FAULT_RETRYThe handler dropped a lock or otherwise needs the caller to retry the fault.Re-enter the fault path with updated flags.
VM_FAULT_COMPLETEDThe handler finished all required work and the caller must not touch the VMA state again.Return without the usual retry or unlock sequence.
VM_FAULT_NEEDDSYNCA synchronous faulting model such as DAX requires an explicit sync step before the access can proceed.Propagate the special condition to the caller.
VM_FAULT_HWPOISONThe target page is hardware-poisoned (see Memory Failure and HWPoison).Deliver the architecture-appropriate fatal signal, often SIGBUS.
VM_FAULT_HWPOISON_LARGEAs above, but for a huge page. The order is encoded separately in VM_FAULT_HINDEX_MASK so the signal can report how much memory was lost.Deliver SIGBUS with BUS_MCEERR_AR and the affected size.
VM_FAULT_SIGSEGVThe fault is invalid in a way MM detected rather than the architecture — for example a request that failed sanitize_fault_flags().Deliver SIGSEGV.
VM_FAULT_NOPAGEThe handler installed the PTE itself and there is no vmf->page to return. The normal outcome of an anonymous fault.Resume execution normally.
VM_FAULT_LOCKEDA filesystem page_mkwrite handler is returning with the folio locked, so the caller must unlock it.Internal to MM; never seen by architecture code.
VM_FAULT_FALLBACKThe huge-page path declined; retry at a smaller granularity.Internal to MM; consumed by __handle_mm_fault().
VM_FAULT_DONE_COWA private file mapping already completed its copy, so the caller must not also install the cache folio.Internal to MM.

The last four rows above never leave mm/. That is worth stating because the bitmask is often described as “the MM-to-architecture protocol”, when in fact it does double duty as the internal protocol between the layers of the fault handler itself. The helper VM_FAULT_ERROR collects the subset that architecture code must turn into a signal or OOM kill — OOM, SIGBUS, SIGSEGV, HWPOISON, HWPOISON_LARGE, and FALLBACK — and everything outside that mask is either success or an instruction to the caller.

Two result bits deserve special emphasis. VM_FAULT_RETRY says the fault is not resolved yet, but it might succeed if re-entered with the correct locks and flags. VM_FAULT_COMPLETED is stronger: it says the callee already performed the relevant follow-up, so the caller must not re-touch transient VMA state or apply the standard completion logic again. These distinctions exist because some subpaths have to drop locks for I/O, userfaultfd handoff, or filesystem work and then reestablish state in a controlled way.

By the end of the generic fault path, architecture-specific details have largely disappeared. The x86 error code or ARM64 syndrome is now expressed as a VMA lookup, a fault flag set, a page-table walk, and one of the generic result combinations above. That transformation is exactly why Linux can keep most demand-paging policy in portable code while still supporting wildly different exception entry mechanisms.

Kernel-Mode Faults and Exception Fixups

Everything described so far in this section concerns user-mode page faults — faults triggered by user-space code accessing addresses within the process's VMA topology. But the CPU can also fault while executing kernel code, and these kernel-mode faults follow an entirely different resolution path that never enters handle_mm_fault(). This is why faults on kernel addresses need separate treatment: vmalloc regions, kernel stacks, and module mappings all live in kernel virtual address space, and faults on those addresses must be resolved (or reported as bugs) through architecture-specific mechanisms rather than the generic VMA-based fault handler.

There are three distinct categories of kernel-mode fault:

Vmalloc Page-Table Synchronization

When the kernel allocates memory through vmalloc() (described in Chapter 8), it updates the reference kernel page tables — the init_mm PGD — but does not immediately propagate those changes into every process's page-table hierarchy. On x86-32, where each process carries a separate copy of the kernel portion of the page tables, a subsequent access to a newly mapped vmalloc address can trigger a page fault because the process's kernel PGD/PMD/PTE entries are stale. The handler resolves this by synchronizing the faulting entry from the reference tables: 

/* arch/x86/mm/fault.c — vmalloc fault synchronization */
static noinline int vmalloc_fault(unsigned long address)
{
	unsigned long pgd_paddr;
	pmd_t *pmd_k;
	pte_t *pte_k;

	/* Make sure we are in vmalloc area: */
	if (!(address >= VMALLOC_START && address < VMALLOC_END))
		return -1;

	/*
	 * Synchronize this task's top level page-table
	 * with the 'reference' page table.
	 * Do _not_ use "current" here. We might be inside
	 * an interrupt in the middle of a task switch..
	 */
	pgd_paddr = read_cr3_pa();
	pmd_k = vmalloc_sync_one(__va(pgd_paddr), address);
	if (!pmd_k)
		return -1;

	if (pmd_leaf(*pmd_k))
		return 0;

	pte_k = pte_offset_kernel(pmd_k, address);
	if (!pte_present(*pte_k))
		return -1;

	return 0;
}

On x86-64, this race does not exist because all processes share the same top-level kernel PGD entries (the upper half of PML4/PML5). The kernel comment says explicitly: "On x86-64 the race does not exist as the vmalloc mappings don't need to be synchronized there." However, the vmalloc_fault() function is still compiled and called on 32-bit x86 kernels, which remain supported. ARM64 similarly shares kernel page-table entries across all processes through the TTBR1 address space, so this synchronization issue is x86-32–specific.

Exception Table Fixups (copy_to/from_user)

When kernel code needs to access user-space memory — through helpers like copy_to_user(), copy_from_user(), get_user(), or put_user() — the access might fault because the user page is not mapped, has been swapped out, or the user pointer is simply invalid. The kernel cannot just crash on these faults; it must recover gracefully and return an error code (-EFAULT) to the caller.

Linux handles this through the exception table mechanism. Each potentially-faulting instruction in a copy_*_user() sequence has a corresponding entry in a compile-time exception table (__ex_table) that maps the faulting instruction's address to a fixup routine. When a kernel-mode fault occurs and the address is not in the vmalloc range, the handler calls fixup_exception(), which searches the exception table for a matching entry. If found, the fixup routine adjusts the instruction pointer and registers so that execution resumes at an error-recovery path instead of continuing the faulting instruction: 

/* arch/x86/mm/fault.c — kernel fault recovery */
static void
kernelmode_fixup_or_oops(struct pt_regs *regs, unsigned long error_code,
			 unsigned long address, int signal, int si_code,
			 u32 pkey)
{
	WARN_ON_ONCE(user_mode(regs));

	/* Are we prepared to handle this kernel fault? */
	if (fixup_exception(regs, X86_TRAP_PF, error_code, address))
		return;

	/*
	 * AMD erratum #91 manifests as a spurious page fault on a PREFETCH
	 * instruction.
	 */
	if (is_prefetch(regs, error_code, address))
		return;

	page_fault_oops(regs, error_code, address);
}
/* arch/x86/include/asm/extable.h */
struct exception_table_entry {
	int insn, fixup, data;
};

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/extable.h */
struct exception_table_entry
{
	int insn, fixup;
	short type, data;
};

The int fields are worth a comment, because they look like a bug and are not. Both architectures define ARCH_HAS_RELATIVE_EXTABLE, so insn and fixup are stored as signed 32-bit offsets from the address of the field itself rather than as absolute addresses. That halves the size of a table that has tens of thousands of entries in a typical kernel, and — more importantly — makes the table position-independent, so it survives KASLR relocation and module loading without any runtime patching. The table is emitted by the _ASM_EXTABLE family of assembler macros, placed in its own __ex_table section, and sorted at build time by scripts/sorttable so that search_exception_tables() can binary-search it.

The two architectures differ in what a fixup is. On x86, data encodes a handler type and the fixup target is code the compiler emitted out of line; on ARM64 the type field selects one of a small set of handlers named in arch/arm64/include/asm/asm-extable.h — EX_TYPE_UACCESS_ERR_ZERO, EX_TYPE_KACCESS_ERR_ZERO, EX_TYPE_UACCESS_CPY, EX_TYPE_LOAD_UNALIGNED_ZEROPAD, and EX_TYPE_BPF — with data naming the registers to set to the error code and to zero. The ARM64 form is thus mostly table-driven rather than out-of-line code, which is why get_user() on ARM64 needs no fixup stub of its own.

The exception table also serves one purpose outside copy_*_user() that is easy to overlook. get_mmap_lock_carefully(), described in Section 6.1, consults search_exception_tables() before it is willing to sleep on mmap_lock for a kernel-mode fault. If the faulting instruction has no fixup entry, the fault is a kernel bug and there is no point blocking; the handler bails out immediately so the oops carries an accurate stack rather than one taken after a sleep. The table is therefore not only a recovery mechanism but a way for the fault handler to ask “did the author of this instruction anticipate a fault here?”

This is why copy_from_user() can safely handle an invalid user pointer without crashing the kernel — the exception table turns what would be a kernel panic into a controlled error return. The mechanism is entirely transparent to the caller: copy_from_user() returns the number of bytes not copied (nonzero on failure), and the caller converts that into -EFAULT.

Kernel Oops — Unrecoverable Faults

If a kernel-mode fault occurs at an address that is neither a vmalloc synchronization case nor covered by an exception table entry, the fault is a genuine kernel bug — a null pointer dereference, a use-after-free, an out-of-bounds access, or a similar error. The handler calls page_fault_oops(), which prints a diagnostic dump (registers, stack trace, code bytes around the faulting instruction) and either kills the current task or panics the entire system, depending on whether the fault occurred in an interruptible context and the panic_on_oops sysctl setting.

These three cases — vmalloc sync, exception fixup, and oops — are handled entirely within the architecture-specific fault entry code. They never reach the generic handle_mm_fault() path because they operate on kernel virtual addresses, not user VMAs. This is the fundamental architectural split in the page-fault handler: user-mode faults go through VMA lookup and the generic MM layer; kernel-mode faults are resolved (or reported) by architecture code before the generic layer is ever consulted.

userfaultfd

Why Move Fault Resolution Into User Space

The classic page-fault model is purely kernel-driven: if a fault is valid, the kernel resolves it — the application never knows a fault occurred at all. userfaultfd deliberately breaks that assumption. It lets user space register virtual address ranges whose missing, write-protect, or minor faults should be delivered to a user-managed handler thread rather than being resolved by the kernel's default demand-paging or copy-on-write paths.

The mechanism was originally developed by Andrea Arcangeli at Red Hat for QEMU/KVM live migration, where it solved a fundamental problem: how to let a virtual machine run on the destination host before all of its memory has arrived over the network. With userfaultfd, QEMU registers the VM's guest RAM range. When the guest touches a page that hasn't been migrated yet, the fault is caught and forwarded to a migration thread, which fetches that specific page from the source host, installs it via UFFDIO_COPY, and lets the guest continue. This post-copy pattern dramatically reduces migration downtime compared to pre-copy approaches.

Since its introduction in Linux 4.3 (2015), userfaultfd has grown far beyond live migration. It is now used by:

  • CRIU (Checkpoint/Restore In Userspace) — restores process memory lazily, populating pages only when accessed rather than loading the entire checkpoint image upfront.
  • Garbage collectors — Java (Shenandoah, ZGC) and Go GC use write-protect mode to track which pages have been mutated during a collection cycle, enabling concurrent compaction without stop-the-world pauses.
  • Language runtimes and DSM systems — distributed shared memory frameworks use userfaultfd to intercept first access to a page and fetch it from a remote node on demand.
  • Userspace file systems and pagers — FUSE-like systems that want to supply page content from user space without implementing a full kernel filesystem.
  • Testing and fuzzing — the UFFDIO_POISON command allows deliberate injection of fault-on-access entries for controlled error testing.

Creating a Descriptor and Negotiating Features

The interface is accessed through either the userfaultfd(2) system call or the /dev/userfaultfd misc device (introduced in Linux 6.1 for better container compatibility). Both return a file descriptor that the caller negotiates with UFFDIO_API, registers address ranges on with UFFDIO_REGISTER, and reads fault events from via read(2) or poll(2).

The security story is more precise than it is usually described, and the precision matters. An unprivileged userfaultfd is dangerous because it lets user space stall the kernel arbitrarily long inside a fault, which converts many hard-to-win use-after-free races into reliable ones: the attacker arranges for a kernel path to touch a registered page, then simply never answers. But the exposure only exists when the userfaultfd is allowed to intercept faults taken by the kernel on the process’s behalf — a copy_from_user() in the middle of a system call, say. The kernel therefore gates exactly that, and nothing more: 

/* fs/userfaultfd.c */
static inline bool userfaultfd_syscall_allowed(int flags)
{
	/* Userspace-only page faults are always allowed */
	if (flags & UFFD_USER_MODE_ONLY)
		return true;

	/*
	 * The user is requesting a userfaultfd which can handle kernel faults.
	 * Privileged users are always allowed to do this.
	 */
	if (capable(CAP_SYS_PTRACE))
		return true;

	/* Otherwise, access to kernel fault handling is sysctl controlled. */
	return sysctl_unprivileged_userfaultfd;
}

SYSCALL_DEFINE1(userfaultfd, int, flags)
{
	if (!userfaultfd_syscall_allowed(flags))
		return -EPERM;

	return new_userfaultfd(flags);
}

So a descriptor created with UFFD_USER_MODE_ONLY is available to any process on any kernel, regardless of the sysctl, and this is the mode every ordinary application — a garbage collector, a checkpoint/restore tool, a distributed shared-memory runtime — actually wants. sysctl_unprivileged_userfaultfd defaults to 0 in fs/userfaultfd.c, so on a stock kernel an unprivileged process cannot create a descriptor that traps kernel-mode faults, but CAP_SYS_PTRACE bypasses the check on the reasoning that a process which can already ptrace can already stall its target. The /dev/userfaultfd device offers the same thing to containers through a file permission rather than a capability: opening it and issuing USERFAULTFD_IOC_NEW calls the same new_userfaultfd(), so access can be delegated with ordinary device ownership instead of loosening a system-wide sysctl. 

/* include/uapi/linux/userfaultfd.h */
#define _UFFDIO_REGISTER		(0x00)
#define _UFFDIO_UNREGISTER		(0x01)
#define _UFFDIO_WAKE			(0x02)
#define _UFFDIO_COPY			(0x03)
#define _UFFDIO_ZEROPAGE		(0x04)
#define _UFFDIO_MOVE			(0x05)
#define _UFFDIO_WRITEPROTECT		(0x06)
#define _UFFDIO_CONTINUE		(0x07)
#define _UFFDIO_POISON			(0x08)
#define _UFFDIO_API			(0x3F)

#define UFFD_API_IOCTLS				\
	((__u64)1 << _UFFDIO_REGISTER |		\
	 (__u64)1 << _UFFDIO_UNREGISTER |	\
	 (__u64)1 << _UFFDIO_API)
#define UFFD_API_RANGE_IOCTLS			\
	((__u64)1 << _UFFDIO_WAKE |		\
	 (__u64)1 << _UFFDIO_COPY |		\
	 (__u64)1 << _UFFDIO_ZEROPAGE |		\
	 (__u64)1 << _UFFDIO_MOVE |		\
	 (__u64)1 << _UFFDIO_WRITEPROTECT |	\
	 (__u64)1 << _UFFDIO_CONTINUE |		\
	 (__u64)1 << _UFFDIO_POISON)

The small integers are the interesting part, not the _IOWR() wrappers built from them. Because every command number is below 64, the kernel can answer a capability query with a single __u64 bitmask, and that is exactly what it does — twice. UFFDIO_API writes UFFD_API_IOCTLS into uffdio_api.ioctls to report which commands the descriptor as a whole supports, and UFFDIO_REGISTER writes UFFD_API_RANGE_IOCTLS (or the reduced UFFD_API_RANGE_IOCTLS_BASIC) into uffdio_register.ioctls to report which commands are legal on the range just registered. A manager therefore never has to guess whether UFFDIO_CONTINUE is available for a particular mapping: it registers the range and reads the answer back out of the same structure it passed in. Both fields are documented as being at the end of their structures because the kernel’s copy_from_user() deliberately stops short of them. 

/* include/uapi/linux/userfaultfd.h — comments omitted */
#define UFFD_API ((__u64)0xAA)

struct uffdio_api {
	/* userland asks for an API number and the features to enable */
	__u64 api;
#define UFFD_FEATURE_PAGEFAULT_FLAG_WP		(1<<0)
#define UFFD_FEATURE_EVENT_FORK			(1<<1)
#define UFFD_FEATURE_EVENT_REMAP		(1<<2)
#define UFFD_FEATURE_EVENT_REMOVE		(1<<3)
#define UFFD_FEATURE_MISSING_HUGETLBFS		(1<<4)
#define UFFD_FEATURE_MISSING_SHMEM		(1<<5)
#define UFFD_FEATURE_EVENT_UNMAP		(1<<6)
#define UFFD_FEATURE_SIGBUS			(1<<7)
#define UFFD_FEATURE_THREAD_ID			(1<<8)
#define UFFD_FEATURE_MINOR_HUGETLBFS		(1<<9)
#define UFFD_FEATURE_MINOR_SHMEM		(1<<10)
#define UFFD_FEATURE_EXACT_ADDRESS		(1<<11)
#define UFFD_FEATURE_WP_HUGETLBFS_SHMEM		(1<<12)
#define UFFD_FEATURE_WP_UNPOPULATED		(1<<13)
#define UFFD_FEATURE_POISON			(1<<14)
#define UFFD_FEATURE_WP_ASYNC			(1<<15)
#define UFFD_FEATURE_MOVE			(1<<16)
	__u64 features;

	__u64 ioctls;
};

The seventeen feature bits are the closest thing this chapter has to a changelog. Reading them in numeric order is reading the history of the interface: write-protect mode at bit 0, the four non-cooperative events at bits 1–3 and 6, shmem and hugetlbfs backing for missing faults at bits 4–5, minor faults at bits 9–10, and the most recent additions — UFFD_FEATURE_POISON, UFFD_FEATURE_WP_ASYNC, and UFFD_FEATURE_MOVE — at the top. The kernel accepts exactly this set, collected as UFFD_API_FEATURES.

The handshake is two-directional, and the direction that is easy to miss is the one that makes the interface durable across kernel versions. User space writes the features it wants into uffdio_api.features; if the running kernel does not implement one of them, UFFDIO_API fails outright rather than silently proceeding with less than was asked for. But a caller may instead pass zero, in which case the kernel overwrites the field with everything it supports and the call succeeds. The same ioctl therefore serves both as a demand — “fail unless you can do all of this” — and as a probe — “tell me what you can do” — which is why a manager written against a recent kernel can run unmodified on an older one and fall back deliberately instead of crashing. The header spells this out for UFFD_FEATURE_MISSING_HUGETLBFS and notes that the rest behave the same way.

Two feature bits change the shape of the interface rather than extending it, and both are worth singling out because they are routinely misread as minor options. UFFD_FEATURE_SIGBUS suppresses event delivery altogether: a fault on a registered range sends SIGBUS to the faulting thread instead of queueing a message and blocking. There is no manager and no round trip, so the registration becomes a way to make a range trap on first touch — what a runtime wants when it is enforcing an invariant rather than implementing a pager. UFFD_FEATURE_EXACT_ADDRESS changes what a message means: without it the reported address is masked down to the base of the faulting page; with it the kernel reports vmf->real_address, the byte the instruction actually touched. That distinction is invisible to a pager, which only needs to know which page to fill, and essential to a debugger or bounds-checking runtime, which needs to know exactly where the access landed.

Only after the handshake may user space register address ranges. UFFDIO_REGISTER takes a range and a mode mask built from UFFDIO_REGISTER_MODE_MISSING, UFFDIO_REGISTER_MODE_WP, and UFFDIO_REGISTER_MODE_MINOR, and the three modes intercept three genuinely different moments in a page’s life. Missing catches the fault on a page that has no contents yet — the post-copy migration case. Write-protect catches a write to a page that is present and readable — the dirty-tracking case. Minor catches a fault where the page cache already holds the data but this address space has no page-table entry for it — the case where a second mapping of shared memory needs to be told about a page that already exists.

Registration is not stored in a side table. It is recorded directly in the VMA, as the flags VM_UFFD_MISSING, VM_UFFD_WP, and VM_UFFD_MINOR, with a pointer to the context in vma->vm_userfaultfd_ctx. That is why registering a subrange of an existing mapping splits the VMA, why an unregistered and a registered VMA can never merge, and why the fault path can test for userfaultfd with nothing more than a flag test on a structure it has already loaded. The predicates in include/linux/userfaultfd_k.h are as thin as that suggests: userfaultfd_missing(), userfaultfd_wp(), and userfaultfd_minor() each return a single vm_flags bit, and userfaultfd_armed() tests all three at once against __VM_UFFD_FLAGS.

Two smaller pieces of the handshake are easy to overlook and both have visible consequences. The flags accepted by userfaultfd(2) are constrained to UFFD_SHARED_FCNTL_FLAGS, which is O_CLOEXEC | O_NONBLOCK, plus UFFD_USER_MODE_ONLY; a BUILD_BUG_ON() asserts the two sets cannot collide. And the kernel records the completion of the handshake in the feature word itself, using a bit that user space can never set. 

/* fs/userfaultfd.c */
/* internal indication that UFFD_API ioctl was successfully executed */
#define UFFD_FEATURE_INITIALIZED		(1u << 31)

static bool userfaultfd_is_initialized(struct userfaultfd_ctx *ctx)
{
	return ctx->features & UFFD_FEATURE_INITIALIZED;
}

Bit 31 is far above the seventeen public bits and is never part of UFFD_API_FEATURES, so it cannot be requested; UFFDIO_API sets it as a side effect of succeeding. Every other ioctl then begins with if (cmd != UFFDIO_API && !userfaultfd_is_initialized(ctx)), and poll() and read() refuse an uninitialised descriptor as well. This is why the handshake is not merely conventional but enforced: a descriptor before UFFDIO_API is inert, and the enforcement costs one bit test because the flag lives in a word the code has already loaded.

Not every mapping may be registered, and the eligibility rules in vma_can_userfault() encode which kinds of memory the resolution machinery can actually service. Registration is limited to anonymous, shmem, and hugetlbfs VMAs — the three cases where the kernel has a way to install content on demand. VM_DROPPABLE mappings are excluded outright, since memory the kernel may discard at will cannot also be memory whose contents user space is responsible for supplying. Minor-fault mode additionally requires hugetlbfs or shmem, because a minor fault presupposes a page cache to have already been populated; there is no such thing for anonymous memory. Write-protect mode on anything other than anonymous memory requires kernel support for PTE markers, for the reason developed in Section 7.6. The one blanket exception is asynchronous write-protect: if UFFD_FEATURE_WP_ASYNC is negotiated and write-protect is the only mode requested, any memory type is permitted, because nothing will ever have to be delivered to satisfy the fault.

Table 9-14. Important userfaultfd ioctl Commands
Command Purpose Typical use
UFFDIO_APINegotiate API version and discover supported features.Mandatory first step after userfaultfd().
UFFDIO_REGISTERRegister a virtual range and desired fault modes; reports the range’s supported commands in uffdio_register.ioctls.Enable missing, write-protect, or minor-fault delivery.
UFFDIO_UNREGISTERDrop the registration on a range, clearing the VM_UFFD_* flags and waking anything still blocked on it.Hand a range back to ordinary demand paging once migration completes.
UFFDIO_COPYCopy user-provided data into the faulting range and wake waiters.Populate a missing page from a migration or pager thread.
UFFDIO_ZEROPAGEResolve a missing fault with a zero-filled page.Cheap anonymous population.
UFFDIO_WAKEWake threads blocked on previously deferred faults.Explicit completion or batch coordination.
UFFDIO_CONTINUEContinue execution after a minor fault once page state is ready.DAX or shmem minor-fault workflows.
UFFDIO_WRITEPROTECTApply or remove userfaultfd write protection.Dirty tracking and post-copy protection.
UFFDIO_POISONInstall a poisoned entry that later faults fatally.Testing or controlled error injection.
UFFDIO_MOVEMove page contents between ranges under userfaultfd control.Advanced migration or defragmentation patterns.

How the Kernel Blocks a Faulting Thread

The kernel integration point is in the ordinary fault path. When a VMA carries the appropriate userfaultfd flags, handle_userfault() intercepts the fault before the normal anonymous, file-backed, or CoW resolver completes it. Instead of installing a page immediately, Linux blocks the faulting thread, queues an event to the userfaultfd reader, and waits for user space to answer with one of the resolving ioctls. Only then does the kernel wake the faulting thread and let it retry the access.

Stated that way it sounds like an ordinary sleep, and the most important thing about the implementation is that it cannot be.
