A faulting thread arrives holding either mmap_lock for read or a per-VMA read lock, and sleeping indefinitely while holding either would be catastrophic: the manager thread that is supposed to resolve the fault needs to call UFFDIO_COPY, which needs the same lock. The fault would wait for the manager and the manager would wait for the fault. userfaultfd is therefore built on VM_FAULT_RETRY — on the ability, described in Section 6.2, for a fault handler to give up, drop the lock, and have the architecture layer re-enter the fault from the top. 

/* fs/userfaultfd.c — handle_userfault(), abbreviated */
vm_fault_t handle_userfault(struct vm_fault *vmf, unsigned long reason)
{
	struct vm_area_struct *vma = vmf->vma;
	struct mm_struct *mm = vma->vm_mm;
	struct userfaultfd_ctx *ctx;
	struct userfaultfd_wait_queue uwq;
	vm_fault_t ret = VM_FAULT_SIGBUS;
	bool must_wait;
	unsigned int blocking_state;

	/*
	 * We don't do userfault handling for the final child pid update
	 * and when coredumping (faults triggered by get_dump_page()).
	 */
	if (current->flags & (PF_EXITING|PF_DUMPCORE))
		goto out;

	assert_fault_locked(vmf);

	ctx = vma->vm_userfaultfd_ctx.ctx;
	if (!ctx)
		goto out;

	if (ctx->features & UFFD_FEATURE_SIGBUS)
		goto out;
	if (!(vmf->flags & FAULT_FLAG_USER) && (ctx->flags & UFFD_USER_MODE_ONLY))
		goto out;

	...

	/* take the reference before dropping the mmap_lock */
	userfaultfd_ctx_get(ctx);

	init_waitqueue_func_entry(&uwq.wq, userfaultfd_wake_function);
	uwq.wq.private = current;
	uwq.msg = userfault_msg(vmf->address, vmf->real_address, vmf->flags,
				reason, ctx->features);
	uwq.ctx = ctx;
	uwq.waken = false;

	blocking_state = userfaultfd_get_blocking_state(vmf->flags);

	spin_lock_irq(&ctx->fault_pending_wqh.lock);
	/*
	 * After the __add_wait_queue the uwq is visible to userland
	 * through poll/read().
	 */
	__add_wait_queue(&ctx->fault_pending_wqh, &uwq.wq);
	set_current_state(blocking_state);
	spin_unlock_irq(&ctx->fault_pending_wqh.lock);

	if (is_vm_hugetlb_page(vma)) {
		must_wait = userfaultfd_huge_must_wait(ctx, vmf, reason);
		hugetlb_vma_unlock_read(vma);
	} else {
		must_wait = userfaultfd_must_wait(ctx, vmf, reason);
	}

	release_fault_lock(vmf);

	if (likely(must_wait && !READ_ONCE(ctx->released))) {
		wake_up_poll(&ctx->fd_wqh, EPOLLIN);
		schedule();
	}

	__set_current_state(TASK_RUNNING);

The ordering in the middle of that function is the whole design, and it is not the obvious ordering. The wait queue entry is added, and the task state is set to sleeping, before the fault lock is released and before userfaultfd_must_wait() re-checks whether the page is still missing. Doing it in the intuitive order — check, then queue, then sleep — would leave a window in which the manager resolves the fault and issues its wakeup after the check but before the sleep, and the faulting thread would sleep forever with nobody left to wake it. Enqueueing first closes the window: any wakeup that arrives from this moment on finds an entry to act on. The comment above __add_wait_queue() makes the consequence explicit — from that instruction onward the pending fault is visible to poll() and read(), so user space may observe and answer it before the faulting thread has even reached schedule().

userfaultfd_must_wait() is the re-check, and it is a hand-written page-table walk rather than a call into the generic fault machinery, because it must run without taking the page-table locks that the faulting thread is about to drop. It descends PGD to PTE, treats any missing level as “still missing, keep waiting”, and for a write-protect fault asks the narrower question !pmd_write(_pmd) && (reason & VM_UFFD_WP). The comment concedes that the read is lockless and explains why that is acceptable: the thread is inside what amounts to a wait_event loop, so a value that changes underneath it merely costs one spurious wakeup.

Three early exits are worth reading as a group, because each encodes a policy rather than an error. A thread in PF_EXITING or PF_DUMPCORE never blocks — a core dump walking a registered range with get_dump_page() must not be held hostage by a manager that may itself be dying. UFFD_FEATURE_SIGBUS takes the VM_FAULT_SIGBUS the function was initialised with, which is how that feature is implemented: not as a separate path, but as declining to queue. And a kernel-mode fault against a UFFD_USER_MODE_ONLY descriptor falls through to the same VM_FAULT_SIGBUS, which is the enforcement half of the permission check in Section 7.2 — the flag is honoured at fault time, not merely at creation time.

The message the manager reads is assembled by userfault_msg(), and its encoding rewards a close reading. UFFD_PAGEFAULT_FLAG_WRITE says whether the access was a write; UFFD_PAGEFAULT_FLAG_WP and UFFD_PAGEFAULT_FLAG_MINOR say why the fault was intercepted. A missing fault is signalled by the absence of both reason flags rather than by a flag of its own, which is a compatibility artifact worth knowing about: missing mode predates the others, so its encoding is the empty one. The ptid field carries the faulting thread’s PID only when UFFD_FEATURE_THREAD_ID was negotiated, and the address is masked or exact according to UFFD_FEATURE_EXACT_ADDRESS — the message format is thus parameterised by the handshake, which is why the handshake is mandatory.

Finally, the resolving thread and the faulting thread meet on four distinct wait queues, not one. struct userfaultfd_ctx keeps fault_pending_wqh for faults that have been raised but not yet read, fault_wqh for faults that have been read and are awaiting resolution, event_wqh for the non-cooperative events of Section 7.8, and fd_wqh for threads blocked in poll() on the descriptor itself. The split between the first two is what lets UFFDIO_WAKE exist as a separate command: a manager may read a batch of faults, resolve them in any order it likes, and wake the corresponding threads later.

One last detail decides whether a stuck manager is a recoverable situation or a permanent one. The state the faulting thread sleeps in is not fixed; userfaultfd_get_blocking_state() derives it from the fault flags, returning TASK_INTERRUPTIBLE for FAULT_FLAG_INTERRUPTIBLE, TASK_KILLABLE for FAULT_FLAG_KILLABLE, and TASK_UNINTERRUPTIBLE otherwise. Because FAULT_FLAG_DEFAULT includes both of the first two, an ordinary user-space fault blocks interruptibly — so a process wedged behind a manager that never answers still responds to SIGKILL, and the administrator is not left with an unkillable task.

The Descriptor as a File: read, poll, and close

Section 7.3 left the faulting thread asleep on fault_pending_wqh with a message attached to its wait-queue entry. Nothing has been copied anywhere; the “queue of pending faults” is the wait queue itself, and the message lives on the stack of the thread that is blocked. That is an unusual design and it is what makes the manager side worth reading on its own, because every operation user space performs on the descriptor is really an operation on those queues. 

/* fs/userfaultfd.c — userfaultfd_ctx_read(), the refile; comments abridged */
		uwq = find_userfault(ctx);
		if (uwq) {
			/*
			 * Use a seqcount to repeat the lockless check
			 * in wake_userfault() to avoid missing
			 * wakeups because during the refile both
			 * waitqueue could become empty if this is the
			 * only userfault.
			 */
			write_seqcount_begin(&ctx->refile_seq);

			list_del(&uwq->wq.entry);
			add_wait_queue(&ctx->fault_wqh, &uwq->wq);

			write_seqcount_end(&ctx->refile_seq);

			/* careful to always initialize msg if ret == 0 */
			*msg = uwq->msg;

Reading a fault does not dequeue it — it moves it, from fault_pending_wqh to fault_wqh. That single list operation is the formal record of the state change from “the manager does not know about this fault” to “the manager knows and owes an answer”, and it is why the context needs two fault queues rather than one. It also explains refile_seq, a seqcount_spinlock_t whose only purpose is to guard this instant: wake_userfault() checks both queues without locking, and during the brief window between the list_del() and the add_wait_queue() a single outstanding fault would appear in neither. Without the seqcount a wakeup issued at exactly that moment would be lost, and the faulting thread would sleep with no one left to wake it. The sequence number lets the waker notice that the lists changed underneath it and look again.

Ordering is deliberate too. find_userfault_in() takes list_last_entry(), walking the queue in reverse precisely so that faults are delivered oldest first, and the comment says so: “walk in reverse to provide FIFO behavior to read userfaults”. Since new sleepers are added at the head, taking the tail is the oldest one. A manager therefore sees faults in the order they occurred rather than in whatever order the wait queue happens to be linked, which matters for a pager that is prefetching along an access pattern.

The read() path above it is a short loop with one carefully chosen asymmetry. userfaultfd_read_iter() fills as many struct uffd_msg records as the supplied buffer will hold, but it sets no_wait = true after the first, so a call blocks only while waiting for the very first message and then drains whatever else has already accumulated without blocking again. A manager can pass a large buffer and get batching for free when the system is busy, and a prompt single-message wakeup when it is not, without having to choose between the two at open() time. Messages are all-or-nothing: copy_to_iter_full() means a buffer smaller than one struct uffd_msg yields -EINVAL rather than a truncated record. 

/* fs/userfaultfd.c — userfaultfd_poll() */
	poll_wait(file, &ctx->fd_wqh, wait);

	if (!userfaultfd_is_initialized(ctx))
		return EPOLLERR;

	/*
	 * poll() never guarantees that read won't block.
	 * userfaults can be waken before they're read().
	 */
	if (unlikely(!(file->f_flags & O_NONBLOCK)))
		return EPOLLERR;

The second test is the surprising one, and it is a genuine interface requirement rather than a defensive check: polling a blocking userfaultfd descriptor returns EPOLLERR. The comment explains why. A fault reported as readable may be resolved and woken by another manager thread before this one gets to read(), so EPOLLIN is a hint and not a promise; on a blocking descriptor the subsequent read() would then sleep indefinitely inside the event loop. Rather than let that happen silently, the kernel refuses to poll a descriptor that could deadlock its caller. Any manager built around epoll must therefore pass O_NONBLOCK at creation, which is the practical reason UFFD_SHARED_FCNTL_FLAGS includes it.

Closing the descriptor is the last operation and the one with the most at stake, because at the moment of the final close() there may be threads asleep in handle_userfault() whose only possible source of wakeups is about to disappear. 

/* fs/userfaultfd.c — userfaultfd_release() */
	WRITE_ONCE(ctx->released, true);

	userfaultfd_release_all(mm, ctx);

	/*
	 * After no new page faults can wait on this fault_*wqh, flush
	 * the last page faults that may have been already waiting on
	 * the fault_*wqh.
	 */
	spin_lock_irq(&ctx->fault_pending_wqh.lock);
	__wake_up_locked_key(&ctx->fault_pending_wqh, TASK_NORMAL, &range);
	__wake_up(&ctx->fault_wqh, TASK_NORMAL, 1, &range);
	spin_unlock_irq(&ctx->fault_pending_wqh.lock);

	/* Flush pending events that may still wait on event_wqh */
	wake_up_all(&ctx->event_wqh);

	wake_up_poll(&ctx->fd_wqh, EPOLLHUP);

The order is the interesting part. released is published first, so that any thread arriving in handle_userfault() from now on takes the early exit described in Section 7.3 and returns VM_FAULT_RETRY instead of joining a queue nobody will service. userfaultfd_release_all() then strips VM_UFFD_* from every registered VMA, returning those ranges to ordinary demand paging. Only then are the existing sleepers woken, and all four queues are drained — pending faults, read-but-unresolved faults, non-cooperative events, and pollers, the last with EPOLLHUP so a manager in another thread learns the descriptor is gone.

The practical consequence is worth stating plainly, because it is the difference between userfaultfd being usable in production and being a way to wedge a machine. A manager that crashes does not strand the process it was managing. The kernel unwinds the registration on its behalf, the woken threads retry their faults, and the faults are now resolved by the ordinary anonymous or file-backed handlers as if userfaultfd had never been involved. The pages will contain zeroes rather than whatever the manager intended, which may well be fatal to the application — but it fails as a program with wrong data, not as an unkillable task in D state.

The same context is also visible from outside the process. userfaultfd_show_fdinfo() walks both fault queues and prints pending, total, and the negotiated API line into /proc/PID/fdinfo/FD. Since pending counts only the queue the manager has not read from, a descriptor whose pending is zero while total stays high is one whose manager is reading faults and failing to resolve them — a distinction that is otherwise invisible and is exactly what an administrator needs when diagnosing a stalled process.

Resolving a Fault: the mfill Family

Everything up to this point has been about stopping a fault. The commands that restart it all land in mm/userfaultfd.c, and although user space sees four different ioctls, the kernel sees one function with a mode argument. 

/* include/linux/userfaultfd_k.h */
/* A combined operation mode + behavior flags. */
typedef unsigned int __bitwise uffd_flags_t;

/* Mutually exclusive modes of operation. */
enum mfill_atomic_mode {
	MFILL_ATOMIC_COPY,
	MFILL_ATOMIC_ZEROPAGE,
	MFILL_ATOMIC_CONTINUE,
	MFILL_ATOMIC_POISON,
	NR_MFILL_ATOMIC_MODES,
};

/* Flags controlling behavior. These behavior changes are mode-independent. */
#define MFILL_ATOMIC_WP MFILL_ATOMIC_FLAG(0)

The split between a mode and mode-independent flags is the reason the four public entry points — mfill_atomic_copy(), mfill_atomic_zeropage(), mfill_atomic_continue(), and mfill_atomic_poison() — are each a single line that sets a mode and calls mfill_atomic(). Filling a page and marking it write-protected on arrival are orthogonal decisions, so MFILL_ATOMIC_WP rides alongside whichever mode was chosen; a migration manager that wants to keep tracking writes to a page it has just delivered gets that in the same ioctl rather than a second one. The four modes themselves answer the question “what should be at this address?”: copy content from a user buffer, install the shared zero page, publish a page that already exists in the page cache, or mark the address as permanently poisoned so any access raises VM_FAULT_HWPOISON.

mfill_atomic() is a loop, and its preamble is a checklist of things a malicious or confused manager might try. The destination range must be page aligned and must not wrap. It must lie entirely within a single VMA — uffd_mfill_lock() enforces that, and the restriction exists because a range spanning two VMAs could span two different registrations. MFILL_ATOMIC_WP is refused unless the destination VMA actually carries VM_UFFD_WP, because arming write-protect on a range that was never registered for it would create PTE state that nothing would ever clean up. MFILL_ATOMIC_CONTINUE is refused on anything that is not shmem or hugetlbfs, since there is no page cache to continue from in an anonymous mapping. And hugetlb VMAs are diverted wholesale to mfill_atomic_hugetlb(), which repeats the same logic at huge-page granularity. 

/* mm/userfaultfd.c — mfill_atomic(), the concurrency guard */
	dst_vma = uffd_mfill_lock(dst_mm, dst_start, len);
	if (IS_ERR(dst_vma)) {
		err = PTR_ERR(dst_vma);
		goto out;
	}

	/*
	 * If memory mappings are changing because of non-cooperative
	 * operation (e.g. mremap) running in parallel, bail out and
	 * request the user to retry later
	 */
	down_read(&ctx->map_changing_lock);
	err = -EAGAIN;
	if (atomic_read(&ctx->mmap_changing))
		goto out_unlock;

That short sequence is the answer to a problem that has no purely kernel-side solution. A manager decides to fill address A because a message said A was missing; between the message and the ioctl, another thread in the target process may mremap() the region so that A now names something entirely different. The kernel cannot tell that the manager’s intent has been invalidated, because the ioctl is perfectly well-formed. So it does the only safe thing: mmap_changing is incremented whenever a non-cooperative event is in flight and not yet consumed, and any resolving operation that observes a non-zero count returns -EAGAIN without doing anything. The manager is expected to drain its event queue and re-derive its intent. map_changing_lock ensures the count cannot be raised in the middle of an operation that has already passed the test.

Inside the loop, one case deserves attention because it looks like an error path and is really a lock-ordering trick. mfill_atomic_pte_copy() must copy a page from the manager’s buffer, and it does so while holding the destination’s mmap_lock. If the source buffer is itself not resident, that copy would fault — potentially on a range registered with the very same userfaultfd, which is an immediate deadlock. The kernel therefore attempts the copy atomically; when it cannot, it allocates the destination folio, returns -ENOENT, and hands the folio back through the foliop out-parameter. mfill_atomic() then drops both locks, performs the copy from user space with no locks held where faulting is harmless, and jumps to retry: with the already-allocated folio in hand. The re-derivation of dst_vma on retry is not wasted work: it is what makes dropping the lock safe.

The last common step, for every mode and every backing type, is mfill_atomic_install_pte(). It builds a PTE with mk_pte(), marks it dirty unconditionally, applies pte_mkuffd_wp() if MFILL_ATOMIC_WP was requested, and decides writability with a rule worth stating explicitly: a page that is already in the page cache is installed read-only in a private mapping even if the VMA is writable, because the first write must still go through do_wp_page() and produce a private copy. Installing it writable would silently turn a MAP_PRIVATE mapping into a shared one. The function then takes the PTE lock, re-verifies that the slot is still empty, and only then commits — so two managers racing on the same address produce one success and one -EEXIST, never two half-installed pages.

A resolving ioctl does not by itself wake anybody. Filling the page and waking the sleeper are separate steps, and UFFDIO_COPY performs the wake only when the caller omits UFFDIO_COPY_MODE_DONTWAKE. A manager expecting a burst of faults in one region can therefore fill an entire span with DONTWAKE set and issue a single UFFDIO_WAKE over the whole range at the end, turning what would have been one wakeup storm per page into one wakeup per batch. This is precisely what the two-queue design of Section 7.3 exists to permit.

Every resolving command carries a mode field, and the flags in it are largely the same few ideas repeated per command. Knowing the pattern is more useful than memorising the list.

Table 9-15. Mode Flags on the Resolving Commands
Flag Available on Effect
..._MODE_DONTWAKECOPY, ZEROPAGE, CONTINUE, POISON, MOVE, WRITEPROTECTResolve the range but leave the blocked threads asleep, so a batch can be woken by one later UFFDIO_WAKE.
..._MODE_WPCOPY, CONTINUEInstall the page already marked uffd write-protected, so the manager keeps tracking writes to a page it has just delivered. Rejected unless the VMA carries VM_UFFD_WP.
UFFDIO_WRITEPROTECT_MODE_WPWRITEPROTECTSelects direction: set when arming, clear when releasing. Its absence is not a no-op but the opposite operation.
UFFDIO_MOVE_MODE_ALLOW_SRC_HOLESMOVECount unmapped holes in the source range as successfully moved instead of failing with -ENOENT.

The DONTWAKE/WAKE split and the MODE_WP combination are both consequences of the same design choice: the kernel exposes each step of resolution as an independent decision rather than bundling them, so a manager composes the policy it wants out of primitives instead of choosing from a fixed menu of behaviours.

Write-Protect Mode and PTE Markers

Missing-mode faults ask a simple question — what content goes here? — and are answered by putting content there. Write-protect mode asks a harder one. The page is already present and correct; what the manager wants to observe is the moment it is modified, and it wants to do so without giving up its own ability to distinguish “user space asked me to protect this” from “this is read-only for some other reason”. 

/* mm/userfaultfd.c */
long uffd_wp_range(struct vm_area_struct *dst_vma,
		   unsigned long start, unsigned long len, bool enable_wp)
{
	unsigned int mm_cp_flags;
	struct mmu_gather tlb;
	long ret;

	VM_WARN_ONCE(start < dst_vma->vm_start || start + len > dst_vma->vm_end,
			"The address range exceeds VMA boundary.\n");
	if (enable_wp)
		mm_cp_flags = MM_CP_UFFD_WP;
	else
		mm_cp_flags = MM_CP_UFFD_WP_RESOLVE;

	/*
	 * vma->vm_page_prot already reflects that uffd-wp is enabled for this
	 * VMA (see userfaultfd_set_vm_flags()) and that all PTEs are supposed
	 * to be write-protected as default whenever protection changes.
	 * Try upgrading write permissions manually.
	 */
	if (!enable_wp && vma_wants_manual_pte_write_upgrade(dst_vma))
		mm_cp_flags |= MM_CP_TRY_CHANGE_WRITABLE;
	tlb_gather_mmu(&tlb, dst_vma->vm_mm);
	ret = change_protection(&tlb, dst_vma, start, start + len, mm_cp_flags);
	tlb_finish_mmu(&tlb);

	return ret;
}

The asymmetry between the two directions is the interesting part. Arming is cheap: MM_CP_UFFD_WP clears the write bit and sets the software uffd-wp bit, and the fault that follows is caught by do_wp_page() exactly as described in Section 6.5, which consults userfaultfd_pte_wp() and calls handle_userfault() instead of copying. Disarming is not the reverse operation. MM_CP_UFFD_WP_RESOLVE clears the marker, but the kernel cannot simply restore write access, because it does not know whether the page was writable before the manager intervened — the VMA may be a private mapping whose pages are legitimately read-only pending CoW, or a PROT_NONE region. MM_CP_TRY_CHANGE_WRITABLE is the conservative answer: try to restore write access where it can be proven safe, and leave the PTE read-only otherwise, accepting one extra ordinary CoW fault rather than risking an unsafe upgrade.

mwriteprotect_range() wraps this with the same mmap_changing protocol as the mfill family and iterates with for_each_vma_range(), requiring that every VMA it touches satisfies userfaultfd_wp() and returning -ENOENT otherwise. Ranges that cover huge pages must be huge-page aligned, because a partial write-protect of a THP would force a split at a point where the caller may not be prepared for one.

That leaves the question the whole mechanism turns on. Write-protect state lives in a PTE bit — but what happens when there is no PTE? A page in a registered range may be swapped out, or may never have been faulted in at all, and in both cases the slot is empty. If emptiness erased the registration, a manager could arm write-protection over a region, watch the kernel reclaim a page, and silently lose an event. Linux solves this with a swap entry that does not refer to swap. 

/* include/linux/swapops.h */
typedef unsigned long pte_marker;

#define  PTE_MARKER_UFFD_WP			BIT(0)
/*
 * "Poisoned" here is meant in the very general sense of "future accesses are
 * invalid", instead of referring very specifically to hardware memory errors.
 * This marker is meant to represent any of various different causes of this.
 *
 * Note that, when encountered by the faulting logic, PTEs with this marker will
 * result in VM_FAULT_HWPOISON and thus regardless trigger hardware memory error
 * logic.
 */
#define  PTE_MARKER_POISONED			BIT(1)
/*
 * Indicates that, on fault, this PTE will case a SIGSEGV signal to be
 * sent. This means guard markers behave in effect as if the region were mapped
 * PROT_NONE, rather than if they were a memory hole or equivalent.
 */
#define  PTE_MARKER_GUARD			BIT(2)
#define  PTE_MARKER_MASK			(BIT(3) - 1)

A PTE marker is a non-present entry of type SWP_PTE_MARKER whose payload is a small bitmask rather than a swap slot. To hardware it is simply absent, so an access still faults; to the fault handler it is a note left by a previous owner of the slot. PTE_MARKER_UFFD_WP is that note for write-protection: when a write-protected page is reclaimed, the marker survives it, and when the page is faulted back in the kernel reads the marker and re-applies the protection before user space can touch the page. The same machinery is what makes UFFD_FEATURE_WP_UNPOPULATED possible — a range can be write-protected before it has any pages at all, because the protection is recorded in the otherwise-empty slots themselves.

The other two markers show that the mechanism outgrew its origin. PTE_MARKER_POISONED is how UFFDIO_POISON is implemented, and the comment is careful to say that “poisoned” here means “future accesses are invalid” in general rather than specifically a hardware memory error — a migration manager that has lost a page can mark it so the guest faults deterministically instead of reading stale data. PTE_MARKER_GUARD implements guard regions, where the marker makes an address behave as if it were PROT_NONE without the VMA splitting that a real mprotect() would cause. Both reuse the observation that an empty page-table slot has room to carry information.

One recent variant deserves a note because it inverts the usual cost model. UFFD_FEATURE_WP_ASYNC arms write-protection without a manager in the loop: a write clears the marker and proceeds, and the fact that it happened is recorded for later retrieval through /proc/pid/pagemap rather than delivered as a message. This is dirty tracking without the round trip — the right trade for a checkpointing tool that wants to know which pages changed but does not need to intervene when they do. The header notes that this feature implies UFFD_FEATURE_WP_UNPOPULATED, since asynchronous tracking over a sparsely populated region is only meaningful if unpopulated slots can carry markers too.

UFFDIO_MOVE: Resolving a Fault Without Copying

UFFDIO_COPY is the command a pager reaches for, and for a pager it is the right one: the page arrives from a socket or a file into a staging buffer, and copying it into place is unavoidable. But there is a second shape of problem where the page is already in this address space, merely at the wrong address, and copying it is pure waste. UFFDIO_MOVE, added in Linux 6.8 and implemented by move_pages() in mm/userfaultfd.c, resolves such a fault by editing page tables alone.

The kernel’s own description of the intended use is unusually concrete, and it is worth following because it explains every restriction the command imposes. A manager receives an incoming page over the network into a scratch region; it then issues UFFDIO_MOVE to relocate that page to the faulting address in the destination region. No copy occurs, no new VMA is created, and the destination mapping appears atomically. Compared with mremap(), which would split and merge VMAs and perturb the address-space layout, this is a page-table operation and nothing more — the source and destination may even lie within the same VMA.

Buying that efficiency means giving up generality, and validate_move_areas() is where the price is charged. 

/* mm/userfaultfd.c */
static inline bool vma_move_compatible(struct vm_area_struct *vma)
{
	return !(vma->vm_flags & (VM_PFNMAP | VM_IO |  VM_HUGETLB |
				  VM_MIXEDMAP | VM_SHADOW_STACK));
}

static int validate_move_areas(struct userfaultfd_ctx *ctx,
			       struct vm_area_struct *src_vma,
			       struct vm_area_struct *dst_vma)
{
	/* Only allow moving if both have the same access and protection */
	if ((src_vma->vm_flags & VM_ACCESS_FLAGS) != (dst_vma->vm_flags & VM_ACCESS_FLAGS) ||
	    pgprot_val(src_vma->vm_page_prot) != pgprot_val(dst_vma->vm_page_prot))
		return -EINVAL;

	/* Only allow moving if both are mlocked or both aren't */
	if ((src_vma->vm_flags & VM_LOCKED) != (dst_vma->vm_flags & VM_LOCKED))
		return -EINVAL;

	/*
	 * For now, we keep it simple and only move between writable VMAs.
	 * Access flags are equal, therefore checking only the source is enough.
	 */
	if (!(src_vma->vm_flags & VM_WRITE))
		return -EINVAL;

	/* Check if vma flags indicate content which can be moved */
	if (!vma_move_compatible(src_vma) || !vma_move_compatible(dst_vma))
		return -EINVAL;

	/* Ensure dst_vma is registered in uffd we are operating on */
	if (!dst_vma->vm_userfaultfd_ctx.ctx ||
	    dst_vma->vm_userfaultfd_ctx.ctx != ctx)
		return -EINVAL;

	/* Only allow moving across anonymous vmas */
	if (!vma_is_anonymous(src_vma) || !vma_is_anonymous(dst_vma))
		return -EINVAL;

	return 0;
}

Each rule follows from the fact that only the PTE is being moved, not the page’s relationship to anything else. Protection bits and access flags must match exactly, because the PTE is transplanted rather than rebuilt from the destination VMA’s vm_page_prot; if they differed, the moved entry would carry the source’s permissions into a region documented to have the destination’s. VM_LOCKED must match because moving a page between a locked and an unlocked region would silently change the process’s locked-memory accounting. Both sides must be anonymous, because a file-backed page is indexed by its offset in the file and cannot simply appear at a different virtual address. And vma_move_compatible() excludes the VMA types whose page-table entries do not stand for ordinary anonymous folios at all — device mappings (VM_PFNMAP, VM_IO, VM_MIXEDMAP), hugetlbfs, and shadow stacks, the last because a shadow-stack PTE’s write-protected-but-dirty encoding is what distinguishes it from ordinary memory to the hardware.

The most restrictive condition is not in that function but in move_present_ptes(), and it is the one a caller is most likely to trip over: 

/* mm/userfaultfd.c — move_present_ptes() */
	if (folio_test_large(src_folio) ||
	    folio_maybe_dma_pinned(src_folio) ||
	    !PageAnonExclusive(&src_folio->page)) {
		err = -EBUSY;

PageAnonExclusive is the same flag that governs copy-on-write reuse in Section 6.5, and it is required here for the same underlying reason. Moving a page means moving its single reverse-mapping relationship from one virtual address to another; if any other page table also maps that folio, the move would have to update those mappings too, and the rmap code has no way to express one anonymous folio reachable from two unrelated anonymous VMAs without creating exactly the non-linear anon_vma structure the comment says must be avoided. A DMA-pinned folio is refused for the complementary reason: a device holds a reference to the physical page and is unaware that its virtual address changed. The documented remedy for the sharing case is to apply madvise(MADV_DONTFORK) to the source region, so that an intervening fork() cannot silently raise the mapcount and turn a working UFFDIO_MOVE into an -EBUSY.

Three source states are handled, and the third shows how much of the work is bookkeeping rather than data movement. move_present_ptes() handles the ordinary case of a resident page. move_zeropage_pte() handles a source that maps the shared zero page, where there is no folio to move at all and the destination simply gains a zero-page mapping. move_swap_pte() handles a source that has been swapped out: the swap entry itself is relocated, so the page is never read back from disk merely to be moved — the eventual fault at the destination will page it in directly. A source that is a huge PMD is routed to move_pages_huge_pmd() and moved whole rather than split.

Because two page tables are manipulated at once, move_pages() needs two PTE locks, and taking two locks of the same class is where deadlocks come from. 

/* mm/userfaultfd.c */
void double_pt_lock(spinlock_t *ptl1,
		    spinlock_t *ptl2)
	__acquires(ptl1)
	__acquires(ptl2)
{
	if (ptl1 > ptl2)
		swap(ptl1, ptl2);
	/* lock in virtual address order to avoid lock inversion */
	spin_lock(ptl1);
	if (ptl1 != ptl2)
		spin_lock_nested(ptl2, SINGLE_DEPTH_NESTING);
	else
		__acquire(ptl2);
}

The rule is the standard one — impose a total order on the locks, here their addresses, so two threads moving pages in opposite directions cannot acquire them in opposite orders. The ptl1 == ptl2 case is real rather than theoretical, since a move within a single PMD finds the same lock on both sides; the __acquire() annotation tells sparse the lock is held without taking it twice. Once both locks are held, is_pte_pages_stable() re-validates that neither entry changed while they were being acquired, returning -EAGAIN to restart if so.

Finally, the error behaviour is deliberately unforgiving, and the reasoning is stated outright in the source: a source hole yields -ENOENT and a destination that is not entirely empty yields -EEXIST, “to avoid any chance of memory corruption going unnoticed if there are userland race conditions”. Two threads racing to resolve the same faulting address do not both succeed; the second receives an explicit error. The return value is a byte count rather than a status, so a move interrupted by a fatal signal reports how far it got and the caller is expected to reissue the command with adjusted arguments to discover the underlying error. UFFDIO_MOVE_MODE_ALLOW_SRC_HOLES relaxes only the first of these, counting holes as moved, which lets a manager relocate a naturally aligned huge region without first determining whether it is actually backed by a huge page — and thus without risking a PMD split it did not want.

Non-Cooperative Mode

Everything so far has assumed a cooperative arrangement: the process being managed knows it is being managed, and does not reshape its own address space behind the manager’s back. That assumption holds for a hand-written pager and fails immediately for the case userfaultfd was extended to serve — live migration of a container or virtual machine whose processes have no idea anything unusual is happening, and which will call fork(), mremap(), madvise(MADV_DONTNEED), and munmap() whenever they please.

Non-cooperative mode is the set of features that make that survivable, and it works by delivering address-space changes to the manager as messages on the same descriptor as faults. Four events exist, each requested by its own feature bit, and the kernel calls into fs/userfaultfd.c from the relevant memory-management path:

Table 9-16. Non-Cooperative Events and Their Kernel Hooks
Event Feature bit Kernel hook What the manager must do
UFFD_EVENT_FORKUFFD_FEATURE_EVENT_FORKdup_userfaultfd(), completed by dup_userfaultfd_complete()Read the new descriptor out of the message and start managing the child too.
UFFD_EVENT_REMAPUFFD_FEATURE_EVENT_REMAPmremap_userfaultfd_prep() / _complete()Translate its own bookkeeping from the old range to the new one.
UFFD_EVENT_REMOVEUFFD_FEATURE_EVENT_REMOVEuserfaultfd_remove()Discard cached state for a range the process has abandoned.
UFFD_EVENT_UNMAPUFFD_FEATURE_EVENT_UNMAPuserfaultfd_unmap_prep() / _complete()Stop managing a range that no longer exists.

The fork event is the one that could not be done any other way. A registered VMA is inherited by the child, so the child’s faults must go somewhere — but the child is a different address space, and a userfaultfd context is bound to one mm_struct. dup_userfaultfd() therefore creates a whole new context for the child during fork(), and the message the manager receives carries the file descriptor for it. If the manager never reads that message, the descriptor leaks; this is a case where ignoring an event is not merely a lost optimisation. The _prep/_complete split visible in three of the four hooks exists because the allocation and the notification cannot happen at the same point — the preparation runs while locks are held and cannot sleep, and the completion runs afterwards and delivers the message.

There is one property of these events that changes how the whole mode must be reasoned about, and it is not visible from the ioctl interface at all: they are synchronous. The process performing the fork(), mremap(), or munmap() does not merely notify the manager and continue. It blocks inside the memory-management operation until the manager has read the event and the kernel has completed it. 

/* fs/userfaultfd.c — userfaultfd_event_wait_completion(), abbreviated */
	spin_lock_irq(&ctx->event_wqh.lock);
	/*
	 * After the __add_wait_queue the uwq is visible to userland
	 * through poll/read().
	 */
	__add_wait_queue(&ctx->event_wqh, &ewq->wq);
	for (;;) {
		set_current_state(TASK_KILLABLE);
		if (ewq->msg.event == 0)
			break;
		if (READ_ONCE(ctx->released) ||
		    fatal_signal_pending(current)) {
			...
			break;
		}

		spin_unlock_irq(&ctx->event_wqh.lock);

		wake_up_poll(&ctx->fd_wqh, EPOLLIN);
		schedule();

		spin_lock_irq(&ctx->event_wqh.lock);
	}

The shape is the same as the fault path of Section 7.3 — enqueue, publish, sleep, and let the reader complete the entry by zeroing msg.event — but the consequence is different in kind. Blocking the faulting thread is the point of a fault event; blocking a thread in the middle of fork() is a much heavier commitment, and it is what makes the guarantee behind mmap_changing possible. If the process could complete an mremap() while the manager was still unaware of it, there would be a window in which the manager’s view were wrong and no amount of counting could tell it so. Because the operation waits, the address space cannot move on until the manager has been told that it moved.

The two escape hatches are therefore essential rather than incidental. The wait is TASK_KILLABLE, so a manager that stops reading events does not produce unkillable processes; and it aborts on ctx->released, so closing the descriptor releases everyone blocked this way, exactly as it releases blocked faults. The WARN_ON_ONCE(current->flags & PF_EXITING) at the top asserts that an exiting task never reaches here, since a thread that is tearing down its address space has no one left to wait for.

The fork event needs a further step that the others do not, and it is the reason userfaultfd_ctx_read() treats UFFD_EVENT_FORK specially instead of completing it inline like the rest. Delivering the event means handing user space a new file descriptor, and installing a descriptor is a sleeping operation that cannot be performed under event_wqh.lock. The read path therefore takes a reference on the child context, moves the entry to a local fork_event list, drops the lock, creates the descriptor with resolve_userfault_fork(), and only then reacquires the lock to complete the waiter. The reference is what makes dropping the lock safe: without it the child context could be freed by a concurrent release in the window between. The companion functions dup_userfaultfd_fail() and mremap_userfaultfd_fail() exist for the mirror-image case, where the operation that prepared an event fails afterwards and the prepared state must be unwound rather than delivered.

The other three events all exist to keep a manager’s picture of the address space from going stale, and they are the reason mmap_changing exists. Between the moment the kernel begins an mremap() and the moment the manager reads the corresponding message, any address the manager holds may be meaningless. Rather than trying to define correct behaviour for an ioctl issued in that window, the kernel refuses it — the -EAGAIN of Section 7.5. Read in that light, mmap_changing is not a lock protecting data; it is a barrier around a conversation, ensuring that the manager cannot act on information the kernel has already superseded but not yet retracted.

Non-cooperative mode is also the reason the security gate of Section 7.2 is set where it is. A process that can register another process’s memory and be notified of every structural change to it has substantial observational power, which is why vm.unprivileged_userfaultfd defaults to 0 and why CAP_SYS_PTRACE — the capability that already confers the right to inspect another process — is the one that bypasses it.

Conceptually, userfaultfd moves one piece of the page-fault handler out of the kernel without weakening the rest. The VMA lookup, permission checks, page-table walking, blocking, and wakeup remain kernel responsibilities. What user space gains is the ability to choose what page content or state should satisfy the fault. That is much narrower and much safer than exposing arbitrary page-table editing directly.

This interface ties back to the earlier sections in a revealing way. The VMA stores the registration state, the generic fault path notices it, and the final resolution still arrives through a normal page-table update. userfaultfd therefore does not replace VMAs, page tables, or demand paging. It inserts a programmable control point into the middle of the existing pipeline.

Creating and Deleting Address Spaces

An address space is not static. Processes create one at fork(), replace it at execve(), and tear it down at exit. These transitions are where the topology objects from the first half of the chapter and the fault semantics from the second half come together. The kernel must copy or rebuild VMA metadata, preserve copy-on-write invariants, replace page-table roots, and eventually destroy every interval, page table, and reverse-mapping structure in the right order.

These three operations — clone, replace, destroy — form the complete lifecycle of a Linux address space, and each has very different performance characteristics and correctness constraints. fork() must be cheap enough that shell scripts creating thousands of short-lived children remain fast; it achieves this through copy-on-write, deferring physical page copies until absolutely necessary. execve() must be thorough enough to enforce process isolation; it achieves this by discarding the entire old topology and building a fresh one from the executable image. exit() must be complete enough that no resources leak; it achieves this through a carefully ordered teardown that satisfies every subsystem (page tables, reverse mappings, userfaultfd, memcg accounting, file writeback) that attached state to the address space during its lifetime.

Understanding these transitions is important because they are not just "plumbing." They directly affect application behavior in observable ways: fork() determines the initial cost of creating a child process and the ongoing CoW pressure on the parent; execve() determines how ASLR, the VDSO, and the initial stack layout are established; and exit() determines when memory is actually returned to the system and how quickly other processes see freed resources. The interplay between these operations also has subtle consequences — for instance, a process that forks and then immediately execs pays the cost of duplicating VMAs and PTEs only to discard them moments later, which is why vfork() and posix_spawn() exist as optimized alternatives.

Fork — Duplicating an Address Space

fork() is the Unix way of creating a new process: the child begins life as an almost-exact copy of the parent. From the address-space perspective, that means the child needs its own mm_struct, its own maple tree full of VMAs, its own page-table hierarchy, and its own set of reference counts and locks — but it should share as much physical memory as possible with the parent to keep the operation fast. A modern server process might have thousands of VMAs covering gigabytes of virtual address space; eagerly copying all of that would make fork() O(resident set size), which is unacceptable.

The solution is copy-on-write at the page-table level. Linux copies the metadata (VMA topology, page-table entries) but marks all private writable PTEs as read-only in both parent and child. The physical folios are shared until one side writes, at which point the do_wp_page() handler from Section 6.5 allocates a private copy. This makes fork() O(page table size) rather than O(physical memory) — and, as Section 8.1 shows when it reaches vma_needs_copy(), for many mappings the kernel does not copy the page tables either. In practice most children either exec immediately (discarding the copied topology) or touch only a small fraction of the parent's pages.

When a process calls fork(), Linux does not build the child from scratch. Instead, copy_mm() in kernel/fork.c decides whether the child will share the parent's address space or receive a duplicate. For the normal fork() case, it allocates a new mm_struct and passes control to dup_mm() and then dup_mmap() in mm/mmap.c. That path clones the address-space topology one VMA at a time. 

/* kernel/fork.c — comments abridged */
static int copy_mm(u64 clone_flags, struct task_struct *tsk)
{
	struct mm_struct *mm, *oldmm;

	tsk->min_flt = tsk->maj_flt = 0;
	tsk->nvcsw = tsk->nivcsw = 0;

	tsk->mm = NULL;
	tsk->active_mm = NULL;

	/*
	 * Are we cloning a kernel thread?
	 *
	 * We need to steal a active VM for that..
	 */
	oldmm = current->mm;
	if (!oldmm)
		return 0;

	if (clone_flags & CLONE_VM) {
		mmget(oldmm);
		mm = oldmm;
	} else {
		mm = dup_mm(tsk, current->mm);
		if (!mm)
			return -ENOMEM;
	}

	tsk->mm = mm;
	tsk->active_mm = mm;
	return 0;
}

Three cases are folded into that one function and the distinctions matter. A kernel thread has no mm at all, so copy_mm() returns immediately with both pointers NULL; such a thread will run on whatever address space it inherits lazily at context-switch time, which is what active_mm tracks. A CLONE_VM caller — every pthread_create() — takes a reference on the existing mm_struct and shares it outright; no topology is copied, which is the whole point of a thread. Only the third case, an ordinary fork(), reaches dup_mm(). That tsk->mm and tsk->active_mm are set to the same value here is the normal state for a user task; they diverge only while a kernel thread is borrowing a user address space, a distinction that returns in Section 8.3 when the exiting task hands its borrowed reference back. 

/* kernel/fork.c — dup_mm(), error paths omitted */
	mm = allocate_mm();
	if (!mm)
		goto fail_nomem;

	memcpy(mm, oldmm, sizeof(*mm));

	if (!mm_init(mm, tsk, mm->user_ns))
		goto fail_nomem;

	uprobe_start_dup_mmap();
	err = dup_mmap(mm, oldmm);
	if (err)
		goto free_pt;
	uprobe_end_dup_mmap();

	mm->hiwater_rss = get_mm_rss(mm);
	mm->hiwater_vm = mm->total_vm;

The memcpy followed by mm_init() is a deliberate two-phase pattern: the bulk copy carries forward layout fields (start_code, mmap_base, start_brk, flags) without enumerating them individually, then mm_init() reinitializes everything that must be unique per address space — a fresh PGD allocation, an empty maple tree, zeroed counters, and new lock and sequence state. The pattern is efficient but fragile in a specific way: any field added to mm_struct is inherited by default, and a field that must not be inherited is correct only if someone remembers to clear it in mm_init(). The high-water marks reset afterwards are an example of the opposite decision — the child’s peak RSS and peak virtual size are deliberately seeded from its inherited size rather than from zero, so that getrusage() reports a figure the child has genuinely reached.

Everything of interest then happens in dup_mmap(), which is the function that actually clones the address-space topology, and it is worth following because almost every VMA property introduced earlier in this chapter reappears here as a decision about what to inherit. It begins by duplicating the maple tree wholesale: 

/* mm/mmap.c — dup_mmap() */
	/* Use __mt_dup() to efficiently build an identical maple tree. */
	retval = __mt_dup(&oldmm->mm_mt, &mm->mm_mt, GFP_KERNEL);
	if (unlikely(retval))
		goto out;

	mt_clear_in_rcu(vmi.mas.tree);
	for_each_vma(vmi, mpnt) {

The order is worth noticing because it is the reverse of the obvious one. Rather than walking the parent’s VMAs and inserting each new one into an initially empty child tree — which would mean a tree allocation and rebalance per VMA — __mt_dup() copies the parent’s tree structure in one pass, after which the loop merely replaces each entry with the child’s copy. The comment at the insertion point spells out the payoff: because the nodes already exist, vma_iter_bulk_store() cannot fail, which removes an entire class of partial-failure handling from the middle of the loop. mt_clear_in_rcu() takes the child’s tree out of RCU mode while it is being built, since nothing can see it yet, and mt_set_in_rcu() restores it on success.

Inside the loop, the first two tests decide whether a region is inherited at all.

Table 9-17. VMA Flags That Change Fork Semantics
Flag Effect in dup_mmap() Why it exists
VM_DONTCOPYThe range is cleared from the child’s tree and its pages are subtracted from the child’s accounting. The child has no mapping there at all.madvise(MADV_DONTFORK). Used for DMA buffers, where a child inheriting the mapping would break the pinning assumptions the device relies on.
VM_WIPEONFORKThe VMA is inherited but with anon_vma forced to NULL and no page-table copy, so the child sees zero-filled memory.madvise(MADV_WIPEONFORK). Lets a library keep per-process secrets — RNG state, session keys — that must not survive into a child.
VM_ACCOUNTThe child’s pages are charged before duplication, and the charge is reversed if the fork fails.Strict overcommit accounting. Note the charge is taken against oldmm, marked /* sic */ in the source — an acknowledged quirk rather than a typo.
VM_LOCKEDCleared unconditionally in the child by vm_flags_clear(tmp, VM_LOCKED_MASK).mlock() is not inherited across fork(), as POSIX requires. A child would otherwise silently inherit a locked-memory charge it never requested.

That last row is the one most likely to surprise, and it has a practical consequence: a process that locks a sensitive buffer with mlock() and then forks has a child whose copy of that buffer is not locked and may therefore be written to swap. The flag is cleared, but the mapping and its contents are inherited as usual.

Each surviving VMA is then duplicated by vm_area_dup() and re-attached to everything it was connected to. vma_dup_policy() copies the NUMA memory policy; dup_userfaultfd() queues the fork event described in Section 7.8; anon_vma_fork() builds the child’s reverse-mapping structures, linking the new anon_vma to the parent’s so that rmap walks can still find every mapping of a shared folio; and hugetlb_dup_vma_private() carries forward hugetlbfs reservation state. If the VMA has a vm_ops->open() callback it is invoked, giving drivers with per-mapping state a chance to take their own reference.

File-backed VMAs need one more step, and its precision is easy to miss: 

/* mm/mmap.c — dup_mmap(), the file case */
			get_file(file);
			i_mmap_lock_write(mapping);
			if (vma_is_shared_maywrite(tmp))
				mapping_allow_writable(mapping);
			flush_dcache_mmap_lock(mapping);
			/* insert tmp into the share list, just after mpnt */
			vma_interval_tree_insert_after(tmp, mpnt,
					&mapping->i_mmap);
			flush_dcache_mmap_unlock(mapping);
			i_mmap_unlock_write(mapping);

The child’s VMA is inserted after the parent’s in the file’s interval tree rather than at an arbitrary position. Both cover exactly the same file range, so a plain insert would be free to order them either way; placing the child immediately after the parent keeps the two adjacent, which matters for the cache behaviour of rmap walks that visit every mapping of a page and would otherwise jump between distant parts of the tree.

The final step of each iteration is copying the page tables, and it is the step whose behaviour has changed most since Linux 2.6. 

/* mm/memory.c */
/*
 * Return true if the vma needs to copy the pgtable during this fork().  Return
 * false when we can speed up fork() by allowing lazy page faults later until
 * when the child accesses the memory range.
 */
static bool
vma_needs_copy(struct vm_area_struct *dst_vma, struct vm_area_struct *src_vma)
{
	/*
	 * We check against dst_vma as while sane VMA flags will have been
	 * copied, VM_UFFD_WP may be set only on dst_vma.
	 */
	if (dst_vma->vm_flags & VM_COPY_ON_FORK)
		return true;
	/*
	 * The presence of an anon_vma indicates an anonymous VMA has page
	 * tables which naturally cannot be reconstituted on page fault.
	 */
	if (src_vma->anon_vma)
		return true;

	/*
	 * Don't copy ptes where a page fault will fill them correctly.  Fork
	 * becomes much lighter when there are big shared or private readonly
	 * mappings. The tradeoff is that copy_page_range is more efficient
	 * than faulting.
	 */
	return false;
}

fork() does not copy page tables for most mappings. A file-backed VMA that has never been written — every shared library’s text and read-only data, the executable itself, any MAP_SHARED region — has no anon_vma, and its PTEs can be reconstructed exactly by the ordinary fault path. Copying them would duplicate information that is already recoverable from the VMA, so the kernel skips it and lets the child fault the entries back in on demand. This refines the familiar claim that fork() is O(page-table size): it is O(page-table size of the anonymous and special mappings only), which for a typical dynamically linked program is a small fraction of the total.

The exceptions in VM_COPY_ON_FORK are precisely the mappings whose page-table contents cannot be reconstructed from the VMA, and reading it is a good test of whether the earlier sections have landed. VM_PFNMAP and VM_MIXEDMAP describe device memory whose PFNs the fault path does not know how to regenerate. VM_UFFD_WP appears because the write-protect state of Section 7.6 lives in PTEs and PTE markers, not in the VMA, so discarding the page tables would silently disarm the registration — and the comment explains the subtlety that this flag must be tested on the destination, since it may have been set on the child alone. VM_MAYBE_GUARD is there for the same reason, guard markers being, as include/linux/mm.h puts it, “a property of the page tables only”.

When copying does happen, private writable mappings are prepared for copy-on-write: both parent and child PTEs are made read-only, reverse mappings are updated, and the actual copying is deferred until a later write fault. That is where the do_wp_page() path from Section 6.5 re-enters the story. The write-protection is not merely bookkeeping, though, and the sequence counter that guards it deserves a closer look than the phrase “helps coordinate” suggests. 

/* mm/memory.c — copy_page_range() */
	if (is_cow) {
		mmu_notifier_range_init(&range, MMU_NOTIFY_PROTECTION_PAGE,
					0, src_mm, addr, end);
		mmu_notifier_invalidate_range_start(&range);
		/*
		 * Disabling preemption is not needed for the write side, as
		 * the read side doesn't spin, but goes to the mmap_lock.
		 *
		 * Use the raw variant of the seqcount_t write API to avoid
		 * lockdep complaining about preemptibility.
		 */
		vma_assert_write_locked(src_vma);
		raw_write_seqcount_begin(&src_mm->write_protect_seq);
	}

write_protect_seq exists to close a race between fork() and lockless GUP-fast, described in Section 11.3. GUP-fast walks page tables without taking mmap_lock, so it can observe a PTE after fork() has decided to write-protect it but before the write-protection is visible, and would then hand a caller a writable pin on a page the parent is about to lose exclusive access to. The counter lets GUP-fast detect that a fork was in progress across its walk and fall back to the slow, locked path. The window matters only for CoW mappings, which is why the counter is taken under is_cow and not unconditionally. The mmu_notifier range around it serves the analogous purpose for secondary MMUs — a KVM guest or an IOMMU must be told about the permission downgrade, and the surrounding comment notes that this is needed only when a downgrade can actually occur.

Two final details close out the function. On success, ksm_fork() and khugepaged_fork() propagate the parent’s opt-in state for page merging and huge-page collapse, so a child inherits not just the memory but the policies that were applied to it. On failure — and dup_mmap() can fail partway through, having built some of the child’s VMAs but not all — the code sets MMF_OOM_SKIP before unwinding, hiding the half-built address space from the OOM killer and the OOM reaper. Without it, the reaper could try to harvest memory from an mm whose maple tree is in a transient state, which is the sort of bug that appears only under memory pressure.

Shared mappings need none of this. A MAP_SHARED file mapping remains shared across parent and child, so there is no CoW transition and, as vma_needs_copy() shows, usually no page-table copy either. Both tasks continue pointing at the same underlying cache or file-backed pages with the same sharing semantics. This distinction is why fork() can cheaply clone a process that has a large shared library set or shared-memory segments: most of that state is already meant to be shared, and the kernel has arranged for the cheap case to be the common one.

Exec — Replacing an Address Space

If fork() is a lazy clone, execve() is a controlled demolition followed by reconstruction. Where fork preserves the parent's topology and defers costs, exec discards everything and starts fresh. This is the point at which a process stops running the old program and begins running the new one — and from the MM perspective, "everything" includes every VMA, every page-table entry, every file mapping, every userfaultfd registration, every mlock, and every anonymous folio reference that the old program established.

The transition has a critical security property: it provides a clean isolation boundary between the old program and the new one. The new executable gets a freshly randomized address layout (ASLR), a clean stack, and no residual access to the old program's private data. This is especially important for setuid/setgid programs, where the new executable may gain elevated privileges — any leaked mapping from the old address space would be a security vulnerability.

The old address space must be discarded, except for carefully preserved process attributes, and a new one must be built around the executable image, its interpreter, its data segments, its stack, and support mappings such as the VDSO. What makes the sequence tractable is a design decision that is easy to miss: the kernel does not modify the caller’s address space and then hope the load succeeds. It builds a second, temporary mm_struct first, and only swaps it in once failure is no longer possible. 

/* fs/exec.c — bprm_mm_init(), MMU case */
	bprm->mm = mm = mm_alloc();
	err = -ENOMEM;
	if (!mm)
		goto err;

	/* Save current stack limit for all calculations made during exec. */
	task_lock(current->group_leader);
	bprm->rlim_stack = current->signal->rlim[RLIMIT_STACK];
	task_unlock(current->group_leader);

	err = create_init_stack_vma(bprm->mm, &bprm->vma, &bprm->p);
	if (err)
		goto err;

This scratch address space belongs to no task. It exists only as bprm->mm, and it holds exactly one VMA: a single page of anonymous stack created by create_init_stack_vma() in mm/vma_exec.c. The argument and environment strings copied from the caller are written into that VMA, which is why the stack rlimit is captured up front — every size calculation during the load is made against the limit in force at the moment of the call, not against a value that another thread might change midway.

The placement of that initial VMA is deliberately provisional. create_init_stack_vma() puts it at STACK_TOP_MAX, the highest stack address the architecture could ever use, and the comment explains that the real STACK_TOP cannot be used yet because it depends on personality and compatibility attributes that the binary format handler has not determined. The VMA is also marked VM_STACK_INCOMPLETE_SETUP, a flag whose entire purpose is to tell other subsystems — notably page migration and mlock — to leave this region alone while it is still being assembled. Later, once the format handler knows the true stack top, setup_arg_pages() calls relocate_vma_down() to slide the VMA and its page tables to their final randomized address in one operation, and clears the flag.

Only after the image has been examined and the arguments staged does the kernel commit. begin_new_exec() is that commitment, and the source labels the moment explicitly. 

/* fs/exec.c — begin_new_exec(), abbreviated */
	/* Once we are committed compute the creds */
	retval = bprm_creds_from_file(bprm);
	if (retval)
		return retval;

	/*
	 * Ensure all future errors are fatal.
	 */
	bprm->point_of_no_return = true;

	/* Make this the only thread in the thread group */
	retval = de_thread(me);
	if (retval)
		goto out;
	/* see the comment in check_unsafe_exec() */
	current->fs->in_exec = 0;
	/*
	 * Cancel any io_uring activity across execve
	 */
	io_uring_task_cancel();

	/* Ensure the files table is not shared. */
	retval = unshare_files();

Older kernels called flush_old_exec() here. It no longer exists. Linux 5.10 split it into begin_new_exec() and the surrounding helpers precisely to make the point of no return explicit, because the old function performed both reversible and irreversible work and it had become genuinely difficult to say which errors a caller could still recover from.

Everything before point_of_no_return can fail harmlessly: the caller keeps its address space, its threads, and its credentials, and execve() returns an error to a program that is still the old program. Everything after it cannot be undone, so a failure becomes a fatal signal rather than an error return. de_thread() is the first irreversible act — it kills every other thread in the group, since execve() is defined to leave exactly one thread running, and a multi-threaded process cannot have its address space replaced out from under threads that are still executing in it. io_uring_task_cancel() and unshare_files() address the same class of problem for asynchronous I/O and shared descriptor tables: anything holding a reference into the old address space must be stopped or detached before that space disappears.

The address-space swap itself is exec_mmap(), and its middle section is one of the more delicate passages in the kernel. 

/* fs/exec.c — exec_mmap(), abbreviated */
	task_lock(tsk);
	membarrier_exec_mmap(mm);

	local_irq_disable();
	active_mm = tsk->active_mm;
	tsk->active_mm = mm;
	tsk->mm = mm;
	mm_init_cid(mm, tsk);
	/*
	 * This prevents preemption while active_mm is being loaded and
	 * it and mm are being updated, which could cause problems for
	 * lazy tlb mm refcounting when these are updated by context
	 * switches. Not all architectures can handle irqs off over
	 * activate_mm yet.
	 */
	if (!IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_ARCH_WANT_IRQS_OFF_ACTIVATE_MM))
		local_irq_enable();
	activate_mm(active_mm, mm);
	if (IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_ARCH_WANT_IRQS_OFF_ACTIVATE_MM))
		local_irq_enable();
	lru_gen_add_mm(mm);
	task_unlock(tsk);

Interrupts are disabled across the pointer update because tsk->mm and tsk->active_mm must change together. A context switch observing them out of step would mis-account the lazy-TLB reference that the scheduler maintains on behalf of kernel threads, and the resulting leaked or dropped reference would surface much later as a corrupted mm_struct. The two IS_ENABLED() arms exist because architectures disagree about whether activate_mm() itself may run with interrupts off — on those that cannot, interrupts are re-enabled first and the window is kept as narrow as the architecture allows. activate_mm() is where the new page-table root actually reaches the hardware: on x86_64 that is a write to CR3, on ARM64 a write to TTBR0_EL1 together with the new ASID.

Three cleanup steps around it are worth naming because each corresponds to a subsystem that had attached state to the old address space. membarrier_exec_mmap() resets the membarrier registration, since expedited membarrier state describes a set of CPUs running a particular mm and that set is now void. mm_init_cid() reinitialises the per-mm concurrency IDs used by rseq. And lru_gen_add_mm() enrolls the new mm in MGLRU’s per-address-space page-table walk list, replacing the old one. Finally, setmax_mm_hiwater_rss() folds the departing address space’s peak RSS into the signal-wide maximum before mmput(old_mm) drops the last reference — which is how getrusage() can still report the high-water mark of a program that has already been replaced.

The detailed ELF-loading story belongs in Chapter 20, where executable formats, interpreters, and segment mapping rules are the main topic. From the perspective of this chapter, the key fact is that execve() replaces the VMA topology wholesale. The new mm_struct receives fresh VMAs for text, rodata, data, BSS, heap base, stack, shared libraries, and architecture support pages. The old maple tree and page tables are not incrementally edited into this shape; they are discarded as a unit.

That clean replacement model is one reason execve() integrates well with ASLR. The new image can choose a fresh randomized layout for its stack, PIE text, mmap base, and heap base without preserving accidental spatial relationships from the old program. setup_new_exec() is where those decisions are recorded — it sets mm->task_size for the personality being adopted and calls arch_pick_mmap_layout() to establish the mmap_base from which every subsequent anonymous mapping will be allocated, applying the entropy described in Section 4. Fork clones an existing topology; exec constructs a new one. The two operations therefore have very different MM costs and invariants.

Exit — Destroying an Address Space

Address-space destruction is the final act in the lifecycle, and in many ways the most complex. While fork copies and exec replaces, exit must account for and release every resource that was attached to the address space over its entire lifetime — and it must do so in an order that satisfies the dependencies between subsystems. Page tables cannot be freed before TLB entries are invalidated. File-backed VMAs cannot be released before dirty pages are accounted. Memcg charges must be decremented after the pages they track are actually freed. Reverse-mapping chains must be torn down before the folios they reference become unreachable. Getting this ordering wrong leads to use-after-free bugs, resource leaks, or accounting inconsistencies that only manifest under specific timing conditions.

The destruction path also has an important distinction: it only runs when the last user of the address space exits. In a multi-threaded process, many tasks share the same mm_struct. Each exiting thread decrements mm_users via mmput(), but only the final decrement to zero triggers the actual teardown through __mmput() → exit_mmap(). Until that point, the address space remains fully live for the remaining threads.

The first step is not destruction at all but detachment. exit_mm() in kernel/exit.c severs the exiting task from its address space while leaving the address space itself intact. 

/* kernel/exit.c — exit_mm(), abbreviated */
	exit_mm_release(current, mm);
	if (!mm)
		return;
	mmap_read_lock(mm);
	mmgrab_lazy_tlb(mm);
	BUG_ON(mm != current->active_mm);
	/* more a memory barrier than a real lock */
	task_lock(current);
	smp_mb__after_spinlock();
	local_irq_disable();
	current->user_dumpable = (get_dumpable(mm) == SUID_DUMP_USER);
	current->mm = NULL;
	membarrier_update_current_mm(NULL);
	enter_lazy_tlb(mm, current);
	local_irq_enable();
	task_unlock(current);
	mmap_read_unlock(mm);
	mm_update_next_owner(mm);
	mmput(mm);

Three details deserve comment. exit_mm_release() handles the CLONE_CHILD_CLEARTID contract — it writes zero to the userspace TID address and wakes any futex waiters — and this must happen while the address space is still mapped, because it is a userspace write. mmgrab_lazy_tlb() then takes a structural reference (mm_count, not mm_users) before current->mm is cleared, because the task is about to become a lazy-TLB user of the address space: it no longer has a userspace mapping, but the CPU is still running on that mm’s page tables until the next context switch installs a different one. Dropping the last mm_users reference here can therefore free the VMAs and page contents, but it must not free the mm_struct or its top-level page-table page. Finally, the interrupt-disabled window around the current->mm = NULL assignment exists for the same reason as its counterpart in exec_mmap(): tsk->mm, the membarrier state, and the lazy-TLB state must appear to change atomically to any observer, and smp_mb__after_spinlock() supplies the ordering that membarrier_private_expedited() relies on when it scans for CPUs to IPI.

mm_update_next_owner() is a memcg concern. The mm->owner field identifies the task whose cgroup charges the address space; if that task is the one exiting, ownership must be handed to another thread that still uses the mm, or cleared if none remains.

mm_users versus mm_count

The two reference counts introduced in Section 2 govern the two halves of teardown, and it is worth restating the distinction here because destruction is where it becomes load-bearing. mm_users counts users of the mapped address space: threads with tsk->mm set, and transient holders such as get_task_mm() callers, ptrace, and /proc/pid/mem readers. mm_count counts references to the structure, and every nonzero mm_users collectively holds exactly one of them. When mm_users reaches zero the contents are destroyed; when mm_count reaches zero the container is freed by mmdrop(). A lazy-TLB CPU therefore keeps an mm_struct alive long after its last page has been freed, which is exactly what mmgrab_lazy_tlb() above arranges.

There is also an asynchronous variant. mmput_async() defers __mmput() to a workqueue and exists for callers who cannot afford to run an unbounded teardown inline — most importantly the OOM reaper, which must not block while holding locks that the victim’s own exit path may need.

__mmput() and the subsystem ordering

/* kernel/fork.c — __mmput() */
static inline void __mmput(struct mm_struct *mm)
{
	VM_BUG_ON(atomic_read(&mm->mm_users));

	uprobe_clear_state(mm);
	exit_aio(mm);
	ksm_exit(mm);
	khugepaged_exit(mm); /* must run before exit_mmap */
	exit_mmap(mm);
	mm_put_huge_zero_folio(mm);
	set_mm_exe_file(mm, NULL);
	if (!list_empty(&mm->mmlist)) {
		spin_lock(&mmlist_lock);
		list_del(&mm->mmlist);
		spin_unlock(&mmlist_lock);
	}
	if (mm->binfmt)
		module_put(mm->binfmt->module);
	lru_gen_del_mm(mm);
	futex_hash_free(mm);
	mmdrop(mm);
}

This short function is essentially a list of every subsystem that is allowed to attach state to an address space, unwound in dependency order. Each of the four calls that precede exit_mmap() removes a source of asynchronous activity against the mappings that are about to disappear: uprobe_clear_state() for installed userspace probes, exit_aio() for in-flight asynchronous I/O whose completion would otherwise write into vanishing pages, and ksm_exit() and khugepaged_exit() for the two background threads that scan address spaces for merge and collapse candidates. The in-tree comment on khugepaged_exit() is unusually explicit — if khugepaged were still able to find this mm on its scan list while exit_mmap() is dismantling it, the collapse path would walk freed VMAs.

The tail is symmetric with mm_init(). set_mm_exe_file(mm, NULL) drops the reference on the executable file that /proc/pid/exe resolves; the mmlist removal takes the address space off the global list that the swapper walks; module_put() releases the binary format handler, which may be a module that is now free to unload; and futex_hash_free() releases the private futex hash table. Only then does mmdrop() release the structural reference taken at allocation.

exit_mmap() and the two-phase lock dance

/* mm/mmap.c — exit_mmap(), abbreviated */
	/* mm's last user has gone, and its about to be pulled down */
	mmu_notifier_release(mm);

	mmap_read_lock(mm);
	arch_exit_mmap(mm);

	vma = vma_next(&vmi);
	if (!vma) {
		/* Can happen if dup_mmap() received an OOM */
		mmap_read_unlock(mm);
		mmap_write_lock(mm);
		goto destroy;
	}

	unmap_all_init(&unmap, &vmi, vma);
	flush_cache_mm(mm);
	tlb_gather_mmu_fullmm(&tlb, mm);
	unmap_vmas(&tlb, &unmap);
	mmap_read_unlock(mm);

	/*
	 * Set MMF_OOM_SKIP to hide this task from the oom killer/reaper
	 * because the memory has been already freed.
	 */
	mm_flags_set(MMF_OOM_SKIP, mm);
	mmap_write_lock(mm);
	unmap.mm_wr_locked = true;
	mt_clear_in_rcu(&mm->mm_mt);
	unmap_pgtable_init(&unmap, &vmi);
	free_pgtables(&tlb, &unmap);
	tlb_finish_mmu(&tlb);

	nr_accounted = tear_down_vmas(mm, &vmi, vma, ULONG_MAX);

destroy:
	__mt_destroy(&mm->mm_mt);
	mmap_write_unlock(mm);
	vm_unacct_memory(nr_accounted);

The most striking feature of this function is that it takes the mmap_lock twice, first for read and then for write, and does the bulk of the unmapping under the read lock. Nobody can be faulting on this address space — its last user is gone — so the lock is not protecting against page faults at all. It is taken to satisfy lockdep and the assertions embedded throughout the unmap machinery, and it is taken for read during the expensive phase so that the OOM reaper, which walks victim address spaces under mmap_read_trylock(), can still make progress concurrently instead of giving up on a process that is already freeing its memory. The upgrade to a write lock happens only for the page-table free and the final VMA walk, where the maple tree structure itself is modified.

Between the two phases sits mm_flags_set(MMF_OOM_SKIP, mm). Once unmap_vmas() has returned, the pages are gone, so the OOM killer must stop considering this task a worthwhile victim — reaping it again would find nothing to free while the reaper spun on a task that is already dead.

Three helpers deserve names. unmap_all_init() populates a struct unmap_desc, the descriptor introduced to replace the long argument lists that unmap_vmas() and free_pgtables() used to carry; the mm_wr_locked field being set between phases is how the second phase tells the page-table walk that it now holds the stronger lock. tlb_gather_mmu_fullmm() selects the whole-address-space flavour of the TLB gather, which lets architectures issue a single full flush rather than tracking individual ranges — on ARM64 this becomes an ASID-wide invalidation, on x86_64 the entire mm is simply dropped from the CPU’s tracking. And tear_down_vmas() in mm/mmap.c performs the final walk with preemption enabled, calling vma_mark_detached() and remove_vma() on each region — which is where vm_ops->close() runs, file references are dropped, anon_vma chains are unlinked, and userfaultfd contexts are released — while accumulating the VM_ACCOUNT page count that the closing vm_unacct_memory() returns to the system commit charge.

The empty-tree shortcut at the top is not a micro-optimization but a correctness case. If dup_mmap() hit an allocation failure part-way through fork, the child is left with an mm_struct whose maple tree may contain nothing at all, and exit_mmap() must tolerate that rather than dereference a null first VMA.

The ordering across the whole path matters, and each constraint traces back to a subsystem. Page-table teardown must follow TLB invalidation, or a CPU could still translate through a freed page-table page. File-backed writable mappings need their dirty accounting settled before the file reference is dropped in remove_vma(). Userfaultfd observers must see the disappearance of the ranges they registered before the contexts are released. Shared memory and memcg accounting must be decremented in a way that keeps global and cgroup totals consistent. Address-space destruction is therefore much more than “free the tree nodes.” It is the global cleanup point for every subsystem that attached policy or ownership to VMAs and PTEs.

Heap Management (brk)

The brk() system call is one of the oldest memory management interfaces in Unix, dating back to the original PDP-11 Unix where it was the only way for a process to obtain dynamic memory. The call adjusts the "program break" — the address of the first byte beyond the process's data segment. Moving the break upward grows the heap; moving it downward shrinks it. Despite its simplicity, brk() remains relevant in modern Linux because every major C library allocator (glibc's ptmalloc, musl's mallocng, jemalloc, tcmalloc) still uses it as the primary backing mechanism for small to medium-sized allocations, typically those below 128 KB (the MMAP_THRESHOLD in glibc).

The division of labor between brk() and mmap() in a modern allocator is a pragmatic engineering trade-off. The heap managed by brk() is a single contiguous region that can only grow or shrink from one end — like a stack. This makes it ideal for the common pattern of many small, short-lived allocations: the allocator maintains free lists within the heap arena and can return memory to the OS by lowering the break when trailing blocks are freed. Large allocations, on the other hand, go through mmap(MAP_ANONYMOUS), which can be individually unmapped without affecting other allocations. The kernel does not know or care about this user-space policy — from its perspective, both paths create or extend VMAs and demand-fault pages through the same machinery described in Sections 5 and 6.

That asymmetry has a consequence programmers meet in practice. Because the break is a single frontier, a live allocation anywhere near the top pins everything below it: freeing a 4 MB buffer in the middle of the heap returns nothing to the kernel, and glibc will only lower the break once the free block adjoining the top exceeds M_TRIM_THRESHOLD. Long-running processes whose RSS never falls back after a burst of allocation are usually observing exactly this, not a leak. The equivalent mmap()-backed allocation would have been released at free() time.

Where the Heap Begins

Two fields in mm_struct describe the heap, and the distinction between them is easy to miss. mm->start_brk is the fixed lower bound, chosen once when the program image is loaded. mm->brk is the moving frontier that the system call reports and adjusts. The heap VMA grows and shrinks between them; the base remembers where the traditional data-segment heap originally began, and nothing in the brk() path ever moves it.

The placement decision belongs to the binary format handler, not to the memory manager. For ELF binaries it is made in fs/binfmt_elf.c, in a passage the source itself annotates as "brk" handling. 

/* fs/binfmt_elf.c — choosing start_brk */
	if (!IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_COMPAT_BRK) &&
	    IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_ARCH_HAS_ELF_RANDOMIZE) &&
	    elf_ex->e_type == ET_DYN && !interpreter) {
		elf_brk = ELF_ET_DYN_BASE;
		/* This counts as moving the brk, so let brk(2) know. */
		brk_moved = true;
	}
	mm->start_brk = mm->brk = ELF_PAGEALIGN(elf_brk);

	if ((current->flags & PF_RANDOMIZE) && snapshot_randomize_va_space > 1) {
		/*
		 * If we didn't move the brk to ELF_ET_DYN_BASE (above),
		 * leave a gap between .bss and brk.
		 */
		if (!brk_moved)
			mm->brk = mm->start_brk = mm->brk + PAGE_SIZE;

		mm->brk = mm->start_brk = arch_randomize_brk(mm);
		brk_moved = true;
	}
