Three things happen here, in order of increasing hardening. The default is that the heap begins immediately after the BSS, which is the classic Unix layout. The first block covers a case peculiar to modern toolchains: a static PIE — an ET_DYN object with no interpreter — is loaded into the mmap() region, so a heap growing up from its BSS would run into shared mappings and the VDSO almost immediately. Such binaries get their break relocated wholesale to ELF_ET_DYN_BASE, an otherwise unused part of the address space. The second block adds a one-page gap between .bss and the break, which is cheap insurance against a buffer overrun in a global array running straight into heap metadata, and then randomizes the base outright.

arch_randomize_brk() is where the two example architectures diverge, though less than one might expect. x86_64 supplies its own version in arch/x86/kernel/process.c; ARM64 uses the generic one in mm/util.c, which it inherits by selecting ARCH_WANT_DEFAULT_TOPDOWN_MMAP_LAYOUT. Both compute the same thing: 

/* arch/x86/kernel/process.c */
unsigned long arch_randomize_brk(struct mm_struct *mm)
{
	if (mmap_is_ia32())
		return randomize_page(mm->brk, SZ_32M);

	return randomize_page(mm->brk, SZ_1G);
}

A 64-bit process therefore gets up to 1 GB of entropy on the heap base and a 32-bit compat process up to 32 MB, in both cases page-granular via randomize_page(). This matters because the heap has historically been the most attractive target for exploitation: heap grooming attacks depend on the attacker being able to predict where allocator metadata will land, and a randomized base defeats the naive form of that.

The escape hatch is CONFIG_COMPAT_BRK, which exists for a small set of very old binaries — notably some libc5-era programs — that assume the break sits exactly at the end of the data segment. Its effect is recorded per task in current->brk_randomized, and the system call consults that flag rather than the config option directly, so that setting randomize_va_space to 2 at runtime can still override a kernel built with the compatibility option enabled.

The brk() System Call

The kernel interface is brk(), implemented in mm/mmap.c. It is short enough to read in full, and the order of its checks is more interesting than any individual one. 

/* mm/mmap.c — SYSCALL_DEFINE1(brk), comments abridged */
	if (mmap_write_lock_killable(mm))
		return -EINTR;

	origbrk = mm->brk;

	min_brk = mm->start_brk;
#ifdef CONFIG_COMPAT_BRK
	if (!current->brk_randomized)
		min_brk = mm->end_data;
#endif
	if (brk < min_brk)
		goto out;

	/*
	 * Check against rlimit here. If this check is done later after the test
	 * of oldbrk with newbrk then it can escape the test and let the data
	 * segment grow beyond its set limit the in case where the limit is
	 * not page aligned -Ram Gupta
	 */
	if (check_data_rlimit(rlimit(RLIMIT_DATA), brk, mm->start_brk,
			      mm->end_data, mm->start_data))
		goto out;

	newbrk = PAGE_ALIGN(brk);
	oldbrk = PAGE_ALIGN(mm->brk);
	if (oldbrk == newbrk) {
		mm->brk = brk;
		goto success;
	}
	...
success:
	mmap_write_unlock(mm);
success_unlocked:
	userfaultfd_unmap_complete(mm, &uf);
	if (populate)
		mm_populate(oldbrk, newbrk - oldbrk);
	return brk;

out:
	mm->brk = origbrk;
	mmap_write_unlock(mm);
	return origbrk;

Start with the error convention, because it surprises people who expect a system call to return a negative errno. brk() does not report failure that way: on any error it restores mm->brk = origbrk and returns the unchanged break. A caller therefore detects failure by comparing the return value against what it asked for, which is exactly what the glibc wrapper does before setting errno to ENOMEM itself. The only exception is -EINTR from mmap_write_lock_killable(), returned before origbrk has even been read, when a fatal signal arrives while the task waits for the lock.

The rlimit check carries a comment with an attribution and a date-stamp of sorts, and it is worth reading closely because it documents a real bug. RLIMIT_DATA is enforced on the requested break, before the page alignment and before the oldbrk == newbrk fast path. Had it been done afterwards, a process whose limit was not page-aligned could creep past it: each request that rounds to the same page appears to be a no-op to the alignment test, yet mm->brk is still updated to the unaligned value on the way out. check_data_rlimit() in include/linux/mm.h is a two-line inline that charges (new - start_brk) + (end_data - start_data) against the limit and returns -ENOSPC — note that it counts the initialized data segment as well as the heap, which is why the limit is documented as applying to the data segment and not to the heap alone.

The fast path deserves a note of its own. Because the break is page-aligned before comparison, a request that stays within the current page updates mm->brk and returns without touching a single VMA. This is not an edge case: it is the common case. An allocator asking for a few hundred bytes at a time hits it repeatedly, and the whole call reduces to a lock acquisition and two arithmetic operations.

That lock is the last structural point. Because brk() may modify the VMA topology, it takes mmap_lock in write mode for the entire operation, which blocks the per-VMA-lock fast path for faults anywhere in the address space (Section 6.3). In practice this is rarely a bottleneck, precisely because user-space allocators batch many small allocations between successive break movements. The _killable variant is used so that a process waiting behind a long-running mmap() or munmap() can still be killed.

Growing the Heap

Growth is the path with real work in it, and it is gated by three separate checks before anything is allocated. 

/* mm/mmap.c — the growth path */
	if (check_brk_limits(oldbrk, newbrk - oldbrk))
		goto out;

	/*
	 * Only check if the next VMA is within the stack_guard_gap of the
	 * expansion area
	 */
	vma_iter_init(&vmi, mm, oldbrk);
	next = vma_find(&vmi, newbrk + PAGE_SIZE + stack_guard_gap);
	if (next && newbrk + PAGE_SIZE > vm_start_gap(next))
		goto out;

	brkvma = vma_prev_limit(&vmi, mm->start_brk);
	/* Ok, looks good - let it rip. */
	if (do_brk_flags(&vmi, brkvma, oldbrk, newbrk - oldbrk, 0) < 0)
		goto out;

	mm->brk = brk;
	if (mm->def_flags & VM_LOCKED)
		populate = true;

check_brk_limits() is easy to misread as an rlimit check — it is not; the data rlimit was already handled above. It calls get_unmapped_area(NULL, addr, len, 0, MAP_FIXED) to give the architecture a chance to veto the range, which is how per-architecture rules such as the x86_64 47-bit address-space cutoff or ARM64's 52-bit-with-LVA layout are applied uniformly, and then mlock_future_ok() to enforce RLIMIT_MEMLOCK when the address space has VM_LOCKED in mm->def_flags — that is, when the process has called mlockall(MCL_FUTURE). Failure there yields -EAGAIN.

The guard-gap check is the modern answer to a class of attack that motivated the Stack Clash work discussed in Chapter 20. The heap grows up and the stack grows down, and if the two are allowed to become adjacent, an attacker who can drive one of them can make a write intended for the heap land in the stack, or the reverse. The kernel therefore refuses to bring the break within stack_guard_gap (256 pages by default, tunable through the stack_guard_gap boot parameter) of the next VMA's effective start. The extra + PAGE_SIZE in both the search and the comparison keeps one unmapped page above the break at all times, so a linear overrun off the top of the heap faults rather than silently reaching whatever is next. vm_start_gap() is what makes the test symmetrical: it returns the next VMA's start minus its own guard gap if that VMA is itself a downward-growing stack, so the two gaps do not have to be reasoned about separately.

Only then does vma_prev_limit() locate the existing heap VMA — bounded by start_brk, so a hole below the heap cannot be mistaken for it — and hand it to do_brk_flags(), which lives in mm/vma.c alongside the rest of the VMA manipulation code. 

/* mm/vma.c — do_brk_flags(), abbreviated */
	vm_flags |= VM_DATA_DEFAULT_FLAGS | VM_ACCOUNT | mm->def_flags;
	vm_flags = ksm_vma_flags(mm, NULL, vm_flags);
	if (!may_expand_vm(mm, vm_flags, len >> PAGE_SHIFT))
		return -ENOMEM;

	if (mm->map_count > sysctl_max_map_count)
		return -ENOMEM;

	if (security_vm_enough_memory_mm(mm, len >> PAGE_SHIFT))
		return -ENOMEM;

	/*
	 * Expand the existing vma if possible; Note that singular lists do not
	 * occur after forking, so the expand will only happen on new VMAs.
	 */
	if (vma && vma->vm_end == addr) {
		VMG_STATE(vmg, mm, vmi, addr, addr + len, vm_flags, PHYS_PFN(addr));

		vmg.prev = vma;
		/* vmi is positioned at prev, which this mode expects. */
		vmg.just_expand = true;

		if (vma_merge_new_range(&vmg))
			goto out;
		else if (vmg_nomem(&vmg))
			goto unacct_fail;
	}

The flag composition on the first line is the whole reason the heap behaves the way it does. Both x86_64 and ARM64 define VM_DATA_DEFAULT_FLAGS as VM_DATA_FLAGS_TSK_EXEC, which supplies VM_READ | VM_WRITE and the three VM_MAY* variants but adds VM_EXEC only for a task whose personality carries READ_IMPLIES_EXEC. That is what makes the heap non-executable by default under NX and PXN respectively, while still accommodating the handful of ancient binaries that expect otherwise. ARM64 adds one bit of its own, VM_MTE_ALLOWED, so heap pages are eligible for Memory Tagging when the process enables it. VM_ACCOUNT enrolls the new pages in the strict-overcommit accounting of Section 4, which is why security_vm_enough_memory_mm() can refuse the growth outright when vm.overcommit_memory is 2. mm->def_flags folds in mlockall(MCL_FUTURE). And ksm_vma_flags() adds VM_MERGEABLE when the process has opted into KSM system-wide through prctl(PR_SET_MEMORY_MERGE), so heap growth automatically joins the merge candidate set.

The vma->vm_end == addr case is the one that fires almost every time: the heap VMA already ends exactly at the old break, so the merge machinery is invoked in just_expand mode, which skips the usual compatibility tests and simply moves the existing VMA's vm_end upward. No new vm_area_struct is allocated and the maple tree entry is updated in place. The fallback path below it — vm_area_alloc(), vma_set_anonymous(), vma_set_range(), vma_start_write(), vma_iter_store_gfp() — only runs the first time the heap is touched, or after a fork() has left the child with a VMA that cannot be extended in place.

On success the accounting is updated in three places at once: mm->total_vm, mm->data_vm, and, when locking is in force, mm->locked_vm. data_vm is the one exposed as VmData in /proc/pid/status, and keeping it in step here is what makes RLIMIT_DATA enforcement coherent across brk() and private writable mmap() alike. perf_event_mmap() emits the record that lets a profiler resolve later samples in the grown range.

Two effects land after the lock is dropped. userfaultfd_unmap_complete() delivers any queued UFFD_EVENT_UNMAP notifications to a registered manager, and if MCL_FUTURE is in force, mm_populate() faults in the whole new range immediately rather than leaving it to demand paging — which is the point of mlockall(), and also why a brk() call in such a process can be dramatically more expensive than usual.

Shrinking the Heap

/* mm/mmap.c — the shrink path */
	/* Always allow shrinking brk. */
	if (brk <= mm->brk) {
		/* Search one past newbrk */
		vma_iter_init(&vmi, mm, newbrk);
		brkvma = vma_find(&vmi, oldbrk);
		if (!brkvma || brkvma->vm_start >= oldbrk)
			goto out; /* mapping intersects with an existing non-brk vma. */
		/*
		 * mm->brk must be protected by write mmap_lock.
		 * do_vmi_align_munmap() will drop the lock on success,  so
		 * update it before calling do_vma_munmap().
		 */
		mm->brk = brk;
		if (do_vmi_align_munmap(&vmi, brkvma, mm, newbrk, oldbrk, &uf,
					/* unlock = */ true))
			goto out;

		goto success_unlocked;
	}

The comment says shrinking is always allowed, and as a matter of policy that is true — the kernel never refuses to give memory back on resource grounds. It can still fail on structural ones. If no VMA covers the range being surrendered, or if the VMA found there starts at or above the old break (meaning something other than the heap occupies the interval, typically because the process mapped over its own heap with MAP_FIXED), the call bails out with the break unchanged. So the guarantee is narrower than it looks: shrinking is never refused for being too aggressive, but it is refused when the heap is not where the kernel expects it.

The most subtle line is mm->brk = brk placed before the unmap. do_vmi_align_munmap() is called with unlock = true, meaning it releases mmap_lock itself on the success path — an optimization that lets the expensive page-table teardown and TLB shootdown happen without blocking other threads longer than necessary. Since mm->brk may only be written under the write lock, it has to be updated while that lock is still held. Hence the separate success_unlocked label: the ordinary exit path unlocks and falls through, whereas the shrink path arrives with the lock already gone.

From there, shrinking is an ordinary aligned unmap of the surplus tail: VMAs are split or removed, page tables are freed, TLBs are shot down, VM_ACCOUNT pages are returned to the commit charge, and vm_ops->close() runs for anything that had one. Everything described in Section 5 applies unchanged. This is the design point worth carrying away from the whole section: even the venerable program break is expressed as interval growth and interval deletion over the same generalized VMA machinery as everything else in the address space.

Table 9-18. Why brk() Can Fail
ConditionCheckResult
