Data-segment limit exceededcheck_data_rlimit(), RLIMIT_DATAold break returned
Architecture rejects the rangeget_unmapped_area() in check_brk_limits()old break returned
MCL_FUTURE would exceed RLIMIT_MEMLOCKmlock_future_ok()old break returned
Would enter the next VMA's guard gapvm_start_gap(), stack_guard_gapold break returned
Address-space or map-count limitmay_expand_vm(), sysctl_max_map_countold break returned
Strict overcommit refuses the chargesecurity_vm_enough_memory_mm()old break returned
Allocation failure building the VMAvm_area_alloc(), vma_iter_store_gfp()old break returned
Something other than the heap occupies the tailvma_find() on the shrink pathold break returned

The Kernel-Internal Break: vm_brk_flags()

There is a second entry point that never reaches userspace. vm_brk_flags() in mm/mmap.c creates an anonymous mapping at a caller-chosen address using the same do_brk_flags() back end, and exists for in-kernel users that need a zero-filled region during program loading. The ELF loader is the main one: when a data segment's memory size exceeds its file size, the trailing BSS is not backed by the file at all, so elf_load() calls vm_brk_flags() to cover the remainder.

Two differences from the system call are worth noting. It refuses any flag except VM_EXEC — the loader needs that one for the rare executable BSS in an old binary, and everything else would be a caller bug. And it calls do_vmi_munmap() first, so an existing mapping at the target address is replaced rather than treated as a collision, which is what makes it usable while an image is still being assembled. The lock handling, userfaultfd_unmap_complete(), and mm_populate() tail mirror the system call exactly.

The difference between start_brk and brk is easy to miss but important. start_brk is the lower bound fixed when the program image is established. brk is the moving frontier exposed through the syscall. The heap VMA may grow and shrink around that frontier, but the base remembers where the traditional data-segment heap originally began.

User-space allocators explain why this interface survives. A library such as glibc can use brk() for smaller expandable heap allocations while switching to mmap() for larger or alignment-sensitive requests. That hybrid strategy reduces fragmentation pressure on the traditional data segment while preserving a contiguous heap for common small allocations. From the kernel's perspective, however, both strategies converge on the same VMA and page-fault machinery described elsewhere in this chapter.

Other Address Space Operations

Sections 5 through 8 covered the primary lifecycle of a mapping: creating intervals with mmap(), destroying them with munmap(), resolving faults, and managing the overall address space through fork/exec/exit. But real applications spend a significant portion of their time modifying existing mappings rather than creating or removing them. A database engine might grow a shared file mapping with mremap(). A JIT compiler might toggle execution permissions with mprotect() as it compiles hot loops. A garbage collector might use madvise(MADV_DONTNEED) to discard pages it knows are dead, or madvise(MADV_HUGEPAGE) to request transparent huge pages for a nursery generation. A real-time audio thread might call mlockall(MCL_CURRENT | MCL_FUTURE) to guarantee that no page fault will ever stall its processing loop.

These operations share a common pattern at the kernel level: they do not create or destroy VMAs as their primary action — they transform existing ones. That transformation often requires the split-modify-merge cycle discussed in Section 3.4: if the caller's address range does not align with existing VMA boundaries, the kernel must split the affected VMA, apply the change to the relevant sub-interval, and then attempt to merge compatible neighbors to keep the VMA count low. This is why every operation in this section interacts heavily with the maple tree, the VMA merging logic, and the page-table update machinery.

Read at the level of the source, all four have converged on a common shape in 7.0.12, and recognizing it makes each one shorter to learn. Every one of them strips any pointer tag from the incoming address with untagged_addr(), so that an ARM64 caller using Top-Byte-Ignore or MTE tagged pointers is not rejected by the range checks. Every one of them takes mmap_lock with the _killable variant, so a process waiting behind a slow operation elsewhere in the address space remains killable. Every one of them iterates the affected range VMA by VMA with for_each_vma_range() or an explicit vma_find() loop, and every one of them funnels its actual flag change through vma_modify_flags() — the thin wrapper over vma_modify() introduced in Section 3 that does the splitting and merging so the callers do not have to. The differences between the four are almost entirely in which checks they apply before that call and what they do to page tables after it.

mremap() — Remapping Memory Regions

mremap() is the only POSIX-adjacent system call that can change the virtual address of an existing mapping without destroying and re-creating it. This is a surprisingly powerful operation: it can grow a mapping in place if adjacent address space is available, shrink it by trimming the tail, or relocate it entirely to a new address while preserving the underlying physical pages and file offsets. The primary consumer is realloc() in user-space allocators — when a large mmap'd allocation needs to grow, mremap() can often expand it without copying any data at all, turning an O(n) memory copy into an O(1) page-table update.

The implementation in mm/mremap.c was restructured around a single descriptor, and the system-call entry point does almost nothing but fill it in. 

/* mm/mremap.c — SYSCALL_DEFINE5(mremap) */
	struct vm_userfaultfd_ctx uf = NULL_VM_UFFD_CTX;
	LIST_HEAD(uf_unmap_early);
	LIST_HEAD(uf_unmap);
	/*
	 * There is a deliberate asymmetry here: we strip the pointer tag
	 * from the old address but leave the new address alone. This is
	 * for consistency with mmap(), where we prevent the creation of
	 * aliasing mappings in userspace by leaving the tag bits of the
	 * mapping address intact. A non-zero tag will cause the subsequent
	 * range checks to reject the address as invalid.
	 *
	 * See Documentation/arch/arm64/tagged-address-abi.rst for more
	 * information.
	 */
	struct vma_remap_struct vrm = {
		.addr = untagged_addr(addr),
		.old_len = old_len,
		.new_len = new_len,
		.flags = flags,
		.new_addr = new_addr,

		.uf = &uf,
		.uf_unmap_early = &uf_unmap_early,
		.uf_unmap = &uf_unmap,

		.remap_type = MREMAP_INVALID, /* We set later. */
	};

	return do_mremap(&vrm);

struct vma_remap_struct — vrm throughout the file — is worth noticing as a design pattern rather than as a data structure. mremap() has to thread roughly a dozen values through a call chain half a dozen frames deep: the two lengths, the two addresses, the flags, the located VMA, three separate userfaultfd unmap lists, whether the lock is still held, whether the expansion must be populated afterwards, how many pages are charged. Bundling them means each helper can both read and update the shared state, which is what allows the lock-dropping optimizations described below to be expressed at all. The same idea appears as struct unmap_desc in the exit path and struct vma_merge_struct in the merge code; it is now the house style for multi-stage VMA operations.

The comment above the initializer is one of the few places in the memory manager where an ARM64 ABI decision is spelled out in full. Under the tagged-address ABI a userspace pointer may carry a non-zero top byte, and system calls that consume such a pointer must ignore it. But mremap()'s new_addr is not consumed, it is a request for where a mapping should live, and mappings are always created at untagged addresses. Silently stripping the tag would let a program create an alias — the same mapping reachable through two pointer values that compare unequal — so instead the tag is left in place, the range check sees a wildly out-of-range address, and the call fails. Rejecting is the safe direction. 

/* mm/mremap.c — do_mremap() */
	vrm->old_len = PAGE_ALIGN(vrm->old_len);
	vrm->new_len = PAGE_ALIGN(vrm->new_len);

	res = check_mremap_params(vrm);
	if (res)
		return res;

	if (mmap_write_lock_killable(mm))
		return -EINTR;
	vrm->mmap_locked = true;

	if (vrm_move_only(vrm)) {
		res = remap_move(vrm);
	} else {
		vrm->vma = vma_lookup(current->mm, vrm->addr);
		res = check_prep_vma(vrm);
		if (res)
			goto out;

		/* Actually execute mremap. */
		res = vrm_implies_new_addr(vrm) ? mremap_to(vrm) : mremap_at(vrm);
	}

out:
	failed = IS_ERR_VALUE(res);

	if (vrm->mmap_locked)
		mmap_write_unlock(mm);

	/* VMA mlock'd + was expanded, so populated expanded region. */
	if (!failed && vrm->populate_expand)
		mm_populate(vrm->new_addr + vrm->old_len, vrm->delta);

	notify_uffd(vrm, failed);
	return res;

The dispatch has three arms. vrm_move_only() is the degenerate case where the length does not change and only the address does; remap_move() handles it directly. Otherwise the VMA is located and check_prep_vma() validates it, after which vrm_implies_new_addr() decides between mremap_to() — the caller nominated a destination, via MREMAP_FIXED or MREMAP_DONTUNMAP — and mremap_at(), which tries to do the work where the mapping already is.

mremap_at() then resolves to one of four remap_type values, and the classification is the clearest statement of what the call actually costs. MREMAP_NO_RESIZE returns immediately. MREMAP_SHRINK is always possible in place and reduces to unmapping the tail, exactly as brk()'s shrink path does — and, like it, calls shrink_vma() with the lock-dropping flag set. MREMAP_EXPAND calls expand_vma(), which first attempts a genuine in-place extension and only falls back to a move if that fails and MREMAP_MAYMOVE was supplied. This is the distinction that matters to a realloc() implementation: the in-place expansion is a maple-tree update and nothing else, whereas the fallback move is proportional to the size of the region's page tables.

move_vma() is where a genuine relocation happens, and its structure repays a close look. prep_move_vma() flushes caches and takes the necessary locks; vrm_calc_charge() determines the commit charge for the new location, since a moved VM_ACCOUNT region is briefly charged twice; vma_start_write() excludes concurrent per-VMA-lock faults for the duration, because a fault resolving against the old page tables while they are being moved would produce a mapping that belongs to neither location. copy_vma_and_data() then creates the destination VMA and calls move_page_tables(), which relocates entries at the largest granularity it can — whole PMD or even PUD entries where alignment permits, falling back to per-PTE copying otherwise. That is why an aligned, huge-page-backed region moves dramatically faster than an unaligned one.

The failure handling deserves a sentence of its own because it is unusual. If copy_vma_and_data() fails after establishing the new VMA, the code does not unwind; it points the descriptor at the new location and unmaps that instead, so the process ends up with the mapping at one address or the other but never at both and never at neither.

The flag combinations express the contract clearly. MREMAP_MAYMOVE allows relocation if in-place work is impossible; without it, a request that cannot be satisfied where the mapping already lives simply fails. MREMAP_FIXED lets user space nominate the destination explicitly and implies MREMAP_MAYMOVE. MREMAP_DONTUNMAP is the newest and least obvious: the mapping is established at the destination but the source range is left mapped, as an empty region that will fault afresh. Its consumer is userfaultfd-driven live migration and moving garbage collectors — a manager can relocate a region's contents and then catch, rather than merely observe, every subsequent access to the old range. dontunmap_complete() implements the exception, and the mutually exclusive normal path unmaps the source.

Two effects again land outside the lock. mm_populate() runs when an mlocked region was expanded, since locked memory must be resident by definition. And notify_uffd() drains the three separate userfaultfd lists the descriptor carries — uf, uf_unmap_early and uf_unmap — which exist because a single mremap() can generate a remap event, an early unmap at the destination, and a late unmap at the source, and a manager must see them in that order. It is called on both the success and failure paths, with failed passed in, because a partially completed remap has still changed the address space.

mprotect() — Changing Access Permissions

mprotect() is the interface through which user space changes the read, write, and execute permissions on an existing virtual address range after it has been mapped. This is fundamental to several important use cases: JIT compilers use it to toggle pages between writable (for code generation) and executable (for running the generated code), enforcing W^X (write XOR execute) security policy. Dynamic linkers use it to make .text segments read-only and .data segments writable after relocations are applied. Security hardening tools use it to implement guard pages and to remove write permission from sensitive data structures once initialization is complete.

There are two entry points and one implementation. SYSCALL_DEFINE3(mprotect) calls do_mprotect_pkey(start, len, prot, -1); SYSCALL_DEFINE4(pkey_mprotect) calls the same function with a real protection key. The -1 is the sentinel for "legacy mprotect(), do not touch the key", and it is worth knowing because it explains why the pkey machinery is invisible in the common path yet costs nothing to carry. Protection keys are an x86_64 feature (CONFIG_ARCH_HAS_PKEYS, backed by PKU); ARM64 does not implement them, so arch_override_mprotect_pkey() compiles away there.

do_mprotect_pkey() establishes the range, then walks it VMA by VMA. The loop body is a sequence of independent vetoes, and it is more instructive than any single line of it. 

/* mm/mprotect.c — do_mprotect_pkey(), the per-VMA loop, abbreviated */
	for_each_vma_range(vmi, vma, end) {
		...
		/* Does the application expect PROT_READ to imply PROT_EXEC */
		if (rier && (vma->vm_flags & VM_MAYEXEC))
			prot |= PROT_EXEC;

		mask_off_old_flags = VM_ACCESS_FLAGS | VM_FLAGS_CLEAR;

		new_vma_pkey = arch_override_mprotect_pkey(vma, prot, pkey);
		newflags = calc_vm_prot_bits(prot, new_vma_pkey);
		newflags |= (vma->vm_flags & ~mask_off_old_flags);

		/* newflags >> 4 shift VM_MAY% in place of VM_% */
		if ((newflags & ~(newflags >> 4)) & VM_ACCESS_FLAGS) {
			error = -EACCES;
			break;
		}

		if (map_deny_write_exec(vma->vm_flags, newflags)) {
			error = -EACCES;
			break;
		}

		/* Allow architectures to sanity-check the new flags */
		if (!arch_validate_flags(newflags)) {
			error = -EINVAL;
			break;
		}

		error = security_file_mprotect(vma, reqprot, prot);
		if (error)
			break;
		...
		error = mprotect_fixup(&vmi, &tlb, vma, &prev, nstart, tmp, newflags);
		if (error)
			break;
	}

The newflags >> 4 test is the one that puzzles every first-time reader, and it is the most important check in the loop. The VM_MAY* bits sit exactly four positions above their VM_* counterparts, so shifting right by four aligns "may I ever have this permission" with "do I have this permission now". The expression therefore asks: is any requested access bit set for which the corresponding may bit is clear? If so, -EACCES. This is what makes mmap(PROT_READ) on a file opened read-only permanently unwritable — the VM_MAYWRITE bit was never granted at map time, and no later mprotect() can conjure it. A single shift-and-mask enforces the entire "permissions can be narrowed but never widened beyond the original grant" rule.

mask_off_old_flags is the answer to a subtler question: what happens to permissions the caller did not mention? The comment in the source is explicit — each mprotect() call passes a complete r/w/x set, so any permission absent from prot must be cleared, not preserved. VM_FLAGS_CLEAR extends the same reset to the architecture-specific bits, ARCH_VM_PKEY_FLAGS and VM_ARCH_CLEAR, so a protection key or an ARM64 MTE bit does not survive a call that did not ask for it.

rier — "read implies exec, requested" — captures the legacy READ_IMPLIES_EXEC personality before the loop and re-applies prot from reqprot at the end of each iteration, so that the implicit PROT_EXEC is granted only for VMAs that carry VM_MAYEXEC rather than being smeared across the whole range.

map_deny_write_exec() is Memory-Deny-Write-Execute, enabled per process through prctl(PR_SET_MDWE) and recorded as MMF_HAS_MDWE. Under it the kernel refuses to produce a writable-and-executable mapping, and also refuses to make a previously writable mapping executable — which is the case that matters, since the classic JIT exploit primitive is to write shellcode into a writable page and then flip it to executable. A legitimate JIT under MDWE must instead keep two mappings of the same memory, one writable and one executable.

arch_validate_flags() is where ARM64 does real work while x86_64 does none. It refuses VM_MTE on a region that was not created with VM_MTE_ALLOWED, so Memory Tagging cannot be retrofitted onto a mapping whose backing store cannot carry tags, and it refuses VM_EXEC on a Guarded Control Stack region, an executable shadow stack being a contradiction. Its companion arch_validate_prot(), called once before the loop, is what makes PROT_MTE and PROT_BTI acceptable values of prot at all — and only on hardware that supports them, via system_supports_mte() and system_supports_bti().

security_file_mprotect() gives the LSM the original reqprot as well as the effective prot, so that a policy such as SELinux's execmem can distinguish what the program asked for from what the personality turned it into. And a VMA whose driver supplies vm_ops->mprotect gets the last word, which is how special mappings that cannot tolerate permission changes decline them.

Table 9-19. Vetoes Applied to Each VMA in an mprotect() Range
CheckRejectsError
vma_is_sealed()Any change to a range sealed with mseal()-EPERM
newflags & ~(newflags >> 4)A permission never granted at map time (VM_MAY* clear)-EACCES
map_deny_write_exec()Making a mapping executable under MDWE-EACCES
arch_validate_flags()ARM64: VM_MTE without VM_MTE_ALLOWED; executable GCS-EINVAL
arch_validate_prot()ARM64: PROT_MTE/PROT_BTI on hardware without them-EINVAL
security_file_mprotect()Whatever the active LSM policy forbidspolicy-defined
vm_ops->mprotectDriver-specific refusal for special mappingsdriver-defined
may_expand_vm()Turning a region into data past RLIMIT_AS or RLIMIT_DATA-ENOMEM
security_vm_enough_memory_mm()Making a private mapping writable under strict overcommit-ENOMEM
gap in the rangevma->vm_start != tmp, an unmapped hole-ENOMEM

Once a VMA passes every veto, mprotect_fixup() performs the change. 

/* mm/mprotect.c — mprotect_fixup(), abbreviated */
	if (vma_is_sealed(vma))
		return -EPERM;

	if (newflags == oldflags) {
		*pprev = vma;
		return 0;
	}
	...
	vma = vma_modify_flags(vmi, *pprev, vma, start, end, &newflags);
	if (IS_ERR(vma)) {
		error = PTR_ERR(vma);
		goto fail;
	}

	*pprev = vma;

	/*
	 * vm_flags and vm_page_prot are protected by the mmap_lock
	 * held in write mode.
	 */
	vma_start_write(vma);
	vm_flags_reset_once(vma, newflags);
	if (vma_wants_manual_pte_write_upgrade(vma))
		mm_cp_flags |= MM_CP_TRY_CHANGE_WRITABLE;
	vma_set_page_prot(vma);

	change_protection(tlb, vma, start, end, mm_cp_flags);

The commit accounting above this excerpt is asymmetric in a way the source comment admits to. Making a private mapping writable increases the commit charge, so VM_ACCOUNT is set and security_vm_enough_memory_mm() may refuse. Making it unwritable again generally cannot reduce the charge, because the kernel has no per-page record of what was already touched — the sole exception being an anonymous VMA that has not yet acquired an anon_vma, and is therefore provably untouched. A program that repeatedly flips a large private mapping between writable and read-only will therefore accumulate commit charge it never gets back.

vm_flags_reset_once() rather than plain vm_flags_reset() is deliberate: it writes the flags with a single store so that a reader running under the per-VMA read lock cannot observe a torn intermediate value. vma_set_page_prot() recomputes vma->vm_page_prot, the cached architecture-level protection that the fault handler stamps into new PTEs, and then change_protection() walks the existing page tables and rewrites the leaf entries that are already there. Both halves are necessary and neither is sufficient — vm_page_prot governs future faults, change_protection() governs pages already mapped.

MM_CP_TRY_CHANGE_WRITABLE is a small but visible optimization. When a mapping becomes writable, the kernel would normally leave PTEs read-only and let the first write take a fault so that dirty accounting and copy-on-write can be resolved. For VMAs where that is unnecessary — vma_wants_manual_pte_write_upgrade() identifies them — change_protection() may mark existing entries writable immediately, eliminating one fault per already-resident page. On a freshly mprotected multi-gigabyte region that is a large saving.

Two smaller behaviours complete the picture. A private VM_LOCKED mapping becoming writable triggers populate_vma_page_range() immediately, because a locked page that would take a copy-on-write fault on first write is not really locked. And PROT_GROWSDOWN or PROT_GROWSUP in prot, handled before the loop, extends the request to the whole of a stack VMA rather than the literal range given — the interface by which a program adjusts the protection of a growable stack whose current extent it does not know. Ranges sealed with mseal() are refused outright; see Section 2's table of address-space system calls.

madvise() — Memory Hints

madvise() is the most versatile of the address-space manipulation calls. Unlike mprotect() or mremap(), which change hard properties of a mapping (permissions, address), madvise() communicates soft policy — information about how the application intends to use a range, which the kernel can use to optimize its behavior. Some hints are truly advisory and may be ignored (like MADV_SEQUENTIAL, which adjusts readahead heuristics), but others have immediate and dramatic effects: MADV_DONTNEED discards pages immediately, MADV_FREE marks anonymous pages for lazy reclamation, and MADV_COLLAPSE triggers synchronous THP collapse. The name "advice" is therefore somewhat misleading — many of these operations are as consequential as munmap().

Because the vocabulary is so wide, mm/madvise.c classifies rather than special-cases. Every behaviour carries a required locking strength, and the enumeration of those strengths is the most useful single thing in the file. 

/* mm/madvise.c */
enum madvise_lock_mode {
	MADVISE_NO_LOCK,
	MADVISE_MMAP_READ_LOCK,
	MADVISE_MMAP_WRITE_LOCK,
	MADVISE_VMA_READ_LOCK,
};

The four levels correspond exactly to what a behaviour does. MADV_HWPOISON and MADV_SOFT_OFFLINE take no mmap_lock at all, because they act on a physical page rather than on the mapping. Behaviours that only walk page tables — MADV_DONTNEED, MADV_FREE, MADV_COLD, MADV_PAGEOUT — need the address space to be stable but do not change VMA structure, so a read lock suffices; several of them now qualify for MADVISE_VMA_READ_LOCK and never touch mmap_lock at all, which is what lets a garbage collector issue MADV_DONTNEED from one thread while another thread faults elsewhere in the same process. Only behaviours that change vm_flags — MADV_HUGEPAGE, MADV_MERGEABLE, MADV_DONTFORK and their inverses — require the write lock, because they may split or merge VMAs through madvise_update_vma().

That split is visible in the dispatcher too. madvise_vma_behavior() returns early for the behaviours with dedicated implementations — madvise_remove(), madvise_willneed(), madvise_cold(), madvise_pageout(), madvise_dontneed_free(), madvise_collapse(), madvise_guard_install(), madvise_guard_remove() — and only then falls through to a switch that computes new_flags and hands it to madvise_update_vma(), with a comment marking the boundary. Reading the file with that division in mind reduces roughly a thousand lines to two ideas.

Table 9-20. Key MADV_* Hints
Advice Intent Typical MM effect
MADV_NORMALReturn to default access assumptions.Clears VM_RAND_READ and VM_SEQ_READ.
MADV_RANDOMIndicate random access.Sets VM_RAND_READ; reduces readahead.
MADV_SEQUENTIALIndicate forward streaming access.Sets VM_SEQ_READ; increases readahead.
MADV_WILLNEEDRequest that data be brought in soon.Asynchronous readahead or swap-in; does not block.
MADV_DONTNEEDIndicate that current contents can be discarded.Zaps page tables; anonymous contents are lost, file pages re-read on next fault.
MADV_DONTNEED_LOCKEDAs above, but do not skip locked pages.Discards even VM_LOCKED ranges.
MADV_FREEMark anonymous pages reclaimable until reused.Lazy free: contents survive until pressure arrives, then vanish.
MADV_REMOVEPunch out backing store contents for supported files.Filesystem hole punching via fallocate(); shared writable file mappings only.
MADV_HUGEPAGEEncourage THP use.Sets VM_HUGEPAGE; eligible for khugepaged collapse.
MADV_NOHUGEPAGEDiscourage THP use.Sets VM_NOHUGEPAGE.
MADV_COLLAPSERequest immediate THP collapse where possible.Synchronous collapse in the calling thread; may drop the lock.
MADV_COLDMark pages as cold.Deactivates folios; demotes reclaim priority without freeing.
MADV_PAGEOUTPush pages toward reclaim or swap now.Actively reclaims eligible folios.
MADV_MERGEABLEAllow KSM merging.Sets VM_MERGEABLE; registers the mm with ksmd.
MADV_UNMERGEABLEDisable KSM merging.Clears VM_MERGEABLE and unmerges already-merged pages.
MADV_DONTFORKDo not inherit the range across fork().Sets VM_DONTCOPY; see Section 8.1.
MADV_DOFORKUndo MADV_DONTFORK.Clears VM_DONTCOPY.
MADV_WIPEONFORKGive the child a zeroed range instead of a copy.Sets VM_WIPEONFORK; anonymous private mappings only.
MADV_KEEPONFORKUndo MADV_WIPEONFORK.Clears VM_WIPEONFORK.
MADV_DONTDUMPExclude the range from core dumps.Sets VM_DONTDUMP; used for key material and huge mappings.
MADV_DODUMPUndo MADV_DONTDUMP.Clears VM_DONTDUMP.
MADV_POPULATE_READPrefault the range for reads.Populates page tables without a user access loop.
MADV_POPULATE_WRITEPrefault the range for writes.Populates writable pages, triggering CoW now rather than later.
MADV_GUARD_INSTALLMake access to the range fatal.Installs guard markers in the PTEs and sets VM_MAYBE_GUARD; no VMA split.
MADV_GUARD_REMOVEUndo a guard installation.Clears the markers; the range faults normally again.
MADV_HWPOISONPoison the backing page (testing).Acts on the physical page; requires CAP_SYS_ADMIN.
MADV_SOFT_OFFLINESoft-offline the backing page (testing).Migrates contents away and retires the page frame.

Guard regions deserve a note because they are the newest entry and they solve a problem the address space could not previously express cheaply. A thread-stack guard, or a red zone around an allocator's chunks, traditionally costs a PROT_NONE mapping — and therefore a VMA, and therefore a split of whatever it sits inside, with the max_map_count ceiling looming over any program that wants thousands of them. MADV_GUARD_INSTALL instead writes a special marker directly into the page tables and records only VMA_MAYBE_GUARD_BIT on the VMA, so an arbitrary number of guards can be installed inside a single mapping without fragmenting it. The corresponding VM_MAYBE_GUARD flag is sticky across merges, for the reason given in Section 3: the kernel cannot reconstruct which pages hold markers, so it must assume the union.

process_madvise() extends part of this vocabulary to remote address spaces reached through a pidfd, and its permission model is worth stating precisely because it is stricter than it first appears. The target is resolved with pidfd_get_task() and then mm_access(task, PTRACE_MODE_READ_FSCREDS) — deliberately a ptrace-strength check, because merely learning which ranges are mapped would leak ASLR placement. Beyond that, a remote caller needs CAP_SYS_NICE, and the behaviour itself must pass process_madvise_remote_valid(), which admits only non-destructive hints; there is no supported way to make another process discard its data. Both checks are skipped when mm == current->mm, which is how the vectored form is usable on oneself as a cheaper batch interface. That vectored form is the other half of the point: the advice arrives as an iovec array through import_iovec() and is applied by vector_madvise(), so a memory manager can retire hundreds of ranges in one system call. The address space remains the fundamental object; which task initiated the advice is secondary.

Memory Locking (mlock/mlockall)

Memory locking is the mechanism by which an application can guarantee that specific pages will never be swapped out or reclaimed under memory pressure. This guarantee is critical for real-time systems (audio processing, industrial control, trading systems) where a page fault that triggers disk I/O could cause unacceptable latency — potentially hundreds of milliseconds on spinning disks or tens of milliseconds even on SSDs. It is also essential for security-sensitive applications: cryptographic key material stored in locked pages cannot be written to swap, where it might persist on disk and be recoverable after the process exits.

Memory locking pins a mapping's contents in RAM so they are not reclaimed or swapped under normal pressure. At the VMA level, this is expressed with VM_LOCKED and related accounting in mm->locked_vm. At the page level, Linux faults pages in as needed and moves the resulting folios onto the unevictable LRU list described in Chapter 8, so the reclaim scanners never consider them.

There are three range-based entry points and they all reach do_mlock(). mlock() passes VM_LOCKED; mlock2() adds VM_LOCKONFAULT when given MLOCK_ONFAULT; munlock() takes a shorter path straight to apply_vma_lock_flags(start, len, 0), since removing a lock cannot fail on resource grounds. 

/* mm/mlock.c — do_mlock(), abbreviated */
	if (!can_do_mlock())
		return -EPERM;

	len = PAGE_ALIGN(len + (offset_in_page(start)));
	start &= PAGE_MASK;

	lock_limit = rlimit(RLIMIT_MEMLOCK);
	lock_limit >>= PAGE_SHIFT;
	locked = len >> PAGE_SHIFT;

	if (mmap_write_lock_killable(current->mm))
		return -EINTR;

	locked += current->mm->locked_vm;
	if ((locked > lock_limit) && (!capable(CAP_IPC_LOCK))) {
		/*
		 * It is possible that the regions requested intersect with
		 * previously mlocked areas, that part area in "mm->locked_vm"
		 * should not be counted to new mlock increment count. So check
		 * and adjust locked count if necessary.
		 */
		locked -= count_mm_mlocked_page_nr(current->mm,
				start, len);
	}

	/* check against resource limits */
	if ((locked <= lock_limit) || capable(CAP_IPC_LOCK))
		error = apply_vma_lock_flags(start, len, flags);

	mmap_write_unlock(current->mm);
	if (error)
		return error;

	error = __mm_populate(start, len, 0);
	if (error)
		return __mlock_posix_error_return(error);
	return 0;

The limit arithmetic contains a correction that is easy to overlook and easy to hit. A naive check adds the requested pages to mm->locked_vm and compares against the limit, which double-counts any part of the range that is already locked — so a program that calls mlock() twice on the same buffer would spuriously fail the second time. The kernel therefore only pays for the expensive walk in count_mm_mlocked_page_nr() when the naive sum has already exceeded the limit, and subtracts the overlap before deciding. Fast in the common case, correct in the awkward one.

can_do_mlock() is a separate gate from the limit: it returns true if RLIMIT_MEMLOCK is nonzero or the caller has CAP_IPC_LOCK, so a task with a zero limit and no capability is refused with -EPERM before any accounting happens at all. CAP_IPC_LOCK then bypasses the limit entirely, which is why privileged real-time processes can lock without tuning ulimit -l.

Note the ordering at the end: the flags are applied under the write lock, the lock is dropped, and only then does __mm_populate() fault the pages in. Population is by far the expensive part — it is a full fault per page, including reading from disk for file-backed ranges — and doing it outside the lock is what keeps a large mlock() from stalling every other thread in the process. __mlock_posix_error_return() exists because POSIX requires ENOMEM where the internal path would produce EFAULT.

mlock_fixup() is the per-VMA worker, and it declines more mappings than one might expect. vma_supports_mlock() filters out the special VMAs — VM_IO, VM_PFNMAP, hugetlb and similar — for which locking is meaningless because the pages were never reclaimable; the request succeeds and simply does nothing, rather than failing. vma_is_secretmem() is refused in the other direction: memfd_secret() memory is already unevictable and must never be unlocked. Everything else goes through vma_modify_flags() to split or merge as needed, updates mm->locked_vm by a signed delta, and calls mlock_vma_pages_range() — except in the case where the VMA was already locked and remains locked, where the flags are simply reset, since mlocking twice would corrupt the folio-level count.

mlockall() extends the concept from one range to a policy for the whole address space. MCL_CURRENT locks pages already mapped, by walking every VMA with for_each_vma() and calling mlock_fixup() on each. MCL_FUTURE instead sets VM_LOCKED in mm->def_flags, which is the mechanism by which every subsequently created mapping — including heap growth through brk(), as Section 9.3 described — is born locked. MCL_ONFAULT adds VM_LOCKONFAULT, asking Linux to defer the population cost until each page is touched: the pages become unevictable once resident, but nothing is prefaulted, which combines lock semantics with demand paging.

The flags do not accumulate. apply_mlockall_flags() begins by clearing VM_LOCKED_MASK from mm->def_flags unconditionally, so a second mlockall(MCL_CURRENT) after an earlier mlockall(MCL_CURRENT | MCL_FUTURE) silently cancels the future policy. Each call states the complete policy, exactly as each mprotect() call states the complete permission set. munlockall() is simply apply_mlockall_flags(0).

Because pinned memory is a scarce resource, RLIMIT_MEMLOCK constrains how much a non-privileged task may lock. The limit exists for the same reason Chapter 8 emphasized reclaimable versus unreclaimable memory: unevictable pages are pressure transferred to the rest of the system. Memory locking is therefore powerful, but intentionally bounded. It is also strictly per-address-space and does not survive process transitions: execve() discards the whole mm_struct along with its def_flags, and dup_mmap() explicitly clears VM_LOCKED_MASK from every child VMA, so a forked child inherits the parent's memory contents but none of its locks. A real-time process that forks must call mlockall() again in the child.

mincore() — Asking What Is Resident

One more call belongs in this section by neighbourhood rather than by shape. mincore(addr, len, vec) transforms nothing: it walks a range and writes one byte per page into vec, with the least significant bit set if the page is “in core”. It is the read-only counterpart of mlock() and MADV_WILLNEED — the question an application asks before deciding whether to prefetch, and the question tools such as fincore and page-cache warmers ask about a file they have just mapped. Its implementation in mm/mincore.c is short, but it is one of the few places where the kernel deliberately answers a memory question incorrectly.

The syscall itself is a chunking loop. It untags the address, insists that it be page-aligned, and checks both ranges with access_ok(). It then allocates a single page as a bounce buffer and processes at most PAGE_SIZE entries per iteration, taking mmap_read_lock() for each chunk and dropping it before copy_to_user(), so that a query over a terabyte mapping never holds the lock for long and never faults on the user’s vector while holding it. Each chunk is handled by do_mincore(), which stops at the end of the current VMA; a hole in the range ends the call with ENOMEM, which is the one error that tells a caller “this address is not mapped” rather than “this page is not resident”. 

/* mm/mincore.c — can_do_mincore() and do_mincore(), abbreviated */
static inline bool can_do_mincore(struct vm_area_struct *vma)
{
	if (vma_is_anonymous(vma))
		return true;
	if (!vma->vm_file)
		return false;
	/*
	 * Reveal pagecache information only for non-anonymous mappings that
	 * correspond to the files the calling process could (if tried) open
	 * for writing; otherwise we'd be including shared non-exclusive
	 * mappings, which opens a side channel.
	 */
	return inode_owner_or_capable(&nop_mnt_idmap,
				      file_inode(vma->vm_file)) ||
	       file_permission(vma->vm_file, MAY_WRITE) == 0;
}

static long do_mincore(unsigned long addr, unsigned long pages, unsigned char *vec)
{
	vma = vma_lookup(current->mm, addr);
	if (!vma)
		return -ENOMEM;
	end = min(vma->vm_end, addr + (pages << PAGE_SHIFT));
	if (!can_do_mincore(vma)) {
		unsigned long pages = DIV_ROUND_UP(end - addr, PAGE_SIZE);
		memset(vec, 1, pages);
		return pages;
	}
	err = walk_page_range(vma->vm_mm, addr, end, &mincore_walk_ops, vec);
	...
	return (end - addr) >> PAGE_SHIFT;
}

For a permitted VMA the answer comes from a generic page-table walk. mincore_walk_ops supplies three callbacks: mincore_pte_range() for populated PMDs, mincore_unmapped_range() for holes in the page tables, and mincore_hugetlb() for hugetlbfs. The definition of “in core” that results is not “mapped by this process”, and Table 9-21 shows why: a pte_none() entry in a file-backed VMA is resolved by looking the file offset up in the page cache, so a page that some other process faulted in, or that read-ahead brought in, is reported as resident even though touching it would still take a (minor) fault here. That is exactly what makes the call useful for page-cache warmers, and exactly what makes it dangerous.

Table 9-21. What mincore() Reports for Each Page
What the walk findsReportedHow it is decided
Present PTE1Mapped now; pte_batch_hint() lets a large folio fill several bytes at once.
Huge PMD (THP)1 for every pagepmd_trans_huge_lock() succeeds and the whole range is memset to 1.
Empty PTE, file-backed VMA0 or 1mincore_page(): is the folio at that file index in the page cache and up to date?
Empty PTE, anonymous VMA0Never faulted in, so nothing can be resident.
Swap entry0 or 1mincore_swap(): is an up-to-date folio still in the swap cache?
tmpfs page swapped out0 or 1The page cache holds a swap value entry instead of a folio; the swap cache decides.
hugetlbfs entry0 or 1mincore_hugetlb(): present unless empty or a PTE marker.
VMA failing can_do_mincore()1 for every pageNot walked at all; the answer is fixed.

The last row is the deliberate wrong answer, and its history is short and instructive. The page-cache side-channel attacks published at the start of 2019 used mincore() on shared library and executable mappings to watch which pages another process had just touched. Linus Torvalds’s first response (574823bfab82, Linux 5.0-rc1) changed the semantics to count only pages actually mapped by the caller; it was reverted three weeks later (30bac164aca7, 5.0-rc4) because real tools, including database page-cache migrators, depended on seeing cache residency of files they had mapped but not touched. The compromise that shipped in 5.2 (134fca9063ad) is can_do_mincore() above: anonymous memory is always reported truthfully, because it is private to the caller already, and file pages are reported truthfully only if the caller owns the file or could open it for writing. For everything else — a shared library mapped read-only, someone else’s data file — the kernel neither fails the call nor walks the tables, but claims every page is resident. An application that uses mincore() to decide whether to prefetch simply skips the prefetch; an attacker learns nothing.

cachestat() (Linux 6.5) is the file-oriented successor for the warmer use case. It takes a file descriptor and a byte range rather than a mapping, needs no mmap() at all, and returns aggregated counts — cached, dirty, under writeback, evicted, recently evicted — instead of one byte per page. It applies the same rule through can_do_cachestat() in mm/filemap.c: the descriptor must be open for writing, or the caller must own the file or be able to open it for writing; unlike mincore() it fails with EPERM rather than lying. That check was itself added after the fact, in 6.14 (5f537664e705), because the newer interface had shipped without the check the older one had gained four years earlier. Chapter 15 shows how it computes those counts from the page cache’s XArray without taking a reference on any folio.

Pinning User Pages: GUP and FOLL_PIN

Every section so far has treated user memory from the process’s side: the process maps, faults, protects, and unmaps, and the kernel touches its memory only briefly through copy_to_user() and copy_from_user(), which run in the process’s own context and resolve any fault on the spot. A large part of the kernel needs something stronger. A disk controller doing O_DIRECT I/O, an RDMA adapter, a GPU, a device assigned to a virtual machine through VFIO, and io_uring’s registered buffers all need the physical pages behind a user address range, as struct page pointers they can map for DMA, and they need those pages to stay put while the device works on them — possibly long after the system call that set it up has returned. ptrace(), /proc/pid/mem and the core dumper need the same thing for a different process’s address space, where copy_from_user() cannot reach at all. The interface that serves all of them is GUP, the get_user_pages() family in mm/gup.c, which in 7.0.12 is some 3,500 lines.

GUP is where the machinery of this chapter meets the rest of the kernel. It walks the page tables of Section 6 and, when a page is missing, runs the same fault handler a user access would have run. It consults the VMA flags of Section 3 to decide whether the access is allowed at all. It interacts with copy-on-write and fork() in ways that took most of a decade to get right. And it is the only path by which a page that user space can see becomes a page a device can write to, which is why its correctness rules are stricter than anything else in the memory manager. The six subsections that follow cover the two kinds of reference GUP can take, the slow and fast paths that take them, the special handling of long-term pins, the interaction with copy-on-write, and the subsystems that use it.

GET Versus PIN

Until Linux 5.6 there was only one kind of GUP reference: get_user_pages() raised the folio’s ordinary reference count, exactly as the page cache, the LRU batching code, or a short-lived lookup would. That was enough to stop the page being freed, but it was indistinguishable from every other transient reference. Nothing else in the kernel could tell that a device was about to write into the page. Writeback would clean a page and write-protect its PTEs while a DMA engine was still filling it; reclaim would unmap it and try to swap it; fork() would share it copy-on-write and then give the original to whichever process wrote first. The fix, by John Hubbard (eddb1c228f79, Linux 5.6), was to split the API in two. pin_user_pages*() takes a pin, a reference that is counted separately so that other code can ask whether a folio may be the target of DMA; get_user_pages*() keeps the old meaning.

The rule for choosing between them is spelled out in the comment above FOLL_PIN in include/linux/mm_types.h: a caller that will access the data in the page — DMA, Direct I/O, a CPU write through a kernel mapping — must pin; a caller that only needs the struct page itself, to look at its flags or map count, may get. The flag that selects the behaviour, FOLL_PIN, is not part of the public FOLL_* enumeration. It lives with FOLL_TOUCH, FOLL_TRIED, FOLL_REMOTE, FOLL_FAST_ONLY and FOLL_UNLOCKABLE among the INTERNAL_GUP_FLAGS in mm/internal.h, and is_valid_gup_args() warns and fails if a caller passes any of them. The only way to obtain a pin is to call a pin_user_pages*() function, and the only way to drop one is unpin_user_page() or one of its batch forms; mixing put_page() with a pin corrupts the count. 

/* mm/gup.c — try_grab_folio(), abbreviated */
int __must_check try_grab_folio(struct folio *folio, int refs,
				unsigned int flags)
{
	if (unlikely(!(flags & FOLL_PCI_P2PDMA) && folio_is_pci_p2pdma(folio)))
		return -EREMOTEIO;

	if (flags & FOLL_GET)
		folio_ref_add(folio, refs);
	else if (flags & FOLL_PIN) {
		/*
		 * Don't take a pin on the zero page - it's not going anywhere
		 * and it is used in a *lot* of places.
		 */
		if (is_zero_folio(folio))
			return 0;

		if (folio_has_pincount(folio)) {
			folio_ref_add(folio, refs);
			atomic_add(refs, &folio->_pincount);
		} else {
			folio_ref_add(folio, refs * GUP_PIN_COUNTING_BIAS);
		}

		node_stat_mod_folio(folio, NR_FOLL_PIN_ACQUIRED, refs);
	}
	return 0;
}

A pin is encoded in two different ways, depending on whether the folio has room to record it. A large folio has a dedicated _pincount field stored in the metadata of one of its tail pages (Chapter 2 showed it in struct folio), so a pin adds one to the reference count, to keep the folio alive, and one to _pincount. A small folio has no spare field, so a pin instead adds GUP_PIN_COUNTING_BIAS — 1,024 — to the ordinary reference count. folio_has_pincount() picks the scheme: on 64-bit kernels the field sits in the first tail page, so every large folio has it, while on 32-bit it sits in the second tail page and only folios of order 2 or more do. The test for a pin, folio_maybe_dma_pinned(), reads _pincount exactly when it exists and otherwise asks whether the reference count has reached 1,024. For small folios the answer is therefore deliberately fuzzy in one direction: “no” is certain, “yes” may be a false positive from a page that genuinely has a thousand ordinary references, and every caller is required to tolerate that. Every pin taken or released is also counted per node, which makes leaks visible from user space as a growing gap between nr_foll_pin_acquired and nr_foll_pin_released in /proc/vmstat.

The point of the separate count is the set of places that consult it. Reclaim in mm/vmscan.c keeps a pinned anonymous folio rather than allocating swap for it, and reactivates a pinned folio it has just unmapped instead of trying to free it, because the device may still be modifying the contents. fork() uses it to decide which pages must be copied immediately, as Section 11.5 explains. UFFDIO_MOVE (Section 7.7) refuses to move a folio that may be pinned, and NUMA balancing in mm/mempolicy.c will not mark one for migration. None of these decisions can be made from a plain reference count, which rises and falls for many reasons that do not imply anyone is writing to the page.

Table 9-22. The GUP Interface Family
FunctionReferencemmap_lockTypical use
pin_user_pages_fast()PinNot held; taken only on fallbackDirect I/O, io_uring, RDMA — the common case
pin_user_pages()PinCaller holds it for readCallers already inside a locked region
pin_user_pages_unlocked()PinTaken and dropped internallyCallers that need flags GUP-fast rejects
pin_user_pages_remote()PinCaller holds the target mm’s lockVFIO, iommufd: pinning on behalf of another mm
memfd_pin_folios()PinNone; looks up by file offsetudmabuf: pins memfd folios without a mapping
get_user_pages_fast()GetNot held; taken only on fallbackShort-lived access to struct page
get_user_pages_fast_only()GetNever; no fallbackAtomic context: fails instead of sleeping
get_user_pages() / _unlocked()GetHeld by caller / taken internallyLegacy users; prefaulting with pages == NULL
get_user_pages_remote()GetCaller holds the target mm’s lockaccess_remote_vm(): ptrace(), /proc/pid/mem
unpin_user_pages_dirty_lock()Releases pinsNoneEnd of DMA: optionally dirty, then unpin

The Slow Path: Walking and Faulting

Every GUP call that cannot be satisfied locklessly ends up in __get_user_pages_locked(), which takes mmap_read_lock_killable() if the caller does not already hold it, records a pinning request by setting MMF_HAS_PINNED on the mm, and calls __get_user_pages() in a retry loop. MMF_HAS_PINNED is set on the first pin and never cleared; its only job is to let fork() skip the pin check entirely for the overwhelming majority of processes that have never pinned anything. The same function quietly adds FOLL_GET to legacy callers that pass a page array without FOLL_PIN, which is how the old get_user_pages() semantics survive without a separate code path.

__get_user_pages() is a loop over pages that looks much like a user access performed by the kernel on the process’s behalf. For each address it finds the VMA, checks that the requested access is permitted, tries to read the page from the page tables, and, if that fails, runs the page fault handler and tries again: 

/* mm/gup.c — __get_user_pages(), abbreviated */
	do {
		if (!vma || start >= vma->vm_end) {
			vma = gup_vma_lookup(mm, start);
			if (!vma && in_gate_area(mm, start)) {
				ret = get_gate_page(mm, start & PAGE_MASK, ...);
				...
			}
			if (!vma) {
				ret = -EFAULT;
				goto out;
			}
			ret = check_vma_flags(vma, gup_flags);
			if (ret)
				goto out;
		}
retry:
		if (fatal_signal_pending(current)) {
			ret = -EINTR;
			goto out;
		}
		cond_resched();

		page = follow_page_mask(vma, start, gup_flags, &page_mask);
		if (!page || PTR_ERR(page) == -EMLINK) {
			ret = faultin_page(vma, start, gup_flags,
					   PTR_ERR(page) == -EMLINK, locked);
			switch (ret) {
			case 0:
				goto retry;
			case -EBUSY:
			case -EAGAIN:
				ret = 0;
				fallthrough;
			case -EFAULT:
			case -ENOMEM:
			case -EHWPOISON:
				goto out;
			}
			BUG();
		} else if (PTR_ERR(page) == -EEXIST) {
			...	/* PFN mapping with no struct page */
		}
next_page:
		...	/* store page (and the rest of a large folio) in pages[] */
	} while (nr_pages);

check_vma_flags() is where GUP enforces the access rules that the MMU would enforce for a user access, plus some of its own. VM_IO and VM_PFNMAP mappings are refused outright, because the memory behind them may not have a struct page at all. memfd_secret() memory is refused for the reason it exists: no kernel path may reach it. A write needs VM_WRITE, and a read needs VM_READ, unless the caller passed FOLL_FORCE; and FOLL_FORCE grants a write to a read-only mapping only if the mapping is a private copy-on-write one. That narrow exception is what lets a debugger plant a breakpoint in a process’s read-only text through ptrace() or /proc/pid/mem: the write lands in a private copy of the page, never in the file. Writes to shadow-stack memory are refused even with FOLL_FORCE, and the architecture gets the final word through arch_vma_access_permitted(), which is where protection keys are checked. Two further refusals are specific to long-term pins and are covered in Section 11.4.

follow_page_mask() walks the page tables under the page-table lock, exactly as the generic fault path in Section 6.2 does, but it never allocates or modifies anything. It returns the page if the PTE is present and grants the access, NULL if a fault is needed — the PTE is empty, swapped out, or read-only when a write was requested — and -EMLINK for the special case of a read-only pin on a page that must first be unshared, described in Section 11.5. On success it takes the reference or pin through try_grab_folio() while the page-table lock is still held, so that the page cannot be unmapped and freed between the lookup and the grab.

faultin_page() converts GUP’s vocabulary into the fault handler’s and calls handle_mm_fault() directly. FOLL_WRITE becomes FAULT_FLAG_WRITE, FOLL_REMOTE becomes FAULT_FLAG_REMOTE, a caller that can tolerate the lock being dropped gets FAULT_FLAG_ALLOW_RETRY | FAULT_FLAG_KILLABLE, and an -EMLINK from the walk becomes FAULT_FLAG_UNSHARE — the flags whose fault-side meaning Table 9-11 described. A VM_FAULT_RETRY that drops mmap_lock makes the outer loop in __get_user_pages_locked() reacquire it and continue with FOLL_TRIED set, mirroring what the architecture fault handlers do. FOLL_NOFAULT short-circuits the whole step, which is what callers that already hold a lock the fault handler might need use to avoid deadlock.

Two facts about what the slow path guarantees are easy to get wrong. First, the page returned is the one that would be accessed at that instant; GUP keeps it from being freed, but does nothing to keep it mapped. If the process later unmaps the range or a copy-on-write fault replaces the page, the caller is left holding a page that the process no longer sees. That is harmless for a pin held for the length of one I/O and is exactly the problem Section 11.5 has to solve for anything longer. Second, the same loop serves callers that pass no page array at all. populate_vma_page_range(), which mlock() (Section 10.4), MAP_POPULATE and MADV_POPULATE_READ/WRITE use, calls __get_user_pages() with pages == NULL purely to run the faults; no reference is taken, and GUP is acting as a prefaulting engine rather than as a page lookup.

GUP-fast

Taking mmap_lock for every Direct I/O request would serialize a busy database against its own mmap() and munmap() calls. GUP-fast avoids the lock entirely: gup_fast() walks the page tables from the PGD down with local interrupts disabled, never looks at a VMA, and pins every page whose PTE it can verify on its own. Both architectures in this book select CONFIG_HAVE_GUP_FAST, so pin_user_pages_fast() and get_user_pages_fast() try this path first and fall back to the slow path only for whatever part of the range it could not handle.

Walking page tables with no lock is safe only because something else keeps those tables from being freed underneath the walker, and the comment above the GUP-fast code in mm/gup.c explains what. Page-table pages are freed either after a TLB-shootdown IPI, or through an RCU-deferred free (MMU_GATHER_RCU_TABLE_FREE); with interrupts disabled the walking CPU can acknowledge neither, so the free cannot complete while the walk is in progress. The same blockade stops a THP split from completing under the walker. Within that window, gup_fast_pte_range() reads each PTE with ptep_get_lockless(), takes a speculative reference with try_grab_folio_fast(), and then re-reads both the PMD and the PTE: if either changed, the folio may have been unmapped and reused between the read and the grab, so the reference is dropped and the walk stops.

Because GUP-fast has no VMA, it cannot apply check_vma_flags(), and it compensates by refusing anything it cannot decide from the PTE and the folio alone. pte_protnone() entries and PTEs that fail pte_access_permitted() go to the slow path, which knows the VMA’s real permissions; pte_special() entries have no usable struct page. try_grab_folio_fast() refuses a folio that a long-term pin would have to migrate. gup_fast_folio_allowed() refuses secretmem folios and, for a long-term writable pin, any file-backed folio other than shmem or hugetlb, since it cannot see the VMA to ask whether the file needs dirty tracking. And a read-only pin of an anonymous page that is not known to be exclusive is refused, so that the slow path can unshare it first.

The last race GUP-fast must handle is with fork(), and it is the reason for the write_protect_seq counter shown in Section 8.1. copy_page_range() write-protects the parent’s private pages while holding the mmap write lock, which the slow path would wait for but GUP-fast ignores. So, for pins only, gup_fast() samples the sequence count before the walk and gives up if a fork is in progress; after the walk it checks the count again, and if a fork ran in between it unpins everything it just took and returns zero. The slow path then retries under mmap_lock, which cannot overlap a fork.

Long-Term Pins and Migration

Most pins last for one I/O: Direct I/O pins the buffer, the bio completes, and the pages are unpinned within milliseconds. Some pins last as long as user space wants them to. An RDMA memory region stays registered until the application deregisters it; a VFIO DMA mapping lasts as long as the virtual machine; an io_uring registered buffer lasts until the ring is torn down. A caller that knows it is in the second category must say so with FOLL_LONGTERM, which is valid only together with a pin. The flag does not make the pin stronger; it makes GUP more careful about which pages it agrees to pin, because a page pinned indefinitely is a page the memory manager can never move again.

That matters because Linux makes promises about certain memory that depend on being able to move pages. ZONE_MOVABLE exists so that a memory block can be emptied and hot-removed; CMA regions (Chapter 8) lend their pages to movable allocations on the understanding that the pages can be migrated away when a driver needs a large contiguous buffer. Chapter 17 describes the migration engine itself (see Page Migration and Memory Failure). One long-term pin in either place breaks the promise. folio_is_longterm_pinnable() in include/linux/mm.h encodes which folios are safe: anything in a CMA or isolated pageblock, coherent device memory, FS-DAX memory, and anything in ZONE_MOVABLE is not; the zero page and ordinary unmovable memory are. 

/* mm/gup.c — __gup_longterm_locked(), abbreviated */
static long __gup_longterm_locked(struct mm_struct *mm, unsigned long start,
				  unsigned long nr_pages, struct page **pages,
				  int *locked, unsigned int gup_flags)
{
	unsigned int flags;
	long rc, nr_pinned_pages;

	if (!(gup_flags & FOLL_LONGTERM))
		return __get_user_pages_locked(mm, start, nr_pages, pages,
					       locked, gup_flags);

	flags = memalloc_pin_save();
	do {
		nr_pinned_pages = __get_user_pages_locked(mm, start, nr_pages,
							  pages, locked,
							  gup_flags);
		if (nr_pinned_pages <= 0) {
			rc = nr_pinned_pages;
			break;
		}

		/* FOLL_LONGTERM implies FOLL_PIN */
		rc = check_and_migrate_movable_pages(nr_pinned_pages, pages);
	} while (rc == -EAGAIN);
	memalloc_pin_restore(flags);
	return rc ? rc : nr_pinned_pages;
}

The strategy is pin first, check second. memalloc_pin_save() sets PF_MEMALLOC_PIN on the task, which strips __GFP_MOVABLE from every allocation it makes, so any page that the faults inside the loop have to allocate is placed in a zone where long-term pinning is acceptable. Pages that were already resident may still live in movable memory, so after pinning the range collect_longterm_unpinnable_folios() scans the result and isolates every unpinnable folio from the LRU, draining the per-CPU LRU batches first if an unexpected reference suggests one is hiding there. migrate_longterm_unpinnable_folios() then drops every pin in the range — migration refuses folios with unexpected references, and a pin is exactly that — and calls migrate_pages() with reason MR_LONGTERM_PIN and a GFP_USER target, which is not movable. If migration succeeds it returns -EAGAIN, and the loop pins the whole range again; this time the PTEs point at the new copies, the check finds nothing to move, and the pins stand. If migration fails the call fails with -ENOMEM or -EBUSY, and the caller sees an error rather than a pin that silently defeats memory hot-remove.

Two kinds of memory cannot be fixed by migration, so check_vma_flags() rejects them before any page is touched. An FS-DAX mapping, where user space maps persistent memory directly with no page cache in between, fails a long-term pin with EOPNOTSUPP: the filesystem must be able to truncate or punch holes in the file, which it cannot do while a device holds the blocks indefinitely. A writable long-term pin of a file mapping that needs dirty tracking fails with EFAULT, a rule added in Linux 6.5 (8ac268436e6d). The comment above writable_file_mapping_allowed() gives the scenario: GUP write-faults the page, so the filesystem sees it dirtied; writeback later cleans it and write-protects the PTE; the device keeps writing through its pin anyway, so the page is modified with no write fault and no filesystem notification; and when the pin is finally dropped the page may be marked dirty again behind the filesystem’s back. Shmem, hugetlbfs and anonymous memory are unaffected, because they do no such tracking.

Table 9-23. How a Long-Term Pin Treats Different Memory
MemoryOutcomeWhere it is decided
Anonymous, shmem or hugetlb in an unmovable zonePinned in placefolio_is_longterm_pinnable()
Folio in ZONE_MOVABLEMigrated, then pinnedcollect_longterm_unpinnable_folios()
Folio in a CMA or isolated pageblockMigrated, then pinnedcollect_longterm_unpinnable_folios()
Coherent device memoryMigrated to system RAM, then pinnedmigrate_device_coherent_folio()
Zero pageAccepted but not pinnedtry_grab_folio()
FS-DAX mappingRefused: EOPNOTSUPPcheck_vma_flags()
Writable pin of a dirty-tracked file mappingRefused: EFAULTwritable_file_mapping_allowed()
PCI peer-to-peer memory (FOLL_PCI_P2PDMA)Refused: the flags are mutually exclusiveis_valid_gup_args()

GUP itself enforces no limit on how much memory may be pinned long-term. Each caller charges the pages to the task’s RLIMIT_MEMLOCK — the same limit that bounds mlock() in Section 10.4 — and records them in mm->pinned_vm or mm->locked_vm. That is why pinned_vm is the one atomic field in the accounting group of Section 2.1: it is updated by drivers outside mmap_lock. The split between the two counters is historical rather than principled: RDMA charges pinned_vm, VFIO charges locked_vm, and io_uring and iommufd charge pinned_vm plus a locked_vm count kept per user (or, for iommufd, optionally per mm).

Pins, Copy-on-Write, and fork()

The hardest correctness problem in GUP is not finding the page but making sure it stays the page. Copy-on-write, as Section 6.5 showed, works by replacing a shared page with a private copy in whichever process writes first. If a device has pinned the original, a replacement on the wrong side detaches the process from the page the device is using: the device keeps reading or writing a page the process can no longer see. For years this produced silent data corruption in two forms. A process that registered an RDMA buffer and then called fork() could lose its own DMA target, because the parent’s next write to the buffer copied the page and left the original to the child. And a read-only pin of a page shared with another process could be stranded the moment either side wrote to it. Linux 7.0.12 closes both with two mechanisms, one at fork time and one at pin time.

At fork time, copy_present_pte() normally shares an anonymous page with the child and write-protects it in both. Before doing so it calls folio_try_dup_anon_rmap_pte(), which consults folio_needs_cow_for_dma(): 

/* include/linux/mm.h */
static inline bool folio_needs_cow_for_dma(struct vm_area_struct *vma,
					  struct folio *folio)
{
	VM_BUG_ON(!(raw_read_seqcount(&vma->vm_mm->write_protect_seq) & 1));

	if (!mm_flags_test(MMF_HAS_PINNED, vma->vm_mm))
		return false;

	return folio_maybe_dma_pinned(folio);
}

If the parent has ever pinned anything and this folio may be pinned now, the duplication fails with -EBUSY and copy_present_page() gives the child a fresh copy immediately, leaving the parent’s PTE writable and pointing at the original. The parent and the device keep the page they agreed on; the child pays a copy it might otherwise have avoided. The VM_BUG_ON records the precondition from Section 11.3: this check is only meaningful while write_protect_seq is held, because that is what stops GUP-fast from taking a new pin between the test and the PTE copy. The MMF_HAS_PINNED test is what makes the scheme cheap, since a process that never pinned skips folio_maybe_dma_pinned() altogether.

At pin time the problem is the reverse: a pin must never be taken on a page that a later copy-on-write could detach. For a writable pin, GUP already write-faults the page, which breaks any sharing first. For a read-only pin, Linux 5.19 (a7f226604170) introduced the unshare fault. gup_must_unshare() in mm/internal.h applies only when FOLL_PIN is set without FOLL_WRITE, and asks whether the anonymous page is PageAnonExclusive — the same bit Section 6.5 showed deciding copy-on-write reuse. If it is not, the walk returns -EMLINK, faultin_page() issues a fault with FAULT_FLAG_UNSHARE, and do_wp_page() either marks the page exclusive or gives this process its own copy, without making the PTE writable. The pin is then taken on a page the process owns exclusively, and since an exclusive page is reused rather than copied on a later write fault, the pin and the mapping stay together. For long-term read-only pins the rule extends to page-cache pages in private file mappings, which a later write would replace with an anonymous copy. A debug check in sanity_check_pinned_pages() enforces the invariant: every pinned anonymous page must be exclusive.

The FAULT_FLAG_UNSHARE row in Table 9-11 is this mechanism seen from the fault handler’s side. It is raised on a page the caller can already read, it never makes the PTE writable, and it exists solely so that GUP can establish the exclusivity a pin depends on.

Who Pins User Memory

The consumers of GUP fall into three groups, and Table 9-24 lists the main ones in 7.0.12. The first group pins for the length of one operation: Direct I/O obtains pages through iov_iter_extract_pages(), which pins user-backed iterators with pin_user_pages_fast() and tells the block layer, through BIO_PAGE_PINNED, that the bio must unpin rather than put its pages on completion (Chapter 16). The second group pins long-term and accounts the pages against RLIMIT_MEMLOCK: io_uring registered buffers (Chapter 24), RDMA memory regions, VFIO and iommufd DMA mappings for device assignment, and udmabuf, which pins memfd folios by file offset through memfd_pin_folios() without needing a mapping at all. The third group still uses plain references, because it only needs a page for an instant: ptrace() and /proc/pid/mem through access_remote_vm(), the core dumper through get_dump_page(), and the prefaulting callers that pass no page array. KVM sits between the groups, as Chapter 2 described: it normally takes a short reference and relies on MMU notifiers to learn when the host mapping changes, and pins only on request.

Table 9-24. Main GUP Consumers in Linux 7.0.12

The pattern every pinning consumer follows is the one Figure 9-12 shows end to end: pin with pin_user_pages*(), map the pages for DMA, let the device work, and finish with unpin_user_pages_dirty_lock(), passing true if the device wrote to them. The dirtying step is not optional for writes: the device modified the page without going through the MMU, so no page-table dirty bit records the change, and without set_page_dirty_lock() the filesystem or swap would never learn that the data changed. The _lock in the name refers to that folio lock, not to mmap_lock.

Summary

This chapter followed the process address space from its static description to its dynamic behavior, covering every kernel subsystem that creates, queries, modifies, populates, and destroys virtual memory mappings. Five questions organize the eleven sections: what the kernel records, how a process changes that record, when the record becomes real memory, how an address space begins and ends, and how the rest of the kernel reaches into it.

Section 1 (Process Address Space Overview) established the fundamental abstraction: a process does not see physical RAM but a sparse, demand-paged virtual address space assembled from executable images, heaps, stacks, file mappings, guard gaps, and architecture-specific pages such as the VDSO. The contrast with Chapter 8’s physical-allocation perspective sets up the rest of the chapter, and the recurring theme is that recording a mapping is cheap bookkeeping while populating it is where the cost finally lands.

Section 2 (The Memory Descriptor) dissected mm_struct, the process-wide root structure holding the PGD pointer, the maple tree of VMAs (mm_mt), mmap_lock, the two reference counts, layout bounds, and accounting fields. The central distinction is that mm_users counts users of the mapped address space while mm_count counts references to the structure, with every nonzero mm_users holding exactly one mm_count; virtual accounting (total_vm) and resident accounting (the MM_* counters behind RSS) likewise measure two different things. The section closed with how kernel threads borrow an address space through lazy-TLB mode and kthread_use_mm(), and how context_switch() handles the four possible mm transitions.

Section 3 (Memory Regions) covered vm_area_struct: its slab cache with SLAB_TYPESAFE_BY_RCU, its lifecycle helpers in mm/vma_init.c, its cache-line layout tuned for the fault fast path, the vm_flags vocabulary now declared as bit numbers before masks, and the vm_operations_struct callback table. Four subsections followed: flag declaration and the vm_flags_set()/vm_flags_clear() accessors that take the per-VMA write lock for the caller; the maple tree that replaced the old rb-tree plus linked list; VMA lookup via vma_lookup() and find_vma(); and merging and splitting, where vma_modify() tries to merge first and splits only as a fallback, bounded by sysctl_max_map_count.

Section 4 (The mmap() System Call) traced the path from the user-space prototype through do_mmap() into __mmap_region(), covering anonymous versus file-backed mappings, the MAP_SHARED/MAP_PRIVATE matrix, and MAP_* flag semantics. Two subsections describe machinery that is new or reworked since Linux 2.6: the mmap_prepare hook, a newer and mutually exclusive alternative to f_op->mmap that lets a filesystem describe a mapping through struct vm_area_desc before any VMA exists, and the unmapped-gap search, whose top-down and bottom-up variants sit underneath the ASLR layout selection made once per exec by arch_pick_mmap_layout().

Section 5 (Releasing Address Intervals) described munmap() as three phases — validate and split at the boundaries, detach the VMAs from the maple tree, then tear down page tables and free the regions — followed by page-table teardown with TLB batching, and the lock downgrade that lets the expensive teardown proceed after the write-side topology change is complete. Detachment before teardown is what makes the downgrade safe: once the VMAs are out of the tree, no new lookup or per-VMA-lock fault can find them.

Section 6 (The Page Fault Handler) — the longest section — covered the whole resolution pipeline. It began with architecture entry points (x86 exc_page_fault() reading CR2, ARM64 do_page_fault() reading FAR_EL1) that normalize hardware exceptions into the FAULT_FLAG_* vocabulary. The generic fault path walks the five-level hierarchy and dispatches on PTE state. Per-VMA locks let most faults run without touching mmap_lock at all, taking a single VMA under RCU via lock_vma_under_rcu(); CONFIG_PER_VMA_LOCK is def_bool y and both architectures in this book satisfy its dependencies, so this is the ordinary path rather than an option, though topology changes still need the write side. Demand paging splits into anonymous faults and file-backed faults with read, CoW, and shared sub-paths. Copy-on-write decides between reuse and copy based on folio mapcount, refcount, and GUP pin state — the accounting hardened after Dirty CoW. Huge page faults cover THP and hugetlbfs. Fault return codes explained the vm_fault_t bitmask protocol, including why it is a bitmask rather than an errno. Finally, kernel-mode faults covered vmalloc page-table synchronization (an x86-32 concern only), exception-table fixups behind copy_to/from_user(), and the oops path for faults with no fixup.

Section 7 (userfaultfd) described how user space intercepts and resolves its own faults through a file descriptor. After the motivation and the UFFDIO_API feature handshake, the section examined how the kernel actually blocks a faulting thread on a wait queue and releases it, how the descriptor behaves as a file under read(), poll() and close(), and the mfill family that resolves a fault by installing a page. Three further modes followed: write-protect mode, whose state lives in PTEs and PTE markers rather than in VMA flags; UFFDIO_MOVE, which relocates an existing folio instead of copying it; and non-cooperative mode, where the monitor also observes fork, remap, and unmap events. Originally built for post-copy live migration, the interface is now used by CRIU, by garbage collectors, and by distributed shared memory, and is gated by vm.unprivileged_userfaultfd.

Section 8 (Creating and Deleting Address Spaces) traced the three lifecycle transitions. Fork clones the topology through dup_mm() and dup_mmap(), which duplicates the parent’s maple tree in one pass so that per-VMA insertion cannot fail partway through. Copy-on-write makes the operation cost proportional to page-table size rather than resident memory, and vma_needs_copy() goes further: page tables are copied only when the destination carries a VM_COPY_ON_FORK flag or the source already has an anon_vma, so many mappings are rebuilt lazily by later faults instead. Not everything is inherited — VM_LOCKED is explicitly cleared in the child. Exec builds a one-page stack VMA before the point of no return, then commits in begin_new_exec(), after which failure is fatal; exec_mmap() installs the new descriptor and setup_new_exec() picks a fresh ASLR layout. Exit distinguishes the two counts once more: the last mmput() destroys the contents through exit_mmap(), whose two-phase lock dance unmaps under the write lock and frees afterwards, while the last mmdrop() frees the container, possibly long after a lazy-TLB CPU finally lets go.

Section 9 (Heap Management) covered brk(), the oldest Unix memory interface and still the one modern allocators use for small allocations below their mmap threshold. The section followed where the heap begins (ELF placement plus arch_randomize_brk(), which x86_64 provides itself while ARM64 inherits the generic helper), then the syscall proper, growth through do_brk_flags() — which now lives in mm/vma.c — shrinkage through do_vmi_align_munmap(), and the kernel-internal vm_brk_flags() used by the ELF loader. The ordering of the checks matters as much as the checks themselves: check_data_rlimit() is evaluated against the requested break before page alignment and before the fast path, because testing the rounded-up value after alignment is precisely the bug the in-tree comment records as fixed.

Section 10 (Other Address Space Operations) surveyed four in-place transforms that all follow the split-modify-merge pattern. mremap() resizes or relocates a mapping by moving page tables rather than copying pages; the modern entry point fills a struct vma_remap_struct and classifies the request before dispatching. mprotect() changes permissions subject to a list of vetoes applied per VMA — the VM_MAY* ceiling, map_deny_write_exec(), architecture validation such as ARM64’s BTI and MTE rules, LSM review, and an optional vm_ops hook — which is what makes it a security boundary as well as a convenience. madvise() spans genuinely advisory hints, destructive operations such as MADV_DONTNEED, guard-region installation, and process_madvise(), which lets a privileged manager apply hints to another process. mlock()/mlockall() pins pages against reclaim; the flags do not stack, since mlockall() clears the existing lock state before applying the new one. The section closed with the read-only query mincore(), whose idea of “resident” includes page-cache pages the caller has not mapped, and which since Linux 5.2 reports every page as resident for files the caller could not open for writing, closing a page-cache side channel without breaking the tools that depend on the call.

Section 11 (Pinning User Pages) covered GUP, the bridge from user virtual addresses to struct page pointers that devices and other kernel subsystems can use. Since Linux 5.6 it has two kinds of reference: a plain get and a pin, which is counted separately — in _pincount for large folios, as a bias of 1,024 on the reference count for small ones — so that reclaim, fork and migration can tell that a device may be writing. The slow path walks the page tables with follow_page_mask() and runs the ordinary fault handler through faultin_page() when a page is missing; GUP-fast does the same walk with interrupts disabled and no mmap_lock, handing anything it cannot verify to the slow path. Long-term pins (FOLL_LONGTERM) migrate folios out of ZONE_MOVABLE and CMA before pinning and refuse FS-DAX and writable dirty-tracked file mappings outright. Copy-on-write is reconciled with pinning from both ends: fork() copies a possibly pinned page to the child at once, and a read-only pin first raises an unshare fault so that the pinned page is exclusive to the process. The section closed with the consumers — Direct I/O, io_uring, RDMA, VFIO, iommufd, udmabuf, ptrace and core dumps — and the RLIMIT_MEMLOCK accounting that bounds long-term pins.

The historical evolution across these sections is as instructive as any single mechanism. The rb-tree plus linked-list VMA store gave way to the maple tree, and most of the VMA machinery moved out of mm/mmap.c into mm/vma.c — much of it relocated rather than rewritten. mmap_sem became mmap_lock, and then stopped being the only lock that matters once per-VMA locking took over the fault fast path. Copy-on-write was hardened after Dirty CoW, and fork learned to skip page-table copying entirely where a later fault can reconstruct the mapping. Folios replaced bare struct page references throughout the fault path. GUP split into get_user_pages() and pin_user_pages(), so that a page a device is writing to can finally be told apart from one that is merely referenced. And whole interfaces appeared that have no counterpart in Linux 2.6: userfaultfd() hands fault resolution to user space, process_madvise() hands policy to a remote manager, and mseal() lets a process give up the right to change its own mappings. Modern Linux virtual memory is still recognizable from Linux 2.6, but its scaling, safety, and programmability are considerably richer.

If you want to continue tracing execution after this chapter, there are three especially productive paths. Revisit Chapter 2 to reconnect these software walks with hardware translation behavior. Revisit Chapter 8 to study how folios supplied during faults are allocated, charged, and reclaimed. Then move forward to Chapter 20, where execve() turns an ELF image into the initial VMA layout that all of these mechanisms later operate upon.

The kernel source files this chapter works from are collected by topic in Source Trails.
